# Psalms 73-150

# PSALM LXXIII

*{A Psalm of Asaph}* Or, *A Psalm for Asaph*.

*{of Asaph}* See notes on Psalm 50, tit.

**Verse 1. ***{Truly God is good to Israel, even to such as are of a clean heart.}* This abrupt beginning by an אך, which is *adversativa particula,* as they call them, and properly signifies *verumtamen,* or *but;* by some translated here, *utique:* and this sudden determination of the business, before any further arguing, may well seem somewhat strange: and puts Interpreters upon this conceit, that the Psalmist had been in a long conflict within himself, arguing the case to and fro, faith on the one side, and the Devil on the other, doing their parts by turns; and at last, faith by the help of God having prevailed, he breaks out of very joy, into this exclamative, or acclamative asseveration, *Truly God,* &c. As for the conceit, that is true enough: the author professes it afterwards, but that he was newly out of it, when he wrote this, and therefore thus abruptly begins, &c. it is but a supposition, which may pass for probable, until somewhat more certain be found out. Whether this will be thought so, that I am now to say, I know not: but it has more ground, I am sure, than a bare supposition. The ancients, those that were very religious, were wont to speak of God, and those things that belong unto God, not with reverence only, but with some fear and terror also, lest through ignorance and incapability they should utter anything, that might offend God either in the expression, or matter. This Plato often professes of himself; as in his Dialogue called *Cratylus,* in that one Dialogue more than once. But to leave them: The Prophet Jeremy upon this very subject, out of a holy zeal to God's glory moved with indignation to see the prosperity of the wicked, and purposing to expostulate the matter with God himself, he thought it necessary to make his way by a previous acknowledgment of God's righteousness, *Righteous art thou, O God, when I plead with thee, yet let me talk with thee of thy judgments,* Jer. 12.1, &c. So the Prophet Habakkuk too, *Art thou not from everlasting, O Lord, my God, mine Holy One?* &c. *Thou art of purer eyes than to behold evil, and canst not look on iniquities:* having said so much by way of necessary premunition, he goes on boldly; *wherefore lookest thou upon them that deal treacherously, and holdest thy tongue when the wicked devoureth the man that is more righteous than he? and makest men,* &c. See also on Psalm 89.5. *the heavens,* &c. I conceive that here likewise the Psalmist being to treat of the same subject, and having proposed unto himself this method, first to make his objections, before he came to his conclusive answers; yet before he began that bold and expostulative discourse, he thought it necessary to premise this pacificatory and mitigative protestation, אך *Atqui: However, Truly,* &c. as if he had said, Whatever I shall say by way of argument that may seem contrary, I first protest and profess this to be my faith and firm belief, that God is just and good, and will deal accordingly with them that fear and serve him with purity of heart, &c. I have said, what I ground upon: but it may serve for further illustration to add, that an ancient Heathen Author, who, though he lived since Christ, had not the happiness to know Christ: but one of the most rational Heathens otherwise that ever was, and highly commended and admired even by Christians for his good life: (he was one of the Roman Emperors) having in few words, and with a great deal of modesty argued the case, why the Gods, (so he speaks, according to the wonted style of heathens though it appears by other places of his Book what he thought of the thing) had not made good and godly men immortal, he concludes, But now because it is not so (if so be that it be not so indeed) be therefore confident, that it was not fit it should be so. For you see yourself, that now seeking after this matter, how freely you do agree and contest with God. But were not the Gods, both just and good in the highest degree, you would not dare thus reason with them. So he. As for the prosperity of the wicked, and the sufferings of the godly in this world, and all that belongs to that question, as it is here resolved by David, and the like; see upon Psal. 37, which is not the same argument as this; *Fret not thy self,* &c.

*{of a clean heart}* Heb. *clean of heart*.

**V. 2. ***{My feet were almost gone}* By murmuring against God, or, doubting of his providence, and liking too well the state of the wicked, ver. 3, &c.

**V. 3. ***{For I was envious at the foolish: when I saw the prosperity of the wicked}* Job 21.7. Psal. 37.1. Jer. 12.1.

**V. 4. ***{There are no bands in their death}* The wicked in this life live in pleasure, and are not drawn to untimely death, by violent diseases, or other chances, like prisoners, but live out their time, and die their natural deaths.

*{in}* Or, *to*.

*{their strength}* Heb. אולם, for אולים, (a *Vau* for a *Jod*) as some chief Rabbins, followed by our English here, and most Interpreters. Others, according to the proper signification of the word, if the *Mem* be radical, (*aula, atrium, vestibulum,*) translate *palatium;* and supplying a כ *similitudinis,* often omitted, make the sense to be; They are as firm, and healthy, as a strong Palace. But others without any such supplement, *their palaces are firm and strong;* that is, say they, they dwell in stately houses and palaces. And why might not אולם be taken for the body itself, which by St. Paul, (and so by divers others) is called σκῆνος, a tabernacle, 2 Cor. 5.1, &c. which though in opposition to the soul, to them that know the worth of it, it be more like a cottage, or prison, as the Pythagoreans and Platonics were wont to call it, than a palace: yet of such as are here described, whose happiness consists in the welfare of their bodies: their body may well be styled their palace: and the Epithet בריא, which properly (as in the margin here) signifies fat, will better agree with this substantive, than any other; and so the meaning will be, that their bodies are fat and lusty. The body is resembled unto a house in Ecclesiastes also, Chapter 12, ver. 3. I think there is as much probability in this both sense and version, as in any of the former: but the consequence of the difference is not great: let the Reader please himself.

**V. 5. ***{in trouble as other men}* Heb. *in the trouble of other men*.

*{like}* Heb. *with*.

**V. 6. ***{Therefore}* They glory in their pride, as some do in their chains, and in cruelty, as some do in rich apparel.

**V. 7. ***{they have more than heart could wish}* Heb. *they pass the thoughts of the heart*.

**V. 8. ***{they speak loftily}* Heb. ממרום, by some translated (as well it may) *de Excelso;* that is, concerning the High God; meaning, against him: for the speech of such concerning God, must be against: either profane, or blasphemous. But I rather approve of the former version, here expressed in our English; which is followed by most, and seems more proper to the words. It is ordinary to see the pride of men by their great words, and high looks. Or, *they speak loftily,* as if they thought themselves above all other men, and would be so accounted of by others.

**V. 9. ***{They set their mouth against the heavens}* They blaspheme God, not fearing his power; and rail upon men, as inferiors.

**V. 10. ***{Therefore his people}* Not only wicked men do evil, but also the people of God oftentimes fall back, seeing the prosperous estate of the wicked, and are overwhelmed with sorrows, thinking that God considers not aright the state of the godly.

*{return hither}* To say, as follows, ver. 11, &c.

*{waters of a full cup are wrung out to them}* Or, *when waters of a full cup are wrung out to them*; that is, when they see wicked men prosper by oppressing others.

**V. 11. ***{And they say}* Thus the flesh moves even the godly to dispute with God touching their poor estate, and the prosperity of the wicked.

**V. 13. ***{washed my hands in innocency}* Psal. 26.6.

**V. 14. ***{chastened}* Heb. *my chastisement was*.

**V. 15. ***{If I say, I will speak thus}* If I give place to this wicked thought, I shall wrong godly men, and condemn them for hypocrites because they are under affliction. Some like better of this version, which indeed is most literal: *Ecce generatio filiorum tuorum: transgressus sum:* that is, Behold, the generation of your children; I have transgressed. As if he had said; Should I say so, Behold, you have a Church nevertheless: a number of children in the world, who notwithstanding these temporal crosses and afflictions, and the prosperity of the wicked continue in your service faithfully: the consideration of these does condemn me: בגדתי, I have sinned: I acknowledge and confess the iniquity and impiety of such thoughts. I do not see but this may do as well; and I am sure it likes some of the best; though opposed by some others. See Psalm 44.17, 18. *All this is come upon us,* &c. And Heb. Chap. 11, the whole Chapter: Chap. 12.1, the inference there upon the former Chapter, *Wherefore seeing we also are compassed about,* &c. It will much confirm this interpretation. Besides these, there be other interpretations of these words too, divers; but not any so probable as either of these; which therefore we pass by.

**V. 16. ***{it was too painful for me}* Heb. *it was labor in mine eyes*.

**V. 17. ***{until I went into the Sanctuary of God}* Until I entered into your School, and learned by your Word and holy Spirit, that you orderest things wisely and justly, reserving judgment for them.

**V. 18. ***{thou castedst them down into destruction}* Heb. *destructions*. That is, a very great destruction.

**V. 19. ***{they are utterly consumed with terrors}* By your fearful judgment.

**V. 20. ***{As a dream when one awaketh}* Of all these words, expressing the suddenness of the destruction of the wicked, and the shortness of their prosperity; and of the sufficiency, or insufficiency of this answer, see, as before, upon Psalm 37, ver. 1 and 2.

*{when thou awakest, thou shalt despise their image}* Heb. בעיר, *in suscitando:* that is, *in waking*; without expression of the person: which has bred variety of interpretations; three, at the least; of which, which is to be preferred, because all warrantable by the Original words affording a good sense, and avouched by good Authors, is not so easy to determine. First, *in awaking*; that is, (as here in the English) *when thou awakest:* When you show your power, hidden for a time, as a man awaking does; Psal. 78.65. *Then the Lord awaked, as one out of sleep:* and Psal. 7.6. *awake for me to judgment:* and 35.23. *awake to my judgment.* Again, *in waking*, that is, *when you awakest them*, say some, that they may see that they have been all this while but in a dream: that all their greatness, and supposed felicity was but imaginary. It is no small aggravation of their misery, that themselves, before they die, shall know that they have been deluded all this while, when they thought themselves most happy; which must needs end in great pangs and agonies of soul. Or lastly; *in waking*, that is, *when you awakest us*, and shall remove the cloud from before our eyes, that we may clearly see and discern the vanity of all worldly things: How quickly this life passes away, and every thing with it; how inconsiderable at the best; and by consequence, how sottish we are; or, as himself speaks afterwards, how foolish and ignorant, yea, brutish, that make such a matter of wicked men's worldly prosperity. This knowledge, though of itself incurrent enough into the eyes of men that have their eyes open; yet how apt we are, either to be lulled asleep by our own, or to be dazzled by the temporal welfare and prosperity of others, so that we need God's continual help and assistance to keep us awake, that is, in a right judgment; appears by the Psalmist, who elsewhere expressly calls upon God for it. So Psal. 39.4. *Lord make me to know mine end, and the measure of my days, what it is, that I may know how frail I am.* Though he make particular application of it to himself; yet he means it not of himself particularly, as though he desired to know how long he should live; but of all men in general, as appears by the 6 verse, *Surely every man walketh in a vain shew;* or, according to the propriety of the words, here retained by most Interpreters, *Every man passes away in a figure*; or, *image:* צלם is the word there; and it is the word here too: *thou shalt despise their image:* צלמם. So again Psalm 90.12. *So teach us to number our days,* &c. Of all these, being all warrantable, as I have said, the Reader may choose which interpretation he best likes. The first is most followed, and as I conceive, if there be any difference, most probable. There be both among the Rabbins, and Christians, that have expounded this awaking, of the resurrection of the dead, and day of judgment. And so the Chaldee Paraphrase, *in die judicii magni evigilabunt è domo sepulchri, initio imaginem eorum despicies.* Somewhat we have to that purpose, Psal. 49.14. *the upright shall have dominion over them in the morning, and their beauty,* &c. by some there expressed, *Et figura eorum:* that is, *their form, or figure*. But the word is not the same in the Original: and being it is not the proper sense here, as the most and chiefest agree, we shall not need to take further notice of it.

*{thou shalt despise their image}* In awaking, you, by these judgments, shall spoil and destroy all their pomp, and goodly show. Or, if in awaking, be referred to men; you shall despise; that is, you shall then show them, and make them understand, by opening the eyes of the one, and awaking the consciences of the other, how vain and despicable a thing in very truth, all that pageant of worldly pomp and pride, and life itself, is. An Image is a thing to look upon; good for little else; and valuable for the outward appearance only: though it be done to the life, and may deceive the eye perchance, yet it can have no reality of life. So is all worldly greatness and magnificence; which makes a show, and passes away: well therefore, not here only, but by ancient Grecians expressed by the word εἴδωλον, an idol, or image.

**V. 22. ***{I was as a beast before thee}* A proper expression. For man, though he consist of a soul which is of an immortal nature, as the wisest Heathens have always taught and acknowledged: yet whilst he looks no further than this life, and confines his thoughts to that which is present and visible, he makes himself very beast; the chiefest and almost only considerable difference between man and beast, being this, that the one when it dies, perishes altogether; the other, not; but survives in his better part. And this may be the meaning of Ecclesiastes also, in those words of his, which seem to say the contrary, as many understand them; *I said in my heart concerning the state of the sons of* *men, that God might manifest them, and that they might see that they themselves are beasts; for that which befalleth the sons of men, befalleth beasts,* &c. Eccl. 3.18, 19. That is, as I understand him, that God would awaken them, (as we have it here) and make it appear unto them, that whilst they place all their happiness in this world, and think that when they die, all dies or expires with them; that is in effect, as he does further aggravate, (and that, using their own known words perchance, that lived in that belief, as most in those days: whereof see before upon Psal. 37.1, 2, and elsewhere) *that which befalleth the sons of men, (in point of death, that is,) befalleth beasts; even one thing,* &c. that whilst they so believe and live accordingly, themselves become beasts indeed. For why should he desire God to manifest that unto man, if that were it he intended, that their souls were mortal: which the generality of men have always been apt enough to believe, of themselves; and can so hardly be persuaded to the contrary, even since the contrary has so clearly been revealed, and manifested; as it has been since Christ his appearance in the world? He himself, (Ecclesiastes I mean) explains himself afterwards, ver. 21, when he says, *Who knoweth the spirit of man that goeth upward; and the spirit of beast that goeth downward to the earth:* by which words he puts a clear difference between the spirit of man, and that of beasts; but that this difference, as he professes, does not by any clear evidence, and demonstration, appear unto men: and therefore wishes that God would manifest it. For which cause also Christ is said, as upon the said 37 Psalm is observed, *to have brought life and immortality to light,* 2 Tim. 1.10. I am glad I am not the first, or only, that have thought of this interpretation of this passage of Ecclesiastes: for it is a very considerable passage; and these words of the Psalmist (which I do not find observed by any) give great light unto it. More shall be said of it upon the place. And to this purpose may those words also of the Psalmist, 49.21. *Man that is in honour, and understandeth not, is like the beasts that perish;* be understood. See more there.

*{thee}* In your account, O God.

**V. 23. ***{I am continually with thee}* By faith I was assured, that your providence did watch always over me, to preserve me, Psal. 63.8. Or, in my greatest troubles and afflictions, You wert present with me, by the inward comforts of your Holy Spirit: far more valuable, than any worldly joys, as the best evidence and security of your fatherly love to me, and an assurance of my future happiness with you, in a better world. So Luke 15.31. when the good son, by reason of that extraordinary acceptance there expressed, *the fatted calf,* &c. thought himself less respected by his father, than his riotous brother; and bitterly testified his indignation for it, to his father: *Lo these many years do I serve thee,* &c. (which is just our case here, when we grieve and fret at the prosperity of the wicked, and think ourselves neglected) the good father's answer was; *Son, thou art ever* (here, continually) *with me:* upon which follows a consequence: *all that I have is thine.* As if he had said: By this your being still with me, though you find it not for the present, and my discipline hitherto relish more of severity, than fond indulgence; yet you mayst be sure, and rest upon it with all security, that I intend better things unto you, than a fatted calf, or any thing else, that has been done to your brother, or upon his occasion, in this way of a temporary merriment and rejoicing.

**V. 24. ***{and afterward receive me to glory}* See upon Psal. 49.15. *for he shall receive me:* by comparing of which passage with this the expression, both, and coherence of matter (in opposition to worldly prosperity) being the same in both; it may appear, that this glory was chiefly intended of eternal glory; which is more than will be granted by them that make a Preterit of this Future, and turn it, *cum gloria accepisti me;* or with the vulgar Latin, *cum gloria suscepisti me:* which some interpret as if David, or Asaph for him, had spoken of God's exalting him to the throne of Israel. But keeping the Future tense, which we find in the Original (though often, we confess, where the sense requires it, used for the Present) in its proper signification, according to most Interpreters; yet whether we must take in temporal honor and glory, as part of the meaning, is a further question. I would not deny it, because I see most Interpreters would have it so: yet I cannot see sufficient reason to induce me to believe it. The whole context and tenor of the words in this and the following verses, seems to me to look another way: to wit, to oppose present spiritual joys and comforts and certain hope of eternal life, to temporal transitory goods and blessings: the proper goods and blessings of the wicked, as the matter seems here to be determined; not because they only have them (God forbid we should think so) but because they have these only to trust to, and look for no other. The Chaldee Paraphrast follows this sense: *In consilio tuo deduces me, in mundo isto,* &c. that is, You shall guide me with your counsel in this life; and this life ended, will receive me to that glory, which you have promised me.

**V. 25. ***{Whom have I in heaven but thee?}* He sought neither help nor comfort of any, save of God only.

**V. 26. ***{strength}* Heb. *rock*.

*{my portion for ever}* He teaches us to deny ourselves, and to have God our whole sufficiency, and only contentment, Psal. 16.5 and 119.57.

**V. 27. ***{For lo, they that are far from thee, shall perish}* No man, be he never so godless, is absolutely Ἔρημος τοῦ Θεοῦ; or, *void of God*: as ancient Philosophers speak: *For, in him we live, move, and have our being,* Acts 17.28. It is spoken of all men in general. Yet Plato the Philosopher, of an insolent proud man, says expressly, that he is Ἔρημος τοῦ Θεοῦ, *void of God*; that is, that God is far from his thoughts, and he as far from God's favor. They that are thus far from God, though they have part of the goods of this world, shall never be admitted to that presence of God, wherein is *fulness of joy,* Psal. 16.11, from which to be excluded, is to perish everlastingly: *Depart from me, ye cursed,* &c. Mat. 25.41.

*{thou hast destroyed all them}* השמדת, a Preterit, which, according to the Hebrew idiom may be translated, either by a Present, *succides,* *you do cut off*, or, *destroy,* (as by some it is) or by a Future, *you shall destroy*: which I think would be more proper here.

*{that go a whoring from thee}* That is, forsake you, as a disloyal wife does her husband.

**V. 28. ***{But it is good for me to draw near to God}* Though all the world shrink from God, yet he promises to trust in him, and to magnify his work, Josh. 24.15.

# PSALM LXXIV

## TITLE

*{Maschil of Asaph}* Or, *A Psalm for Asaph to give instruction*, Psal. 32, Tit.

*{of Asaph}* There is no certainty, neither concerning the author of this Psalm, nor concerning the time, when it was composed. First for the author; it is not improbable that Asaph, who lived in David's time, a Prophet, and the author of some sacred hymns, as is recorded, 2 Chron. 29.30, was the author of some of those Psalms in this Book of Psalms, that have his name prefixed; but the occasion of this, having been, as appears by it, some notable profanation and devastation of the Temple, such as happened many ages after in the time of the Babylonish Captivity; and since that, once or twice; this Asaph, that lived in David's time, could not be the author of it. That he should write prophetically so long before, is the opinion of some, I know; but grounded upon very little probability, as I conceive. They that shall read it, and well consider of all particulars, will hardly believe that it is a prophetical style. Others therefore, and those of eminent learning, have rather chosen to refer it to what we read 1 Sam. 4, of the taking of the Ark by the Philistines. It is likely that so judicious a man could have said more to make this opinion more plausible, than it does appear to me: but it is little, that he has said; of which see more upon ver. 6, and *hammers*) and less do I find in the History, to which he does refer us, to persuade me. I should sooner believe what some others have conceived, that either it was another of that Name, who lived in those days, when these things happened; or that the author, however called, delivered it to some of Asaph's posterity, to be tuned and published; and so came to have that name prefixed. But all this is very uncertain: neither does it very much concern. It will more conduce to the right understanding of the Psalm, to know the time, and particular occasion; but, that too, has its difficulties. For if we refer it to Antiochus as Junius, and Bucer are of opinion; he indeed did much profane the Temple, by idolatrous sacrifices, and by erecting his idols in the Sanctuary; but that he made such havoc of these magnificent structures, according to the expressions of the Psalmist, importing little less than an absolute destruction, we read not; and certainly, he did not. It is answered by some, first that these expressions may be intended not of the Temple, but of divers religious houses, called Synagogues, erected in divers places of the land, for religious meetings: of which frequent mention is made in the New Testament. I will easily grant, that Synagogues by some of those expressions might be meant particularly, or comprehended in the generality of the word מועד: which though it be used of the Tabernacle particularly that stood in Moses his time, and of Solomon's Temple afterwards: yet because it properly signifies a meeting; and then, as ἐκκλησία in Greek, a place of meeting; it may fitly be used of a Synagogue also, which is a Greek word almost of the same signification, as if we said, a gathering together; that being the proper use of Synagogues, where people did meet, to pray, and to read, and to expound, and exhort; but not to sacrifice, or to perform any other religious services appointed by the Law, proper to the people only. So מועד may probably be rendered ver. 8. *They have burnt all the Synagogues of God in the land;* as it is there in the English: though some, even there, choose rather to translate *Tabernacles;* by which they understand no other than the Temple, expressed so plurally, (as elsewhere it is, and has been noted before) because of the several rooms and divisions of it. But to leave that as a thing either way not improbable; yet that by קדש in the third verse, where *perpetual desolations* are mentioned: or by מקדש, or משכן in the seventh, *they have cast fire into thy Sanctuary, they have defiled (by casting it down) the dwelling place of thy Name to the ground;* Synagogues should be intended, is altogether unlikely. Neither do those expressions, verse 5 and 6, so well agree to Synagogues (of the magnificence, or costly building of which, we read not elsewhere) as to the Temple. Secondly therefore it is answered, that the Author, or Psalmist, though the particular occasion were Antiochus his present persecution and profanation; yet upon that occasion might describe former sufferings of the Temple by the Chaldeans under Nebuchadnezzar; which, if a man consider not only how much time passed between; but also that they were different Temples, that which was destroyed by the Chaldeans, and that which was profaned afterwards by Antiochus; and apply to this the tenor of the words all along, he will think no ways credible. These things considered, there is more probability that the absolute demolition and destruction of Solomon's magnificent Temple by the Chaldeans should be deplored: neither is there any considerable objection against it, but this, that whereas Ezekiel, Jeremy, Daniel, (all noted Prophets) lived and prophesied in those times; it is said in the 9 verse, *We see not our signs, there is no more any prophet,* &c. But if it be considered how long those desolations lasted before the return of the Israelites out of Babylon; and the rebuilding of the Temple; it may easily be granted that those few named Prophets, did not constantly prophesy, but that there might be a long time of intermission: neither could their prophesying, when they did prophesy, supply the defect of those constant and ordinary Prophets (the word Prophet being of a very large notion and use) or sacred Ministers, which should have administered comfort to the people, and taught them out of the Law, in their several habitations: which they stood so much in need of in this their general calamity. This we take to be the most probable opinion: but we will not impose it upon any others, wherein we acknowledge ourselves not yet fully satisfied.

**V. 1. ***{Why hast thou cast us off for ever?}* The Church of God being oppressed, prays to God, by whom this yoke was laid upon them for their sins in the Babylon captivity, when the Temple was burnt, ver. 7.

**V. 2. ***{the rod of thine inheritance}* Which inheritance, you have measured out for thyself, as with a line or rod.

*{rod}* Or, *tribe*.

**V. 3. ***{Lift up thy feet to perpetual desolations}* The words in the Original, according to the best Interpreters (some embracing the one, others, the other) will afford a double sense: either, Lift up your feet, (steps, or hammers; any of which the Hebrew פעמים will yield,) to perpetual desolations; that is, say some, to requite your enemies and ours, with eternal or final destruction. Or, according to others, *Lift up thy feet;* (that is, make haste, to come and see,) *to perpetual desolations;* that is, those final and irreparable (by human power) destructions and desolations of your sacred Habitations, which the fury of our merciless enemies has wrought. This I take to be the more probable of the two. We often, according to the Scripture phrase, desire God to look upon us when we desire him to be merciful, to our assistance: and king Hezekiah, though he knew God was not ignorant what Sennacherib of Assyria, had written to him; yet *he went up to the House of the Lord,* says the Text, *and spread it before the Lord,* 2 Kings 19. The Letter was not more in the sight of God by it, than it was before; but Hezekiah made his faith and confidence in God by that the more to appear to the world, which prevailed much with him.

**V. 4. ***{Thine enemies roar in the midst of thy Congregation}* Cry out as conquerors.

*{their ensigns}* In sign of victory over us and our Religion.

**V. 5. ***{A man was famous, according as he had lifted up}* The words may seem to imitate the nature of the thing that is spoken of. סבך here translated *thick,* properly signifies *perplexity:* as it is here word for word expressed by some. Intricacy is intended, that is certain, whether in wood, or work: and there is much intricacy in the words, which makes Interpreters much to differ in their Expositions. Some subtle Grammarian would have made much of it, had he found the like in Homer, or Virgil. But we need not be so curious. If we can but make plain sense of this perplexity, it will serve our turn. Not therefore to take notice of every less difference, which would perplex the Reader perchance, more than satisfy: the main is, whether the founders, or confounders, the builders or destroyers of the Temple, are intended in these words. The *but,* which the next verse begins with, being properly *adversativa particula,* has moved many to conceive an antithesis between the two verses: which to make good, there being no doubt at all of the meaning of the next verse, they have searched, in this, where the obscurity has left a liberty for variety of conjectures, upon this sense: *He that (heretofore) in the perplexity of wood*, or, *in the thick wood (did cut down trees) with the ax*, (for, or towards the building of the Temple; must be understood) *was known (as famous*; or, *in as much reputation*) *as if he had offered unto the High (God)* or, *as if he had offered sacrifices*: or: *offered (to God) on high*; that is, in heaven.) This is the sense followed and prosecuted by the chiefest Rabbins: by whom we are among other things, referred to Zach. 4.7. where indeed we find somewhat that may seem not altogether impertinent: *and he shall bring forth the head-stone thereof with shoutings, crying, Grace, grace unto it.* Others thinking this to be too far fetched, make this to be the sense; *A man (among those merciless enemies) was famous, according as he did lift up his ax*, (or, *for lifting up of his ax*) *as if he had lifted it upon the thick trees*; that is, He that carried himself in this business with most barbarousness and cruelty, that had no more regard either to the holiness of the place, or the curiosity of the work, than if he had been at work in the wood, felling down trees, or any thing else promiscuously; he had most credit, and was most applauded among them. Or, as some others, *according as he did lift up his ax upon the wrought and carved work* (or, *infolded graven work*) *of the Temple.* This I should easily grant to be the most likely sense that can be made of the words, if we could tell what to do with that of the next verse. In the Hebrew it is ועתה, which properly signifies *And now:* which is by some thus made to cohere with the former words: This cruel barbarous enemy, after this destruction (what by fire, what with axes and hammers,) of the Temple, has not yet done: his fury is not yet assuaged: but even now, he takes into the ruins and rubbish of those glorious structures; and whatever he can find, that is not yet wholly abolished, or defaced, he does exercise his rage upon it: careful, that nothing should be preserved as a monument of that quondam so glorious, and so magnificent fabric: the very ruins whereof, but for this their insatiable rage and malice, could not but have been precious. So Junius, and some others.

**V. 6. ***{But now they break down the carved work}* Hebrew ואת. This is the reading in the Text: in the margin ועתה, long ago by St. Jerome translated, *Et nunc,* And now. Which I note the rather, because, since that, some have made bold to tamper with the Hebrew, to bring it to the Vulgar Latin: herein much different from the Hebrew. So also פתוחיה, by the same Jerome translated *valvulas ejus,* according to the proper signification of it; some would have to signify here, *januas ejus* (*the gates or doors*,) but why I know not; except it be because of its affinity with פתח, which comes from the same *radix*, and signifies *janua,* *a door*. This difference is the more considerable, because should we grant *janua* to be the right meaning of the word, there would be ground for an argument, that the sufferings of the Temple, by Antiochus, should be the subject (of which, see more upon the title) of the Psalm, forasmuch as the gates of the Temple, though not any other part of it, at that time, were burnt by the said Antiochus: as we read in the History of the Maccabees, 1 Mac. 4.38.

*{and hammers}* Heb. וכילפות. *Vox Philistina,* (says a learned man here) *ad notandum tempus.* His meaning is, that this word being as he deems it, of the Philistine tongue, by others called, *Lingua Palaestina,* was here used of purpose, for an indication of the time; that is, that by that word it might be known in after ages when those things were done, and by whom, which are spoken of in this Psalm. Now his opinion is, of which has been spoken already upon the title of the Psalm, that that taking of the Ark by the Philistines, related 1 Sam. 4, and what happened upon it, was it that gave occasion to all these complaints. The title, I believe, and name of Asaph there prefixed, was his chiefest ground: of which see more there. As for this word, I know no ground at all he had, to say it was a Philistine word, except he first take it for granted, (which is the main controversy, and will not, I think, be easily granted by any, except his bare Authority persuade them) that the Philistines, not Chaldeans, or Grecians, or Romans, are they that are here spoken of. Rabbi Solomon makes the word to be an Arabic word, wherein he is followed by many. Junius makes it a Syriac word, both this and the former כשיל, and has the same observation upon them, in confirmation of his own opinion, that therefore Syriac words, and not Hebrew, are used, *ad evidentiorem temporis quo haec gesta sunt designationem.* It seems neither of them are elsewhere to be found in the Scripture: which makes them to be suspected for strange words. But that, as I conceive, is but a weak argument. For there be many other of that kind, not occurring above once in the whole Bible, which nevertheless are acknowledged to be Hebrew. And how can it be otherwise, but that as in all other writings, of no greater bulk especially, some words are found, but once used, by some one Author; which nevertheless in some other, are more frequent: so it should be in the Bible too; which, were there any other ancient Authors written in the Hebrew tongue now extant besides the Bible, we might haply find to be the case of these two words. Neither is it any argument that they are not Hebrew words, because now found in the Arabic, or Syriac: which two Languages, as all men know, are but Dialects of the Hebrew, and have preserved to us many Hebrew words, as by all learned in those tongues is acknowledged, which otherwise might altogether have perished. There is not any thing in the sound or pronunciation of either of these, as in divers other strange words that are found in the Scripture, which should give us occasion to suspect it: and it is some argument that they should be Hebrew rather, because from them, as some learned Etymologists have judged, some Greek words, (as many others, yea most, that are held Greek words, out of the Hebrew, in general,) have been formed: as out of the last and most suspected, particularly, κολάπτω; and some others. This may be answered concerning the Syriac or Arabic. But for the Philistine, of which nothing now remains, why either of these words should be attributed to that Language, I know no ground at all. It is but a bare conceit, and needs no further answer, than a bare denial. Some learned men are of opinion, that it was a mixed language out of the Hebrew and Egyptian: which they would ground upon St. Jerome's words, concerning the Canaanitish Tongue. It is called in the Scripture, *the speech of Ashdod,* Nehem. 13.24. but that does not concern us here.

**V. 7. ***{They have cast fire into thy Sanctuary}* Heb. *they have sent your Sanctuary into the fire*, 2 Kings 25.9.

**V. 8. ***{They said in their hearts}* They were so cruel, that they purposed to destroy God's people, and to suppress all Religion.

*{destroy}* Heb. *break*.

*{in the land}* For there were synagogues in other places besides Jerusalem, to pray in, and read the Law, and instruct the people, Luke 4.15 and 7.5. Acts 15.21. See more upon the Title.

**V. 9. ***{We see not our signs}* Miracles, as signs of deliverance.

*{There is no more any Prophet}* For Ezekiel prophesied but to the fourteenth year, after the destruction of Jerusalem, Ezek. 40.1. and it is likely Jeremiah was dead before: or, it is spoken in the person of those who knew not of any Prophets then being, though there might be some, 1 Kings 19.10. See more upon the Title.

*{neither is there among us any that knoweth how long}* They lament that they have no Prophet among them, to show them how long their miseries should endure, and when they might expect deliverance.

**V. 12. ***{in the midst of the earth}* Meaning, in the sight of all the world.

**V. 13. ***{Thou didst divide the sea}* Exod. 14.21.

*{divide}* Heb. *break*.

*{of the dragons}* The Captains of Pharaoh's Army, Exod. 15.4.

*{dragons}* Or, *whales*, as Psal. 44.19.

**V. 14. ***{the heads of the leviathan}* The great power of Pharaoh.

*{to the people inhabiting the wilderness}* To the wild beasts and fowls in the desert, who fed on their carcasses, being cast on shore, Ezek. 38.20. Psal. 79.2. So ants and conies are called people, Prov. 30.25, 26.

**V. 15. ***{Thou didst cleave the fountain and the flood}* Exod. 17.5. Num. 20.11.

*{thou driedst up the mighty rivers}* Josh. 3.13.

*{mighty rivers}* Heb. *rivers of strength*.

**V. 16. ***{The day is thine}* Seeing that God, by his providence, governs and disposes all things, he prays, that he would take special care for his children. See also upon Psal. 33.6. *By the word of the Lord.*

*{Thou hast prepared the light and the sun}* It is likely that the Psalmist does here allude to the story of the Creation, where we first read of the *light* by itself, Gen. 1, ver. 3, and afterwards of the sun, v. 14, &c. where though light was made essential to the sun; and the chiefest light (material light) of the world, reduced into that body: yet still light, in general, (which is in divers natural things, besides the sun) is a different thing from the sun itself, and the sun, not from light only considerable, but for other properties different from light, though (as in the sun) inseparable from it. The vulgar Latin expresses it, *auroram:* which certainly came from the Hebrew word here in the Text: but it is more probable, that it was intended to express the Greek φαῦσις, which signifies the same: and for the sense, it might be understood of that light seen both mornings and evenings, when the sun itself (from which it proceeds) is not seen. The Rabbins, with the Chaldee Paraphrast, by *light* here understand the Moon; and they give this reason, because, say they, it is the idiom of the Holy Tongue, to call the worst, or more ignoble of the same kind, by the word of the *genus:* whereof they give an example, ver. 2, of this Psalm, *thy hand, and thy right hand;* that is, as they interpret it, both hands. But if they have no better ground for their observation, I should not much regard it. For in those words, *thy hand, and* (or, *even;* as the ו may import as well) *thy right hand;* it is much more probable that one and the same hand should be intended; and so the speech is more emphatical: your hand, even your right hand: a hand of power and terror, such as our present condition does require. The *right hand of God,* so used proverbially, see upon Psal. 80.17. *Let thy hand,* &c. Neither is it to be wondered, if the Moon be not here expressly or particularly mentioned: of which see upon Psal. 72, ver. 7. *So long as the moon and.* If that which was set down at the first, does not satisfy; I think we may probably conclude, that by *the light and the sun,* may be meant no more, than barely, *the light of the sun,* by a figure used in all languages, called by Grammarians, ἓν διὰ δυοῖν: that is, when one thing is expressed by two, severally, as Gen. 3.15, 16. *I will greatly multiply thy sorrow and thy conception;* that is, the labor, or sorrow of your conception: and *of the hope and resurrection of the dead:* (that is, of the hope of the return of the dead) Acts 23.6. *Paterâ libamus & auro:* a noted example; and a thousand such in Greek and Latin Authors.

*{and the sun}* Or, that is, *the sun*.

**V. 17. ***{Thou hast set all the borders of the earth}* By dividing sea and land, Acts 17.26.

*{thou hast made summer and winter}* Gen. 8.22.

*{made}* Heb. *made them*.

**V. 19. ***{of thy turtledove}* He means the Church of God, Cant. 2.14 and 5.2 and 6.9, which is exposed as a prey to the wicked.

**V. 20. ***{Have respect unto the covenant}* Either that with Abraham, Psal. 105.8, 9, &c. *He hath remembered his covenant for ever,* &c. *which he made with Abraham, and his oath with Isaac,* &c. Or that with David, 2 Sam. 7.16. *And thine house, and thy Kingdom shall be established for ever before thee,* &c. and 23.5. *He hath made with me an everlasting Covenant,* &c. See also Psal. 89.34. *My Covenant will I not break,* &c. Some refer us to Zach. 9.11. where the Covenant in Christ's blood, common to all Nations, is mentioned: which cannot be proper here. Others, with more likelihood, to the Covenant with Noah, Gen. 6.13, &c. as if he would say, that the earth was in danger of another flood, if God did not prevent it, because of this wonderful prevailing of wickedness and cruelty in all parts of it.

*{for the dark places of the earth are full of the habitations of cruelty}* That is, all places where your Word shines not, are full of tyranny and ambition. Or, more literally and probably, that the land, through these desolations and devastations that the enemy had made, now altogether dark, (that is, wild and desert-like) was become an habitation of thieves and murderers: full of robberies, and all other violence.

**V. 21. ***{O let not the oppressed return ashamed}* Deny not their suit, lest they be ashamed; but deliver them, that they may praise you.

**V. 22. ***{plead thine own cause}* He shows, that God cannot suffer his Church to be oppressed, except he lose his own right.

*{increaseth}* Heb. *ascendeth*.

# PSALM LXXV

## TITLE

*{Altaschith}* Or, *Destroy not*. See Notes on the Title of Psal. 57.

*{A Psalm or Song}* See Notes on the Title of Psal. 30.

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph*: for it seems that David made it, vers. 3. See upon Psal. 50, the title.

**Verse 1. ***{For that thy Name is near}* See upon Psal. 9.10. *that know thy Name.*

*{thy wondrous works declare}* God's great works for his, show that he is near to them.

**V. 2. ***{When I shall receive}* Or, *when I shall take a set time*.

*{the Congregation}* The whole Kingdom of Israel; for it seems then he had Judah only subject unto him. Or, when I shall have conquered Jerusalem, the place appointed for public meetings.

*{I will judge uprightly}* Heb. *uprightnesses*; that is, most uprightly.

**V. 3. ***{The earth and all the inhabitants thereof are dissolved}* The Land is divided into factions; but I will heal them by just government.

*{earth}* Or, *land*.

**V. 4. ***{Lift not up the horn}* Zach. 1.21. Against me, whom God has appointed to rule over you.

**V. 5. ***{speak not with a stiff neck}* Submit your necks to the yoke of my government.

**V. 6. ***{For promotion cometh neither from the East}* For God has set up me for a King, and will destroy them that oppose my kingdom.

*{nor from the south}* Heb. *from the wilderness*: and so contains both north and south; for there was a wilderness on both sides of Canaan. So Junius, and some others, of this place: thereby endeavoring to prevent their curiosity, who if either *south,* as it is here in the Text, and in divers other Translations; or *north,* as in others, (Vatablus, &c.) were particularly expressed, and one of the two, as by consequent it must, left out: to prevent their curiosity, I say, who would be ready to ask why in this specification of the parts or quarters of the world three being named, the fourth only should be omitted: and if of purpose omitted, why *promotion* should not be thought by the Psalmist as well to come from the north, or south, (one of them being left out, according to most Translations) as from either east, or west, &c. Now the matter is answered, or rather the question prevented, if the desert, or wilderness here does comprehend both. But this by some of the chief, for learning and judgment, is proposed but very tenderly; rather as the opinion of others, than their own: because indeed without example, and of itself not so probable. For though we read of a wilderness, 1 Kings 19.15, which stood on the north; yet is not that called *the wilderness,* absolutely, as that which stood on the south, is often: but expressed by its proper name, *the wilderness of Damascus.* And upon such an occasion as this; in a matter of such moment, and so solemnly delivered, as this is; it is not very probable, that David would contract himself by such an ambiguous expression, when he might almost as shortly, but much more emphatically have said: *Nor from the North, or South.* But besides; *promotion,* implying wealth and honor; and a *wilderness,* a place of penury and misery; (*They wandered in the wilderness,* &c. *Hungry, and thirsty,* &c. Psal. 107.4, 5.) being in themselves so repugnant, though it be said, that by a *metonymy,* or what other figure, by *wilderness,* not a wilderness properly; but either North, or South, or both are intended; yet still there is some kind of incongruity, or impropriety in the business, that such a word should be used upon such an occasion, rather than either North, or South, or both. For the vulgar indeed (*Quia neque ab Oriente, neque ab Occidente, neque a desertis montibus, quoniam Deus,* &c.) where no *promotion* is mentioned, but help, or assistance, or deliverance to be supplied: why *desert mountains* should be mentioned, some kind of reason is given by them that are set to maintain that Translation, be it never so false, or ridiculous; because, say they, people that come from barren desert places, are commonly (as using to live upon the rapine, because their own country will not maintain them) more fierce, and warlike, than those that live in better countries. This has some show of reason: but will not help us, who follow (and upon better grounds, I hope, though the Chaldee too go much that way:) a different, both sense, and translation. For my part, though for the sense in general, I differ not from the rest: yet for the ground of the expression, (not less considerable than the sense itself in this place:) I am of a far different opinion; which though I find nowhere else, yet I conceive grounded upon so much probability, as that I shall not be afraid to propose it. I say then first of all, that there be many secret allusions in the Scriptures, in later books especially, to the books of Moses, the most ancient, and most known of all the Scripture; which if not observed (as I believe they are not always) must needs breed great obscurity. We observed one upon the former psalm verse 16. *Thou hast prepared:* but that as probably only, as is there showed. But Psal. 19.12, 13. *Who can understand,* &c. and, *Keep back thy servant,* — *So shall I be innocent,* &c. There is no question but that the Psalmist in all that passage does allude to Numb. 15.27, 30, which is so apparent, that most Expositors upon the place do refer thither in general: though when they come to the handling of particular words, as to tell us, why *presumptuous sins,* called *the great transgression,* &c. they seem to have forgotten their quotation: and seek for reasons and interpretations elsewhere, which that place only, if it be granted that David does allude unto it, does afford.

Now as concerning this place we are now upon, my opinion is, that the Psalmist does allude by these words (if he do not use some common and proverbial speech among the Jews, grounded at first upon that Story: which I think as probable:) to the Story of Balak and Balaam, as recorded, Numb. ch. 23, and ch. 24. Here first we have three places expressed from whence *promotion* should come. So there Balaam, expecting great *promotion* from Balak, in case he could please him by his prophecies, Numb. 22.17, 37, and 24.11, did three several times; 23.10, and in three several places: (Ch. 23.3, 14, and Ch. 24.1.) apply himself, turning himself to several quarters of the World, to curse Israel. Whether he turned himself East, and West, the two first times, is not expressed in the Scripture: but only that he stood upon a *high place,* the first time, Ch. 23.3. Upon *the top of Mount Pisga* the second time, verse 14, the situation of which places though we know, (as may be easily known of Mount Pisga:) yet that will not help us, except it were expressed withal, how his face was turned. But that he stood East and West, may most probably be inferred by common tradition among the Jews, because it is directly said of the last or third place, that he did *set his face towards the wilderness,* (the very same word in the Original, המדבר, which is here used by the Psalmist, though the Hebrews want not variety of words to express a *wilderness:*) Ch. 24, first verse. And that very *phrase of setting his face towards,* has an intimation of some request, or expectation of accomplishment, to come from that place, towards which the face is turned, and words of prayers, or wishes directed. See upon Psal. 5.7. *towards thy holy temple.* And whereas it is said of Hezekiah, 2 Kings 20.2. that he *turned his face to,* or towards (for it is the same word אל in the Original:) *the wall:* the Chaldee Paraphrast, as I remember (for I have not the book at this time:) does refer it to that custom. That Balaam's chief aim in removing from place to place, if by any means could be inspired to his mind, was his own profit and *promotion,* besides what is thence to be learned in the History; we have further confirmation from 2 Pet. 2.15. *the wages of unrighteousness;* and upon the 11 verse, *for reward;* besides Nehem. 13.2. *hired Balaam.*

Now, when Balaam had tried so many places, and so many (as often as he changed place) several quarters and prospects of the heavens, to satisfy his ambition, highly inflamed by King Balak his goodly promises: what was the issue? The Story tells us; *And Balak's anger was kindled against Balaam,* &c. *and said, Therefore now flee thou to thy place: I thought to promote thee unto great honour: but I see the LORD hath kept thee back from honour,* Numb. 24.10, 11. And is there not ground enough, think we, from all these particulars in this Story, to infer, and to make a proverb, or common speech (from so remarkable, so memorable a History) of it: *That promotion cometh neither from the East, nor from the West, nor from the wilderness: But God is the judge: he putteth down one, and setteth up another.* If this be not plain, I know not what is: neither do I know of any one objection that can be made against it. Yet I have taken the more pains to make it plain, because the silence of so many learned men that have written upon the Psalms, might make a man suspect, what otherwise would be undoubtable. I confess I have them not all that have written upon the Psalms: not so many by far, as I had when these Annotations were first printed. Yet I have the chiefest and generally best accounted of, now in sight: and in them I am sure I find no such thing so much as mentioned. But if to be found any where else, I shall be so much the gladder, that the truth (the only thing we should all aim at) may have so much more confirmation. Now if this be a right interpretation, which we have all this while labored about; then it does clearly follow, that either *North* as some, or *South,* as our English here, in the Text; with divers others: instead of *desert,* as in the margin, or *wilderness;* (which I like better of because it is the word used Numb. 24, first verse:) is a bad, that I say not false, translation, and does much obscure the reference to the Story without which no good and satisfactory reason (as before said and showed) can be given, why the Psalmist should use such a kind of speech. As for the matter of these words, it is more fully (more emphatically it cannot be) expressed 1 Chr. 29.11, 12, by the same David, (if David were the Author, as is most probable, though Asaph's name be to it:) *Thine O Lord, is the greatness, and the power, and the glory,* &c. *both riches and honour come of thee, — to make great, and to give strength unto all.* Which nevertheless is not so generally, and absolutely to be taken, but that it is liable to divers exceptions, and restrictions. The *Mammon of unrighteousness,* would not have so many servants, if he had not wherewith to reward them: nor so many wicked men, come to highest places of power and preferment, did they not act by some other power, than the power and Spirit of God. And though we do not believe, nay we know to be most false, what the Devil did once arrogate to himself, that all the Kingdoms of the world are at his disposing, Luke 4.5, 6. Yet it is not probable that by Christ himself he would have been styled the *Prince of this world,* John 12.31, &c. or by his Apostle, *The god:* 2 Cor. 4.4, had not he great power given him in the things of this present world. It is enough for us to be certain, that he can do nothing, no, not in the meanest things, or against the meanest men of the world, without God's permission, who does restrain, and confine his power as Himself pleases, and makes his own purposes to stand, and his promises to take effect against all opposition of either men or devils, with as much (yea greater incomparably) facility, as the greatest and most absolute Monarch of the world can crush the meanest of his subjects. St. Chrysostom does often affirm, that wicked men have not their wealth from God: which rightly understood, is true enough: nor repugnant to this place. But I must end this Note.

**V. 7. ***{God is the Judge}* Psal. 50.6.

*{he putteth down one, and setteth up another}* 1 Sam. 2.7. Psal. 113.7.

**V. 8. ***{For in the hand of the Lord there is a cup, &c.}* A cup alone is an ordinary similitude, for ordinary troubles and afflictions, Matt. 20.22, 23. *Ye shall drink of my cup,* &c. to which purpose the ancient Latins were wont to say, *Ex eodem poculo bibere;* as is found in some of them. But a cup of *red wine,* as we have it here: or, *of wrath and fury;* as by others it is expressed: (as Jer. 25.15. Revel. 14.10.) imports, usually, extraordinary judgments of God upon some great provocation. How here intended, best Interpreters are divided. Some apprehend an antithesis in the words, (in this our English observed) *but the dregs thereof,* &c. whereupon they make this sense: That God, the supreme moderator, has appointed troubles, more or less, and afflictions to all men, both good and bad in this world: but that he deals them in a certain measure and proportion: that is, to his people, out of his fatherly care over them, afflictions so moderated and limited, that they shall not be overcome by them, but overcome them to their own further good, and to his glory: (this to be intended by the first part of the similitude; *it is full of mixture: and he poureth out of the same*) but, to the wicked and godless, sorrows and vexations without stint or measure; terrible judgments; ending in despair and utter destruction: this to be intended, *by the dregs of his cup,* or wine: *wrung out and drunk, by all the wicked of the earth.* The sense is plausible. But if we compare with other like places, where this similitude is used, there will appear no such difference; but that all promiscuously, whether they be God's people, (in outward profession at least) or others, are said to drink of the same cup, and mixture: not as is here conceived, the one to drink the wine; and the other, the dregs. So Isa. 51.17. *Awake, awake, stand up, O Jerusalem, which hast drunk at the hand of the Lord the cup of his fury; thou hast drunk the dregs of the cup of trembling, and wrung them out.* So Ezek. 23.32. *Thou shalt drink of thy sister's cup, deep and large,* &c. (it is spoken to Judah) *the cup of astonishment and desolation,* &c. *Thou shalt even drink it, and suck it out:* the word is מיץ; the same as is here translated, *to wring out*. (מיץ שמרים, shall wring them out, and drink them) See also Jerem. 25.15, 16, 17, &c. and Revel. 14.10. So that this intention of dregs here, is, if interpreted by these places, rather, by way of aggravation, to set out the greatness of either God's anger, or judgment, than by way of antithesis, as is conceived. And so indeed is this place expounded by very good Interpreters; as Calvin for one. Neither is there any necessity of turning this אך by a *veruntamen,* or *but:* it may as well be translated, *utique,* (certainly, truly,) as here it is by divers; and of necessity must be so sometimes, where a *but* would spoil the sense. As for example, Psal. 39.6. *Surely every man walketh,* &c. there be two אך there; both translated, *utique,* or *surely.* See more of the like upon Psal. 73.1. *Truly God,* &c. Nevertheless, because the former interpretation is approved by the greater number, and is of itself, as to the Original words very well warrantable, I am not against it, if any shall like it better. The πιθοπλασία of the ancient Heathens, of Gods dealing good and evil out of one, as Hesiod; or two several vessels unto men, as Homer, had some resemblance to this: and perchance might have its first beginning from some such expression.

*{it is full of mixture}* Some make a mystery of this *mixture,* as though it were intended by this word, That though the godly be not exempted from troubles and crosses, yet they have them tempered by God, with proportionable comforts; and so moderated, that they do not exceed the measure of their ability to bear them. A good doctrine, but very weakly grounded upon this word. For certainly they are in the right, who by *mixture,* understand liquor mixed; that is, fitted and prepared; and now ready for drinking. Who knows not that the Greek κεράννυσθαι, and the Latin *miscere,* are very commonly so taken; the reason being, (as many have well observed) because the ancients were not wont to drink wine, nor think it fit to be drunk, (hot wines, and in hot countries especially) before it were mixed and tempered with water? And so we find the word κεράννυσθαι used Revel. 14.10. *The same shall drink of the wine of the wrath of God which is poured out without mixture:* τὸ κεκερασμένον ἀκράτου. The words, if translated word for word, would imply a contradiction; *mixed without mixture:* but, the meaning is, as it is well expressed by the English, *poured out:* and that *without mixture,* there, is but an interpretation of what is here termed, *red wine:* and so indeed it is here too in the translation of the Septuagint, (ποτήριον ἐν χειρὶ κυρίου οἴνου ἀκράτου.) So that here too, would be the same contradiction, if this *mixture* were taken in the proper signification. They therefore that have censured this interpretation, a *dilute* interpretation, may recall their jest, and acknowledge their error.

**V. 10. ***{All the horns of the wicked also will I cut off}* I will in my reign pull down bad men, and set up good.

*{cut off}* Jer. 48.25.

# PSALM LXXVI

## TITLE

*{Neginoth}* See the Notes on the Title of Psal. 4.

*{A Song or Psalm}* See the Notes on the Title of Psal. 30.

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph*.

**Verse 1. ***{In Judah is God known}* By this word, and by the destruction of their adversaries. This Psalm is of the same subject, as Psal. 46. *God is our refuge and strength,* &c. and perchance written upon the same occasion. Some notable victory, doubtless; whether over the Ammonites, as some think, or any other. Both Psalms compared together, will illustrate one another.

**V. 2. ***{In Salem also is his Tabernacle}* Which afterward was called Jerusalem.

**V. 4. ***{than the mountains of prey}* Kingdoms full of extortion and rapine, compared to mountains full of ravening beasts; or to mountains where robbers use to shelter themselves, and lie in wait for prey, Amos 4.1. Or, *more glorious than the mountains of prey,* is a proverbial expression and amplification of greatest glory and exultation. For so the words are used to that purpose in some other places of Scripture: *They joy before thee, according to the joy in harvest, and as men rejoice, when they divide the spoil,* Isa. 9.3 and 53.12. *Therefore will I divide him a portion with the great, and he shall divide the spoil with the strong.* See also upon 62.10. *Trust not in oppression,* &c.

**V. 5. ***{they have slept their sleep}* Their last sleep, or sleep of death; as in the next verse. And some, not improbably, conceive, that this might have reference to that great and miraculous defeat of the Assyrians by night, related 2 Kings 19.35, which happened in King Hezekiah his time. If so, then we know both the time and particular occasion of the Psalm: neither is there any thing in the whole Psalm, but may very well stand with that conjecture. This of their sleep, repeated again in the next verse, makes it very probable.

*{and none of the men of might have found their hands}* Some think is meant, that God took away their courage, and that their wonted strength failed them in the battle. But if it were meant of the Assyrians, as we have mentioned upon the former words, there was no fight at all. And besides, having told us of their last sleep in the words immediately before these, it is not so likely that he should in the same verse, touch upon their faint and heartless carriage in fight. More likely therefore, that these words have relation to the said sleep. A man that is in a sound sleep, may upon the working of his phantasy, strive and endeavor much, as either to rise, or to run, or perhaps to defend himself; but he finds neither legs nor hands, till he be awaked. Virgil the Poet, upon a like occasion, speaking of one, (a mighty and invincible man otherwise) whom he feigns in his encounter with Aeneas, suddenly to have been bereft of his wonted power and strength by the gods, in favor to the said *pius Aeneas,* has the very same similitude, of a man striving in his sleep to no purpose:

*Ac veluti in somnis oculos ubi languida pressit
Nocte quies, nequicquam avidos extendere cursus
Velle videmur,
Et in mediis conatibus aegri Succidimus: non lingua valet, non corpore notae
Sufficiunt vires: nec vox, nec verba sequuntur:
Sic Turno (quacunque viam virtute petivit) Successum Dea dira negat,* &c.

No man can wish for a better comment upon these words of the Psalmist. But I will not do the Poet so much wrong, as to go about to English him, neither in prose, or verse.

**V. 7. ***{who may stand in thy sight}* God, with a look, is able to destroy all the power and activity of the enemies, were they never so many or mighty.

**V. 8. ***{from heaven}* You have delivered us by your power, manifested as from heaven, Psal. 57.3.

**V. 9. ***{to judgment}* To revenge the wrongs done to your Church, and to deliver it.

**V. 10. ***{Surely the wrath of man shall praise thee}* The rage of your foes, and ours, will turn to your praise, who being overcome, your people shall praise you for their deliverance. And the more cruel they, the greater our deliverance, and our thanks accordingly: Or, as some others, the enemies themselves confounded and convicted, shall acknowledge that it is your doing, and shall be humbled before you.

*{the remainder of wrath shalt thou restrain}* Either they that remain and survive of the people, 2 Kings 19.30. *And the remnant that is escaped of the house of Judah,* &c. Or, you shall prevent the further attempts of our enraged enemies. You will suffer them to do no more in their fury, than may turn to your glory.

*{restrain}* Heb. *gird*. That is, keep it within compass, as with a girdle.

**V. 11. ***{Vow, and pay unto the Lord your God}* Psal. 56.12. Eccl. 5.4, 5.

*{let all that be round about him bring presents}* To wit, the Levites, that dwell about the Tabernacle: or, the people among whom God does dwell. See upon Psal. 89.7. *of all that are about him.*

*{unto}* Heb. *to fear*.

**V. 12. ***{He shall cut off the spirit of Princes}* Heb. יבצר, by some translated, *vindemiabit,* that is, he will gather as grapes. As men gather grapes, so shall he easily and suddenly take away their lives, and disappoint their enterprises. The metaphor shows, That the destruction of the mightiest, with God, is but as the nipping of a bunch of grapes with men.

# PSALM LXXVII

## TITLE

*{to Jeduthun}* Psal. 39, tit.

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph*.

**Verse 1. ***{I cried unto God with my voice}* The Prophet teaches us by his example to flee unto God for help in our necessities.

**V. 2. ***{my sore}* Heb. *my hand.* If taken properly, he means, *My hand was stretched out in prayer*; or, *bedewed with my tears*.

*{ran}* Or, *was stretched out*.

*{refused to be comforted}* Jer. 31.15.

**V. 3. ***{and was troubled}* This may be referred to the immediate words (his *remembering* of God: by which most understand praying: either as an adjunct, or a consequent. If as an adjunct; then, importing the commotion and agitation of his soul in his meditation, or prayer: which often happens in fervent prayers, upon earnest occasions. Such a prayer was that of Hannah, 1 Sam. 1.10, &c. and that of Christ, Heb. 5.7. *with strong crying and tears,* &c. and some naturally express more passion in their prayers, than others, who nevertheless may pray as earnestly, and effectually; as on the other side, we should not rashly condemn others for their earnestness, except it be very manifest by their lives, or some other convincing circumstance, that they are hypocrites. If we take the word as a consequent of the former action, then the meaning is, that his very prayer or meditation, to which in his anguish and extremity, he did betake himself for comfort, added to his grief, rather than eased it. A thing very remarkable, and incidental, as to David, then; so to the best of men, at all times, So it pleases God sometimes, to deal with them that are most dear unto him. He does withdraw himself, as to present sense (though most present sometimes, when he seems farthest off:) and will not be found in a good while; and all for our good, in due time, if we have patience to the end; and submit to his ways and wisdom, though to us incomprehensible. We must not give over praying for all that, except it be for a while, in case our present trouble be ready to overcome us: especially, if there be any distemper of body concurring at the same time. He might also say that he was *troubled,* when he *remembered* God; meaning, that when he thought upon former times, how good and gracious then; how different now (in appearance) it troubled him very much: as having just occasion to fear, that God had quite cast him, or them off; then which nothing could be more dismal and terrible. The Greek here ἐθυραύσθην: and after them the Latins, *delectatus sum:* a quite contrary sense, for which though there might be some ground from the use of the word in some other place, yet it seems here contrary to the coherence.

**V. 4. ***{Thou holdest mine eyes waking}* God laid so many sorrows on him, that he could not sleep.

*{I am so troubled that I cannot speak}* As one that is astonied with some sore blow received.

**V. 5. ***{I have considered the days of old}* Psal. 143.5.

**V. 6. ***{my song in the night}* Of thanksgiving, which I was accustomed to sing in my prosperity.

*{I commune with mine own heart}* Psal. 4.4.

*{my spirit made diligent search}* For the causes of this great change, and the means to recover my former happy estate.

**V. 7. ***{Will the Lord cast off for ever?}* Psal. 13.1.

**V. 8. ***{for evermore}* Heb. *to generation and generation*.

**V. 10. ***{I said this is my infirmity}* See upon the next words, *I will remember.*

*{I will remember the years of the right hand of the most High.}* The ambiguity of the word שנות, which may be translated either *years,* as it is here; or *mutations,* that is, *changes,* as by divers; has occasioned much variety both of Translations, and Expositions. If, *years,* the most probable sense will be (which he further prosecutes in the following verses) that to his present infirmity, and doubts, he has opposed the consideration of former times, when God did use his *right hand;* that is, did show his wonderful power either in particular deliverances afforded to himself in his necessity; or public, to his people in general in their extremities, when oppressed by their enemies, and the like. So Psal. 74, after a long complaint of their present condition, ver. 12, 13, &c. he remembers, and relates what God had formerly done for them; *For God is my King,* &c. *Thou didst divide the sea,* &c. and so in many places besides. But if, *changes,* then this: *This is my infirmity; it is a change of the right hand of the most High;* as if he said, Why should this change of the right hand of God towards me, this alteration of my former estate and condition, from good to bad, so much trouble me, or seem so strange? As I have received good, why not evil also from the same hand? *Shall we receive good at the hand of God,* &c. Job 2.10. and since we are born to adversity, as well as prosperity (Eccl. 7.14.) and, *If thou faint in the day of adversity, thy strength is small,* Prov. 24.10. well mayst you then acknowledge your infirmity. There be sundry other interpretations, to be found in Expositors, besides these, according to both Translations, either *years,* or *changes:* but these, the most approved, and in my judgment, the only probable. Some, for, *this is my infirmity,* translate, *infirmari me fecit?* or, *should I be sick for this?* but it comes all to one. Some again, *Et cogitavi, mors mea est.* Or, *Et dixi, Occidere meum:* as if he intended to say, that in his sudden thoughts; in the extremity of his sufferings, he gave himself over for a dead man: he despaired even of life: (2 Cor. 1.8.) *tunc recordatus sum,* &c. Then did he call to mind. It is Vatablus his translation and interpretation; which for his sake I take notice of. So it would be the same as Psal. 31.22. *I said in my haste, I am cut off,* &c. See there, and Psal. 116.11. *I said in my haste,* &c.

**V. 11. ***{I will remember thy wonders of old}* Psal. 44, verse 1.

**V. 13. ***{Thy way, O God, is in the sanctuary}* Your manner of proceeding is to be learned in your word; which is taught in the sanctuary, Psal. 73.17.

**V. 14. ***{among the people}* The English is ambiguous, more through defect of the English tongue, than any fault of the Translators. In the Original Hebrew, and so in other tongues, it is a plural, distinct and different from the singular, *τοῖς λαοῖς, in populis,* among several people, or nations, from whom, through whom, and to whom you did bring your people.

**V. 15. ***{thine arm redeemed thy people}* Exod. 6.6.

**V. 16. ***{The waters saw thee, O God}* When God brought his people through the Red Sea, the waters fled away like a man frighted, Psal. 114.3, 5.

**V. 17. ***{The clouds poured out water}* Heb. *The clouds were poured forth with water.* It rained, thundered, and lightened, when God delivered his people, Exod. 14.24. Psal. 68.7, 8, 9. 1 Cor. 10.1, 2.

*{thine arrows also went abroad}* Psal. 68.33.

**V. 18. ***{The voice of thy thunder was in the heaven}* Psal. 18.13, 14.

**V. 19. ***{Thy path in the great waters}* That is, in deep waters.

*{thy footsteps are not known}* Or, as by some expressed, *were not known.* For when you hadst brought over your people, the water returned to her course: and the enemies that thought to have followed them, could not pass through, Exod. 14.28, 29. Hence it appears that this parting of the sea, was not from any natural cause, or accident, but wrought by God of purpose, that his people might pass through the sea. Again, *thy foot-steps are not known;* in a more general sense, (as the words are understood and applied by many ancients) of the ways of God in general, both towards his Church, and in his general administration of the world, and of every man's salvation; *thy ways are incomprehensible:* many times contrary to man's judgment and expectation. *How unsearchable are his judgments, and his ways past finding out. For who hath known the mind of the Lord, or who hath been his Counsellour?* Rom. 11.33, 34. Of God's works in general, Job 26.14. *Lo, these are parts of his ways: but how little a portion is heard of him? but the thunder of his power who can understand?* And so, *Thy way is in the sea,* &c. should be by way of similitude; according to Prov. 30.18, 19. *There be three things which are too wonderful for me,* &c. *The way of an eagle in the air, — the way of a ship in the midst of the sea,* &c. Again, *thy way is in the sea,* &c. may be understood of God's wonders in the sea; according to Psal. 107.23, 24, &c. *They that go down into the sea in ships, that do business in great waters; These see the works of the Lord, and his wonders in the deep,* &c.

# PSALM LXXVIII

## TITLE

*{Maschil of Asaph}* Or, *A Psalm, for Asaph to give instruction*.

**Verse 1. ***{O My people}* The Prophet, under the name of a Teacher, calls the people his, and the doctrine his, as Paul calls the Gospel his, whereof he was, but the preacher, Rom. 2.16 and 16.25.

*{to my law}* That is, my doctrine.

**V. 2. ***{I will open my mouth in a parable}* Psal. 49.4. See more there. These words of the Psalmist, with some alteration, are cited by the Evangelist, upon occasion of Christ his speaking, to the people in parables: not with reference to the meaning of the Psalmist in these words; (for so the application might seem violent, and impertinent) but to the words themselves, and to that sense which of themselves they are very capable of; yea and more proper to them, according to their more ordinary use and notion. See upon Psal. 49.4. The words indeed might seem to import more, *That it might be fulfilled which was spoken by the Prophet, saying, I will open my mouth in parables,* &c. Mat. 13.35. but it is an ordinary formula, used by the Evangelists as well in allusive and indirect, as in more direct and proper applications; as Expositors will tell you there. Hence it is, that whereas the Psalmist says here, *I will utter dark sayings of old:* the Evangelist, the better to fit the words to his purpose, makes it, *I will utter things which have been kept secret from the foundation of the world.* This allusion to have proceeded immediately from the Evangelist himself, appears by the Septuagint here, whose Translation is, φθέγξομαι προβλήματα ἀπ᾽ ἀρχῆς: not παραβολάς, as in the Evangelist.

*{I will utter}* Plentifully, as a spring sends out water.

*{dark sayings}* Sharp ones, (according to the propriety of the Original word) that require wit to understand and expound them.

*{of old}* Of things that have been done long since.

**V. 3. ***{heard and known}* Psal. 44.1.

**V. 5. ***{a testimony in Jacob}* Psal. 147.19, 20. By the testimony and law, he means either the whole written word or law, which they were commanded to teach their children, Deut. 6.1, 2, 3, &c. Or, more particularly, the things afterwards rehearsed in this psalm; for the perpetual remembrance whereof unto all posterity, they had a particular charge, Deut. 4.9, 10. *Only take heed to thyself,* &c. which seems most probable.

*{our fathers have told us}* Which were the people of God, Psal. 44.1.

*{that they should make them known to their children}* Deut. 4.9 and 6.7.

**V. 6. ***{That the generation to come might know them}* There is not any thing more binding either in point of religion or of civil laws, and customs, than traditions received from fathers, and fore-fathers. Therefore such care is taken here and elsewhere, for due information of posterity.

**V. 7. ***{That they might set their hope in God}* Those many miracles that God had wrought in former times, he did them not for those times only, but for after-ages, also that the memory of them being faithfully transmitted to posterity, the power of God might be known by them: who though he think it not fit to do such wonders at all times, nor all times fit for miracles, yet is he still the same God, as able and powerful as before; which is ground enough for our faith.

**V. 8. ***{And might not be as their fathers}* Though children are commonly very apt of themselves to follow their fathers examples; yet when such examples come to them with the commemoration of such and such events and judgments as have ensued upon such and such examples: and those judgments recorded by the same hand, from which they have the examples: then such examples, so recorded and transmitted, are no inducements for imitation to any one, that is not extremely desperate; but stand for warnings; and are commonly very profitable and available to that purpose.

*{that set not their heart}* Heb. *that prepared not their heart*.

*{their heart aright}* Verse 37.

**V. 9. ***{The children of Ephraim}* First, they fled before Jephthah, Judg. 12.4, 6. Secondly, before the Philistines, 1 Sam. 4.10. which seems the rather, because after mentioned in this psalm, v. 57, 61, 67. Or; before Abijah, 2 Chr. 13.17. See also, 1 Chr. 7.21, 22. *And Zabad his son,* &c. to which some of the best Interpreters think these words to refer especially: and so the Rabbins too, Kimchi; and some others.
