Others, by the *children of Ephraim,* understand the Israelites in general so called by reason of the preeminency of that Tribe; and particularly of Jeroboam, an Ephraimite, the head of that division and defection of the ten tribes, which they conceive is here intended. If so; the psalm cannot be David's. But it does not seem to me probable, that this should be here mentioned, before so many things of ancienter times, that follow after: and that which follows in the next verse, *They kept not the covenant of God,* &c. cannot be restrained to either Ephraimites, or Israelites (as divided from Judah) alone, but belongs unto the Jews in general. Some therefore very judicious, think that *the tribe of Ephraim,* partly for the supereminent valor of it above other Tribes at that time, and partly because the Ark of God, was then among the Ephraimites; that is, in Shilo, a Town of Ephraim, and carried with it, as it were the interest of the whole Tribes, (as appeared afterwards, when removed to Judah, that Tribe became the head Tribe:) for these reasons, I say, that one Tribe might be intended for the whole commonalty of the Jews in general.

*{carrying bows}* Heb. *throwing forth, or, shooting with bows*; as Jer. 4.29.

**V. 12. ***{in the field of Zoan}* A place of note in Egypt, Isa. 19.13. Ezek. 30.14.

**V. 13. ***{He divided the sea}* Exod. 14.21.

**V. 14. ***{In the daytime also he led them with a cloud, and all the night with a light of fire}* Exod. 13.21 and 14.24.

**V. 15. ***{He clave the rocks in the wilderness, and gave them drink as out of the great depths}* Exod. 17.6. Num. 20.11. Psal. 105.41. 1 Cor. 10.4.

**V. 16. ***{to run down like rivers}* So that they drank of the water all the while they were in the wilderness, for it followed them in the Desert, Deut. 9.21. Psal. 105.41. 1 Cor. 10.4.

**V. 17. ***{And they sinned yet more against him}* Their wickedness could not be overcome by God's benefits, which were great and many, Isa. 5.4.

*{provoking the most High in the wilderness}* Psal. 95.8.

**V. 18. ***{by asking meat for their lust}* To require more than is convenient, to satisfy wanton lust, and to doubt of God's power, if we have it not, is to tempt God.

**V. 19. ***{Yea, they spake against God}* Num. 11.4.

*{Can God furnish a table in the wilderness?}* Thus when we give place to sin, we are moved to doubt of God's power, unless he will always be ready to serve our lusts.

*{furnish}* Heb. *order*.

**V. 20. ***{Behold, he smote the rock, that the waters gushed out, and the streams overflowed}* Exod. 17.6. Num. 20.11.

**V. 22. ***{and trusted not in his salvation}* That is, in his fatherly providence, whereby he cares for his, and provides sufficiently for them.

**V. 23. ***{Though he had commanded the clouds}* So that they had that which was convenient, but their lust made them to covet more.

**V. 24. ***{And had rained down manna upon them to eat, and had given them of the corn of heaven}* Exod. 16.14, 31.

**V. 25. ***{Man did eat angels food}* Or, *every one did eat the bread of the mighty*. Such as angels might eat, if they used food. Excellent food. So the tongue of angels is put for an excellent tongue, 1 Cor. 13.1. Or, food that came from the habitations of Angels (Heaven) as the Chaldee Paraphrase expresses it; and before, called, *the corn of heaven,* v. 24. Or, prepared and sent to them by the ministry of Angels; whom God does use in most things that belong unto men, either good, or bad; though they be not always mentioned.

**V. 26. ***{He caused an east wind to blow}* God used the winds to teach them, that all creatures were at his command, and that no distance of place could let his working.

*{to blow}* Heb. *to go*.

**V. 27. ***{feathered fowls}* Heb. *fowl of wing*.

**V. 28. ***{in the midst}* Of the places where they used to encamp, or, in a place near the camp: for it follows, *round about their habitations;* not in the midst of them.

*{of their camp}* Heb. *his camp:* That is, the camp of Jacob, mentioned, verse 21.

**V. 30. ***{from their lust}* From the meat they desired; or from desiring more.

*{while their meat was yet in their mouths}* Num. 11.33.

**V. 31. ***{slew the fattest of them}* Though others were not spared, yet chiefly they suffered, who were most puffed up, and were chiefest among them.

*{smote down}* Heb. *made to bow*.

*{chosen men}* Or, *young men*.

**V. 32. ***{they sinned still}* Thus sin by continuance makes men insensible, so that no plagues will mend them, 2 Chron. 28.22.

**V. 33. ***{in vanity}* In fruitless journeys in the wilderness, so that they could not come to Canaan, Num. 14.29.

**V. 34. ***{When he slew them}* Such was their hypocrisy, that they sought unto God for fear of punishment, though in their heart they loved him not, Hos. 5.15.

**V. 37. ***{For their heart was not right with him}* Whatsoever comes not from the pure fountain of the heart, is hypocrisy.

**V. 38. ***{forgave their iniquity}* Because he would ever have some remnant of a Church to praise his Name on earth, he suffered not their sins to overcome his mercy.

**V. 39. ***{For he remembered that they were but flesh}* *Behold, he putteth no trust in his Saints; yea the heavens are not clean in his sight. How much more abominable,* &c. Job 15.14, 15, 16. This consideration of man's frail nature prevailed with God, Gen. 8.21. *I will not again curse the ground any more, for mans sake: for the imagination of mans heart is evil from his youth.* Or, flesh, an object of compassion; by its natural constitution subject to much misery, and of little continuance.

*{a wind that passeth away, and cometh not again}* *O spare me that I may recover strength, before I go hence, and be no more,* Psal. 39.13. See also Job 7.7. *O remember that my life is wind,* &c. Of the soul, and the immortality of it, not mentioned at all in such expressions, see upon Psal. 6.5. *For in death,* &c. and upon Psal. 37.1, 2.

**V. 40. ***{provoke him}* Or, *rebel against him*.

**V. 41. ***{turned back and tempted God}* Or, *tempted God again*.

*{limited the holy One of Israel}* By appointing what he should do; or including his power within their capacity.

**V. 42. ***{They remembered not his hand}* The forgetfulness of God's benefits, is the root of rebellion, and of all vice.

*{from the enemy}* Or, *from affliction*.

**V. 43. ***{wrought}* Heb. *set*.

**V. 44. ***{And had turned their rivers into blood}* Exod. 7.20.

**V. 45. ***{He sent divers sorts of flies among them, which devoured them}* Exod. 8.24.

*{He sent divers sorts of flies}* This word signifies, a confused mixture of flies, and venomous worms. Some take it for all sorts of serpents: some, for all wild beasts.

*{frogs, which destroyed them}* Exod. 8.6.

**V. 46. ***{He gave also their increase unto the caterpillar}* Exod. 10.13. Here appears not here all the miracles that God did in Egypt, but some which might be sufficient to convince the people of ingratitude.

**V. 47. ***{He destroyed their vines with hail, and their sycomore trees with frost}* Exod. 9.23.

*{destroyed}* Heb. *killed*.

*{frost}* Or, *great hail-stones*.

**V. 48. ***{He gave up}* Heb. *He shut up*.

*{hot thunderbolts}* Or, *lightnings*.

**V. 49. ***{He cast upon them the fierceness of his anger, wrath and indignation}* Some, because of the ten plagues of Egypt, they find but seven here mentioned, think the rest, three in number, to wit, that of the lice, which in that order Moses relates them, was the third, Exod. 8.16, &c. that of *boils and blains* the sixth, Exod. 9.8, &c. and lastly, that of the *darkness in all the land,* the ninth, Exod. 10.21, &c. might be comprehended by these many general words here heaped together. But then we shall be as much troubled to give a reason why the rest, (the greater number, seven of ten) being particularly mentioned and expressed, these three should be thus put together, and that in such general terms, as may comprehend any of the rest, or all of them, as well as these. And then secondly, if three plagues were intended, just so many; why the words exceed that number; being four at least; *fierceness of anger, wrath, indignation,* and *trouble:* which has moved the author of this conceit, contrary to what himself had before set down, of three only omitted, or not expressed particularly, here to find four; to wit, the first also, the *murrain of cattle:* of which there, indeed, some make a doubt, whether particular mention be made of it, or no? as may appear by the different translation of those words, (for there it must be, or nowhere) of the fiftieth verse, *or gave their life over to the pestilence;* in the text; but *gave their beasts to the murrain,* in the margin. Then again; supposing we could satisfy concerning the number; another question by some other may haply be raised about the order, why the Psalmist does not observe the same order that Moses does: that being the second here, which in Moses is the fourth; and that third here, which in him is second; and that fourth here, which in Moses makes the eighth: that fifth, which in Moses, the seventh: and lastly, that fifth here, which there the sixth. Our best answer therefore will be, if any stick at the omission of either three, or four, of the ten, to desire an answer from them, whether it was not free for the Psalmist to mention as many, or as few, as he thought fit himself, without any further regard than to his own fantasy, and his pen, or verse? Or if he had not mentioned any at all, whether he could have been justly charged of any omission? If that cannot be showed, as I believe it cannot; then is it against reason, to require a reason why all not expressed, as well as some. And so we shall conclude, that these words (the words of this verse) be general words, which relate to all the plagues in general, and not to any particularly. See also upon Psal. 105.28. *He sent darkness,* &c.

*{by sending evil Angels among them}* Some plainly understand Devils, whom God does use sometimes as instruments, in such cases, as by the history of Job, and many examples in the New Testament does appear. Others, good Angels, called evil Angels, whilst under God, ministers of evil, or executioners of God's just judgments. There be examples of both good and evil, used by God in these cases. But that those were *evil angels* that were used by God in that business, here mentioned, some think is intimated by Moses, in the word משחית, *the destroyer,* Exod. 12.23. But that can be no argument, since the Angel whom God used about the three days pestilence, described, 2 Sam. 24, in the same Chapter and verse is styled משחית, *the destroyer;* and yet *the Angel of the Lord:* which is not likely to be meant of Satan: who indeed had been by his tempting of David, (as we read, 1 Chron. 21.1.) the occasion of that plague; but not the instrument, under that name at least of the punishment. Rabbi M. Ben. Maimon, the most sober and learned of all that profession, in his excellent Treatise, called, *More Nevochim,* l. 2, c. 6. disputes at large, that whatsoever is operative under God, as the elements, and all natural faculties, and the like, are called *Angels* in the Scriptures: neither does he seem to acknowledge (his proper error, opposed therein by other Rabbins) any other evil angels, but figuratively. So, *fire, hail, famine and death,* Eccl. 39.28, 29, are termed *spirits, created by God for vengeance;* and in the New Testament too, the word is sometimes, if I be not mistaken, used in that notion. So that nothing hinders, but that evil angels here also, may be interpreted, dreadful plagues and judgments inflicted by God's immediate power.

**V. 50. ***{He made a way}* Heb. *He weighed a path*.

*{their life over to the pestilence}* Or, *their beasts to the murrain*, Exod. 9.3. See upon ver. 49.

**V. 51. ***{And smote all the firstborn in Egypt}* Exod. 12.29.

*{the chief of their strength}* The first-born are so called, Gen. 49.3.

*{of Ham}* That is, Egypt: for it is called Mizraim, of Mizraim that was the son of Ham.

*{like sheep}* Psal. 77.20.

**V. 53. ***{so that they feared not}* They had no occasion to fear, for God destroyed their enemies; yet they did fear sometimes out of unbelief.

*{overwhelmed their enemies}* Exod. 14.27 and 15.10.

*{overwhelmed}* Heb. *covered*.

**V. 54. ***{He brought them to the border of his Sanctuary}* Or, *holiness*; that is, to the holy land: so called in divers respects, but especially, because of his Sanctuary, the place of his residence: to which he makes all the land to be but as bounds and limits, because of the eminency of that place, the holiness whereof did as it were spread to all other parts of the land, as if the whole had been a Sanctuary, and consecrated ground. It is therefore to the honor of the whole land, as well as of the Sanctuary, that he calls it, *the holy border,* or, *border of his Sanctuary.*

*{which his right hand had purchased}* Not got by them by their own power and strength; as Psal. 44.3. *For they gat not the land in possession by their own sword — but thy right hand,* &c.

**V. 55. ***{divided them an inheritance by line}* Josh. 13.7.

**V. 57. ***{dealt unfaithfully}* Nothing more displeases God in children, than when they continue in that wickedness, which their fathers had begun.

*{deceitful}* Or, *slack, or warping*. The similitude, it seems, was commonly used by way of proverb. So Hos. 7.16. *They return, but not to the most High: they are like a deceitful bow.* As on the other side, *to trust to his bow,* Psal. 44.6. and elsewhere. And, *a bow that will not turn back,* used also proverbially, 2 Sam. 1.22. The reason of which expressions are best able to give an account of.

**V. 58. ***{For they provoked him to anger}* Deut. 32.21. By serving God otherwise than he had appointed.

**V. 60. ***{So that he forsook the tabernacle of Shiloh}* 1 Sam. 4.11. For their ingratitude he suffered the Philistines to take the Ark which was the sign of his presence from among them, and never brought it back to Shiloh again, Jer. 7.12, 14.

**V. 61. ***{delivered his strength}* See upon Psal. 63.2. *to see thy power and thy glory.*

**V. 62. ***{Was wroth with his inheritance}* His people, that were as dear to him, as our inheritance is to us.

**V. 63. ***{The fire consumed their young men}* They were suddenly destroyed, 1 Sam. 4.10.

*{their maidens were not given to marriage}* They had no marriage songs; that is, they were not married, Jer. 25.10. Revelations 18.23.

*{given}* Heb. *praised*.

**V. 64. ***{their widows made no lamentation}* They were so overwhelmed with grief, that they died themselves, and could not lament for their husbands, 1 Sam. 4.19, &c.

**V. 65. ***{Like a mighty man that shouteth by reason of wine}* Or, *after wine*: as others. Now by such similitudes the Scripture has a respect unto the nature of man, there being nothing usually, that affects the fancy more, among the vulgar, than the representation of such mighty men, and Giants, as appears by the many fables, with much applause, received among such, in all nations, concerning both the persons and actions, and habitations of Giants, and giant-like men. For the same reason the Sun also, Psal. 19.5, is represented to *a strong man* (or giant) *that runs a race.* See more upon Psal. 110.6. *He shall fill the places,* &c.

**V. 66. ***{He smote his enemies}* With diseases, 1 Sam. 5.6, was a reproach to them, 1 Sam. 6.4.

**V. 67. ***{He refused the tabernacle of Joseph}* That is, Shiloh, a town in the tribe of Ephraim, the son of Joseph: where the Ark had abided a long time, till it was taken by the Philistines: but after that, God would not have it to return thither any more, but after many migrations, 1 Sam. 6.12, &c. it was brought by David to Jerusalem, and placed in the tabernacle which he had prepared for it.

**V. 69. ***{He built his sanctuary like high places}* In some Bibles that I have seen, and used, it is printed very absurdly, *like high places,* instead of, *like high palaces;* Or, as some others, *like high castles.* Whereupon some conceive that the stability and perpetuity of the Church is here intimated. The Hebrew word רום does comprehend both. And indeed Solomon's Temple was like a Palace, for largeness and magnificence: and Herod's Temple, as it stood in Christ his time of his conversation among men, it was not only like a palace, but like a castle too, compassed about with towers and all manner of fortifications, as appears by the story of Vespasian's siege.

*{Which he hath established}* See before upon Psal. 68.16. *yea the Lord will dwell in it for ever.*

*{established}* Heb. *founded*.

**V. 70. ***{He chose David also his servant}* 1 Sam. 16.11. 2 Sam. 7.8.

**V. 71. ***{From following}* Heb. *From after*.

*{to feed Jacob his people, and Israel his inheritance}* 2 Sam. 5.2. 1 Chron. 11.2.

**V. 72. ***{and guided them by the skilfulness of his hands}* The Original word תבונות properly signifies *Prudence:* or if we will; with divers good Interpreters, precisely keep to the Hebrew, because it is a plural, *Prudentias;* that is, *prudentiae;* which plural is by some resolved into a kind of superlative, *summa prudentia:* that is, exquisite, or singular prudence. But *prudentia,* singular or plural, is the word in most other translations that I have seen. The Greek σύνεσις, expressed by the Latin *intelligentia,* may also import φρόνησις, prudence. But because Prudence does not properly belong to the hands, our Interpreters, it seems thought *Skillfulness* (which the Hebrew does also signify) more proper to this place. But I am of another mind, I must confess, and like *prudence* here much better. For if I be not mistaken, the intention of the Psalmist here, was to characterize a good King, by two excellent properties, which indeed comprehend almost all that can be said to the commendation of an absolute Prince. The first is, goodness; not counterfeit, or fictitious; but natural and sincere; εὐήθεια, in Greek, which comprehends justice, clemency, and many other virtues. But *goodness* though always commendable of itself, whatever it be; yet *goodness* without valor in a King, is but a lame commendation, and may probably make (there be examples enough to be found) an unfortunate both king and people. I conceive that *valor* is included in the word *hand;* the usual emblem of action and power. But again, because valor, without prudence and discretion, does more hurt oftentimes, than good; as valor is added to goodness, so prudence to valor: but not as a distinct virtue from it, because in very deed (as Philosophers also teach) valor, without discretion, does not deserve the name of valor; and may more properly be called rashness, madness, desperateness, or any thing, than valor. And we know that David is set out in the Scriptures, not as a good and pious Prince only, but also valiant: a mighty valiant man, and a man of war, 1 Sam. 16.18. By those two properties also does Homer, the Poet of Poets, characterize a good King: βασιλεὺς τ᾽ ἀγαθός κρατερός τ᾽ αἰχμητής: which has been accounted in after ages a complete Elogium of most exemplary Princes. However if this seem to relish of too much subtlety, which I profess I desire to avoid in such a work as this; I shall content myself with most Interpreters, by *prudence of hands,* to understand, prudent, discreet administration, and discreet carriage of all manner of businesses. For indeed, where such prudence is; valor, or at least, the effects of valor; either personal, or by Ministers and Officers well chosen, (that is, with judgment and discretion;) cannot be wanting.

# PSALM LXXIX

## TITLE

*{Of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph*.

**Verse 1. ***{The heathen are come into thine inheritance}* The people cry unto God against the barbarous tyranny of the Babylonians; who spoiled God's inheritance, polluted his Temple, destroyed his religion, and murdered his people.

*{thy holy temple have they defiled}* Psal. 74.7.

**V. 2. ***{The dead bodies of thy servant}* The Prophet shows, to what extremities God suffers sometime his Church to fall, to exercise their faith, before he set to his hand to deliver them.

*{fowls of the heaven}* Psal. 74.14.

**V. 3. ***{there was none to bury them}* Their friends and kinsfolk durst not bury them, for fear of the enemies: or, there were so many slain, that the living sufficed not to bury the dead.

**V. 4. ***{We are become a reproach to our neighbours}* Psalm 44.14.

*{a reproach to our neighbours}* Whereof some came of Abraham, but were degenerate: and others were open enemies to your religion; but they both laughed at our miseries.

**V. 5. ***{How long, Lord?}* Psal. 89.46.

*{shall thy jealousy burn like fire?}* Will you utterly consume us for our sins, before you takest us to mercy?

**V. 6. ***{Pour out thy wrath upon the heathen that have not known thee}* Of imprecations in general, see before upon Psalm 35.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c. However, there be, of the most judicious, who think this imprecation (in form) may be resolved into a modest expostulation, as if he had said, or intended to say; Lord, how can it stand with your goodness and justice, to lay so much upon us, who are your peculiar people, who both know and worship you; and to be so indulgent to those, that are mere strangers? Though those *that know thee not, nor call upon thee?* But whether we take them as an imprecation, or expostulation, certain it is, that the aggravation is not properly (as it might seem at first) in that *they know not God;* (which in some cases would be an extenuation rather, according that of our Savior's, *Jesus said unto them, If ye were blind, ye should have no sin,* &c. John 9.41. And, *That servant which knew his Lord's will,* &c. *But he that knew not,* &c. Luke 12.47, 48.) but in that which follows, (the fruit and effects of that ignorance, perchance, though the same be done sometimes by them also, that profess they know God,) in the next verse, *For they have devoured Jacob, and laid waste his dwelling place.* These two verses (6 and 7) are used by the Prophet Jeremy also, but with some little alteration and addition, as will appear, if compared, Jer. 10.25.

**V. 7. ***{For they have devoured Jacob}* Heb. אכל, *voravit,* in the singular: *He hath devoured;* that is, *unusquisque eum;* *every one of them*; as some of the Rabbins here. Or else it may be, a י is wanting, which is supplied by Jeremy, Jer. 10.25. (See upon the former verse,) where the word is twice repeated, and both times in the plural.

**V. 8. ***{O remember not against us former iniquities}* Isa. 64.9. Which we and our fathers have committed.

*{former iniquities}* Or, *the iniquities of them that were before us.*

*{let thy tender mercies speedily prevent us}* And stay not till we be overwhelmed with our troubles.

**V. 9. ***{O God of our salvation}* Seeing we have none other Savior, neither can we help ourselves, and also by our deliverance, your name shall be praised. Therefore, O Lord, help us.

*{name's sake}* Josh. 7.9.

**V. 10. ***{Where is their God?}* Psal. 42.10 and 115.2.

*{revenging}* Heb. *vengeance*.

*{revenging the blood of thy servants which is shed}* Psal. 58.10.

**V. 11. ***{Let the sighing of the prisoner come before thee}* Though in respect of God they were justly punished for their sins, yet in consideration of their cause were unjustly vexed.

*{thy power}* Heb. *your arm*.

*{preserve thou those that are appointed to die}* Heb. *reserve the children of death.*

*{those that are appointed to die}* Which were captives among their enemies and could look for nothing but death, Psal. 44.22. He presses this upon God, as a thing most beseeming his infinite both power and mercy, to help them, when there is least hope: because man's extremity is God's opportunity. It is mentioned as the proper work of God, Psal. 102.20. *To hear the groaning of the prisoner, to loose those that are appointed to death.* And it is the property of all that are good and generous among men, to imitate God therein, so far as their power extends, *If thou forbear to deliver them that are drawn unto death, and those that are ready to be slain, if thou sayest,* &c. Prov. 24.11, 12.

**V. 12. ***{And render unto our neighbours seven fold}* To wit, to our unmerciful neighbors, who in our calamity, when we expected pity and compassion, did cruelly insult over us, and upbraided us as other heathens did, of our vain confidence in our God; and made our temporal sufferings an argument of our false worship. Of this, and of desire of revenge in general, see, as before, Psalm 35.4. *Let them be confounded,* &c.

*{Into their bosom}* Isa. 65.6, 7.

**V. 13. ***{sheep of thy pasture}* Psal. 95.7 and 100.3.

*{we will shew forth thy praise to all generations}* Heb. *we will number.* That is, record the particular benefits for which you are to be praised. We ought to desire no benefit of God, but on this condition, To praise his Name, Isa. 43.21.

*{to all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation.*

# PSALM LXXX

## TITLE

*{Shoshannim}* See Notes on Psal. 60, tit.

*{Eduth}* A contestation: wherein the Psalmist earnestly entreats God for freedom out of those miseries, which by his desertion had befallen him.

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph.*

**Verse 1. ***{Thou that leadest Joseph like a flock}* By Joseph, most understand the Tribe of Ephraim, the son of Joseph, which was the head of the ten Tribes, separated from Judah: so that the calamities of the ten Tribes apart by the Chaldeans, are conceived to be the subject of this Psalm. So Amos 6.1, 6, Judah is reproved, because not sensible of the *afflictions of Joseph,* ver. 6. that is, of the ten Tribes, carried into captivity about 150 years before the taking of Jerusalem by the same Chaldeans. Others, because mention is made of the Ark, (*between the Cherubims*) where God was said to dwell (of which see Exod. 25.22. *And there I will meet thee,* &c.) and that the ten Tribes were departed from the worship of the true God that was said to dwell there, and worshipped Idols, and by consequence had no interest either in the Ark, or in the God of the Ark; they therefore conceive that the ten Tribes cannot here be meant particularly; but by Joseph, they understand all the Tribes in general, as Psalm next, verse 5. *This he ordained in Joseph for a testimony,* &c. But the objection is in very deed of little consequence. For if the Psalmist may be allowed to address himself to the true God, for them that had forsaken him; why not as well to the God dwelling in the Ark, or among the Cherubims of the Ark; being an ordinary *periphrasis,* or expression of the true God in the Scripture? besides, as is well answered by some, though the generality of the ten Tribes had forsaken God; yet many there were of that division, who not only had forsaken them locally, 2 Chr. 11.16, 17. but also remaining among them, worshipped the true God in their hearts; nor did conform to the outward idolatrous worship of the rest, 1 Kings 19.18. *Yet I have left me seven thousand in Israel,* &c. A greater objection may be, as I conceive, though no notice be taken of it, from the next words, as we shall see there.

**V. 2. ***{Before Ephraim, and Benjamin, and Manasseh, stir up thy strength}* Why these Tribes should be joined, and why these only mentioned is a question; and whether all the Tribes in general, (of which see what has been already said upon the first verse) and Psal. 68.27. *the Princes of Judah*) or the ten only, be intended. They that would have all the Tribes included, say, that it is usual in the Scripture, by naming of some, to understand all; that the Tribe of Ephraim and Manasseh, before the Kingdoms were settled in the Tribe of Judah, were the chiefest Tribes, being Joseph's posterity: that these three Tribes, when all the Israelites, divided into four equal parts, encamped about the Ark, made one part, Numb. 2.17, 18, &c. and with reference to that ancient relation, though long after, might probably be named together. And besides, that Benjamin had a particular relation to the Tribe of Ephraim and Manasseh, because Benjamin and Joseph were not only brethren, as all the rest of the Patriarchs, all sons of Jacob: but also ὁμομήτριοι, as St. Basil here calls them, sons of one mother. This, upon a supposition that the twelve Tribes in general are the subject of these complaints. But that by some others, men of great judgment and learning, will not be granted: that all should be intended, and yet neither Judah, the very chief (in those days) of all the Tribes; nor the Temple, or City, far more considerable to the Jews in all their joys or sorrows, than life, or any thing else, should be mentioned. But they that object so strongly, and in my judgment plausibly enough, against the former, take no notice at all, (which I much wonder at) of one strong objection that may be made against their opinion. For if the ten Tribes only, or the Israelites, as then called, were here concerned; how comes Benjamin to be named; which was a distinct Tribe from the Israelites, and adhered unto Judah, when that unhappy division, by the revolt of ten Tribes, happened? In answer to this, all that I can think of (since I find nothing by others either objected, or answered about it,) that has probability, is this; That haply the words both of this and the former verse, were some ancient form of prayer used of old, when they were all one body, either upon the marching of the Ark, or some occasion of imminent danger, but an ancient public formula, whatever the occasion were; and as an ancient form, (which in prayer is ordinary, wherein many have been very scrupulous to alter any thing, either words, or matter, though sometimes for the best;) here used and retained, though alteration of times have now much altered the case. So Psal. 68.1. *Arise, O God,* &c. was an ancient form, as is noted there: and so again, Psal. 132.8, 9, 10. *Arise, O Lord,* &c. *Let thy Priests,* &c. *For thy servant,* &c. as appears by 2 Chr. 6.41, 42. As for the conjunction of these three Tribes, in general, this also, besides their encamping together, is observed, that they, or the remnants of them, dwelled together in Jerusalem with Judah, after the captivity; as is recorded 1 Chron. 9.3.

**V. 3. ***{Turn us}* Or, *Return us*. To wit, out of captivity.

**V. 4. ***{How long wilt thou be angry against the prayer of thy people?}* The faithful fear God's anger, when they perceive that their prayers are not forthwith heard.

*{wilt thou be angry}* Heb. *wilt thou smoke.*

**V. 5. ***{Thou feedest them with the bread of tears}* 1 Kings 22.27. Psal. 102.9. Isa. 30.20. See upon Psal. 42.3. *My tears.*

**V. 6. ***{Thou makest us a strife unto our neighbours}* Our neighbors have a continual strife and war against us.

**V. 7. ***{Turn us again}* See verse 3.

**V. 8. ***{Thou hast brought a vine out of Egypt}* See Isa. 5.1, &c. *Now will I sing to my well beloved,* &c. *For the vineyard of the Lord of hosts is the house of Israel,* &c. And Jer. 2.21. *Yet I had planted thee a noble vine,* &c. Seeing that of your mercy you have made us a most dear possession to you, and we through our sins are laid open for wild beasts to devour us, declare again your love, and finish your work that you have begun, Psal. 44.2.

*{cast out the heathen}* Psal. 44.2.

**V. 10. ***{The goodly cedars}* Heb. *the cedars of God.*

**V. 11. ***{and her branches unto the river}* To wit, Euphrates, as Psal. 72.8. *He shall have dominion,* &c.

**V. 12. ***{broken down her hedges}* Psal. 89.40. Isa. 5.5.

**V. 13. ***{The boar}* Or, That is, *the boar*.

*{The boar out of the wood doth waste it}* That is, as well they that hate our religion, as they that hate our persons. Or more probably, if any such interpretation be allowed, that is, that in this allegory of wild beasts, the *boar out of the wood,* and *the wild beasts of the field,* must be interpreted of different parties; which is not necessary: but if any such; more probable, that by the *wild boar,* as some interpret here, the tyrants themselves or Great ones, that were chief actors in this sad tragedy, and set others on the work: and by the *wild beast of the field;* (as some Rabbins conceive, that the word חית properly imports,) the *reptilia,* that is, crawling beasts or vermin, the common soldiers, and other inferior people, should be understood. But, as was intimated before, it may very well be, and it is the more received opinion, that by both those particulars in the allegory, one and the same thing is literally intended; to wit, very barbarous and cruel enemies.

**V. 14. ***{look down from heaven}* They gave not place to temptation, knowing, That albeit there were no hope on earth, yet God was able to succor them from heaven, Isa. 63.15.

**V. 15. ***{And the vineyard which thy right hand hath planted}* There be two words in this verse, כנה, (here translated *vineyard*) and בן (here, *branch*) about which there is great controversy. As for the first, it is nowhere extant but in this place; the Rabbins can tell us nothing of it, of certainty, and all that either by them, or any other, is said of it, is either grounded upon the etymology; as it is conceived to be a derivative of כן, which signifies *aptare, firmare, disponere,* &c. or upon the context here, being thus joined with the verb נטע, which signifies *to plant.* Upon the first ground, some translate it, *a base, or prop, or stay:* such as are used about vines, to bear the branches, and sometimes the body: and upon the second others, *a vine;* as here: or, *a plant.* But there be, who think that כ is here instead of ג: and thereupon render *hortum,* that is, *a garden.* Some think it to be an Egyptian word: but I see little ground for it, except all words, but once to be found in the Scripture, must be concluded to be strange words. However, if Egyptian, yet so it would, as they interpret it, signify *a plant;* so that all this variety comes to one effect, and makes no considerable difference of sense. As for the other word בן, I know not why there should be any difference about it. It is true, the ordinary signification of it is, *a son:* but it is as undoubtedly true, that it signifies sometimes a *bough,* or *branch,* as Gen. 49.22. twice in one verse; *Joseph is a fruitful bough,* &c. besides בנות the feminine of it, properly *daughters,* there also translated *branches:* which signification being very proper and natural here, why any should rather choose the former; because more ordinary, and thereupon tell us, that Christ is meant; or, as some others, that Solomon; I see no cause.

*{that thou madest strong for thyself}* So that without your leave, no power can prevail against it; and which as a young bud, you raisest up again, as out of the burnt ashes.

**V. 16. ***{they perish at the rebuke of thy countenance}* When you in your anger deliverest it over to the sword of the enemies, Psal. 39.11.

**V. 17. ***{Let thy hand be upon the man of thy right hand}* By the *man of thy right hand* most understand, the man of your love, as Benjamin (properly, *a son of the right hand*) was to Jacob. And by *hand,* understanding power and protection, is as usual. See upon Psal. 74.16. *thou hast prepared:* they make this to be the meaning; Protect and defend *the man of thy right hand,* that is, say some, that vine, that branch, which has been so dear unto you; upon which you have bestowed so much cost; taking the word *man,* not, as properly, personal, but as they call it, real, referring to the particular thing, spoken of before, whether man, or whatever else it be. To this purpose they bring, *the man of hunger,* for a hungry: and *a man of chidings,* for the object of chidings; *a man of war,* for a warrior, and the like. Wherein nevertheless there is a manifest difference: because *man* in these phrases, though not principally intended to signify a person; yet it refers to a particular man, not any thing else; and therefore may be called personal: whereas here it is (as they expound) otherwise; referring immediately to a vine, and in that vine, metaphorically, to a whole Nation. Others therefore by *the man,* understand the King of the Israelites, whoever he was that had right to that kingdom, in those calamitous times. But they are put to it, to give a reason, why the Psalmist should pray, not only for an idolatrous wicked King, but for an usurper, whose predecessors had impiously revolted from their lawful King, the King of Judah. So that after they have said as much as they can to that purpose, they come about again, and would rather have the whole Church understood. And by the *son of man,* the more ordinary sort of people, that none might be excluded from the benefit of these prayers. Others finding but little probability in this exposition, would have the Messiah intended, either in both places; or at least, Christ in the first, *the man of God's right hand;* and the Church in the last, by *the son of man.* The Chaldee Paraphrast refers it either to David personally; which is not so likely; or to some, whether Messiah or other, that had interest in the promises, made by God unto David. For he translates, as Geberard expresses him, *Sit manus tua super virum, cui jurasti in manu dextera tua;* that is, Let your hand be upon the man to whom you have sworn by your right hand. Wherein I make no question, but he had a respect to Psal. 89.35. *Once have I sworne by my holinesse, that I will not lye unto David,* and Psal. 132.11. *That hath sworne in truth unto David,* &c. I am so far of his mind, though in this exposition of *the man of thy right hand,* I will bind no man to follow him, since the first may stand as probably: yet so far of his mind, as peremptorily to conclude, that if the Psalmist his intention was that way, as to recommend any unto God by these words, the surest way of exposition must be grounded upon these words concerning David in the same Psalm, *I have laid help upon one that is mighty,* &c. *I have found David my servant,* &c. *With whom my hand shall be established; mine arme also shall strengthen him:* verse 19, 20, 21. So that both *the man of thy right hand,* and *the son of man,* be understood of some one, nor divers, but the same, whether man or people, that were conceived to have interest in these expressions and promises, uttered of and to David at first, personally. And since David was so much concerned (as all men are in theirs) in his posterity, and that God's promises to him were most concerning his issue, and the stability and perpetuity of his people and kingdom after him, the Psalmist might very well, in desiring God, in that sense, to strengthen David, (as surviving in his posterity, though deceased himself personally, many ages before) intend any, whether king or people, that had relation unto David, either of people or progeny: and so David the immediate object of the words, and yet in him, either the ten Tribes, or all the twelve in general. Had it been for these words, which gave me so much light, to which I was happily guided by the Chaldee Paraphrase, I must confess I have found so little satisfaction otherwise from any Expositors, that I should have been bold to have ventured upon another exposition, quite different from any of these; but not less literal, or proper to the words, as I conceive. Wicked men, we know, whom God does employ to execute his just judgments, are called *the sword, and the hand of God;* as Psal. 17.13, 14. *From wicked men, which are thy sword: from men of the world, O Lord, from men of thy hand,* &c. And how God does use to strengthen such, Isaiah declares at large, *Therefore their habitations were of small power: they were dismayed and confounded,* &c. 37.27. And the Prophet Habakkuk says, that God has *ordained and established* (or strengthened, as the word imports) such, *for correction,* 1.12. And as for God's *right hand,* though commonly mentioned against the wicked in the defense of his people; yet is that also sometimes turned (and so mentioned) against God's own people too; as Lam. 2.3, 4. *He hath drawn back his right hand from before the enemie,* &c. *He hath bent his bow, he stood with his right hand as an adversary,* &c. or that instead of a prayer, it might be made an imprecation of these words. But I rather adhere to the former interpretation, as more agreeable to the sense of other Expositors: but especially to the 15 verse, where some of these words are used, and would not probably (though sometimes by a figure called *antanaclasis,* it be so:) be repeated here so soon but to the same purpose. Some further confirmation we have also from Isa. his words: *Fear thou not, for I am with thee:* (they are directed to the Church in general, not to any particular) *be not dismayed, for I am thy God; I will strengthen thee; yea, I will help thee; yea, I will uphold thee with the right hand of my righteousness,* 41.10. I must not omit that some have thought Zerubbabel the particular man (if any particular) intended by these words: which if we could grant the twelve Tribes in general, the subject of these complaints (which we cannot well, as said before) we should not altogether dislike. There be some things in the Prophets, as Haggai, and Zechariah, spoken of him, that would fit very well to this purpose. But I have said enough.

**V. 19. ***{Turn us again, O Lord God of Hosts: cause thy face to shine upon us, &c.}* See verse 3. We had this verse before; but with some alteration verse 3 and 7. so that although it be the same in substance, and so repeated in this Psalm three times in all; yet there is diversity to be observed in every verse; as thus: ver. 3. *O God:* ver. 7. *O God of Hosts.* And here, (last verse) *O Lord God of Hosts.* Of which observation I would not make any great mystery, but that this addition of words makes the speech somewhat more rhetorical and pathetical. *O God: O God of Hosts: O Lord God of Hosts.* But besides this, there is other use to be made of such observations; of which see upon Psal. 34. last verse, *that trust in his strength.* And for those words, *cause thy face to shine:* see upon Psal. 67.1. *cause his face to shine.*

# PSALM LXXXI

## TITLE

*{Gittith}* See the Notes on the title of Psal. 8.

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph.*

**Verse 1. ***{Sing aloud unto God}* It seems that this Psalm was appointed for solemn feasts and assemblies of the people, to whom these ceremonies at such times were usual.

**V. 3. ***{in the new moon}* This they did also in other feasts, Numb. 10.10.

**V. 4. ***{For this was a statute for Israel}* Levit. 23.24.

**V. 5. ***{This he ordained in Joseph for a testimony}* Joseph is put for all Israel, because he had the double portion, which was one part of the birth-right, 1 Chron. 5.1, 2. So is Judah sometimes, because he had the government; which was the other part, Gen. 49.10.

*{when}* Or, *because.*

*{when he went out through the land of Egypt}* Or, *when he went out of the land of Egypt:* or *from the land of Egypt;* as the Septuagint renders it: taking על for מעל, which is not without example. The Law was given we know concerning the Passover, at their going out of Egypt properly; and the laws concerning other feasts in the wilderness, in their way from Egypt to the Land of Canaan. Some retain the propriety of the word על and refer the words to God, *As he went out* (as elsewhere he is said, *to go out before his people*) *against the land of Egypt.* So Junius, and some others. But I conceive the former more probable.

*{where I heard a language that I understood not}* A people of a strange speech, and a hard language, whose words you can not understand, according to Ezekiel's expression, 3.6. one of the least grievances of travelers in foreign countries: mentioned also by the Prophet Jeremy, as a judgment: *Lo I will bring a nation upon you from far,—a nation whose language thou knowest not, neither understandest what they say,* Jer. 5.15. Were it not for those places, I should think the better of their interpretation, who make this to be the meaning: I heard a strange language; a language (or a lip, according to the propriety of the Hebrew word:) that had not been heard before among men: to wit, the language, or the voice of God. The mention of this so great and so unusual favor, and honor done to the Israelites, would have been very proper in this place, where divers others, very great, but not so extraordinary and miraculous, are mentioned. Moses where he rehearses divers particulars of that nature, wherein God had magnified himself, and manifested his love towards the Israelites, speaks of it, as of the highest, and gives it the first place, with answerable sublimity of words. *For aske now of the days that are past,* &c. *Did ever people hear the voice of God, speaking out of the midst of the fire, as thou hast heard, and live?* Deut. 4.32, 33. and again in the 36 verse, *Out of heaven he made them to hear his voice,* &c.

**V. 6. ***{shoulder from the burden}* Isa. 9.4 and 10.27.

*{were delivered}* Heb. *passed away.*

*{from the pots}* Psal. 68.13. *Though ye have lyen among the pots,* &c. If they were never able to give sufficient thanks to God for this deliverance from their corporal bondage, how much more are we indebted to him for our spiritual deliverance from the tyranny of Satan and sin!

**V. 7. ***{calledst in trouble, and I delivered thee}* Psal. 50.15.

*{in the secret place of thunder}* By a strange and wonderful manner, out of the clouds whence thunder came, Exod. 14.19, 20. and 19.16. Or, as some others, *in the secret place of thunder*, that is, out of the black cloud, from whence God speaking, a rattling noise was heard, as it were of a thunder: So John 12.28, 29. when a voice came from heaven to Christ, *the people that stood by and heard it, said it thundered.* Other interpretations there be of these words, but not any that can satisfy, besides these.

*{proved thee at the waters of Meribah}* Exod. 17.5. He opposes their extreme ingratitude and infidelity, to God's great goodness towards them, which sets it out the more; according to that of the Apostle, *But God commendeth his love towards us,* &c. Rom. 5.8. Some translate *usque ad aquas:* that is, *unto, (or, until) the waters of Meribah*: as if he said; after I had proved you, and borne with many provocations, even to the waters of Meribah, and by that time knew you sufficiently, yet from those waters, (Exod. 17. &c.) you did advance to mount Horeb; where, passing by all former provocations, for which you did deserve to have been rejected, I gave you the Law; immediately mentioned in the following verses.

*{Meribah}* Or, *strife.*

**V. 8. ***{Hear, O my people}* He condemns all people that are not attentive to hear God's voice, and to give obedience to the same.

**V. 9. ***{There shall no strange God}* Or, *let no strange god.*

*{neither shalt thou worship}* Or, *neither worship.*

**V. 10. ***{open thy mouth wide, and I will fill it}* Enlarge your desire never so much, and it shall be satisfied, Psal. 37.4. Eph. 3.20.

**V. 12. ***{So I gave them up to their own hearts lust}* Acts 14.16.

*{unto their own hearts lust}* Or, *to the hardness of their hearts, or, imaginations.*

**V. 13. ***{O that my people had hearkened unto me}* God shows, that the obedience of his people is as pleasing to him, as things wished for to men, Deut. 5.29. Isa. 48.18. 1 John 3. See before upon the title of Psalm 72. of God's conditional promises, out of Ezek. 33.13, &c.

**V. 14. ***{and turned my hand against their adversaries}* If they had obeyed me, ver. 12.

**V. 15. ***{haters of the Lord should have submitted themselves unto him}* Psal. 37.20. Rom. 1.30.

*{submitted themselves}* Or, *yielded feigned obedience, Heb*. *lied.* Psal. 18.44. See more there.

*{but their time should have endured for ever}* If the Israelites had not broken covenant with God, he would have given them perpetual felicity. So Matt. 23.37. *O Jerusalem, Jerusalem, thou that killest,* &c. *how often would I have gathered,* &c. *and ye would not?*

**V. 16. ***{with the finest of the wheat}* Heb. *with the fat of wheat.* That is, with most fine wheat, and abundance of honey.

# PSALM LXXXII

## TITLE

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph.*

**Verse 1. ***{God standeth in the congregation of the mighty}* The Prophet shows, that God, who is present among Judges, observes whether they do their duty or no, that he may reward or punish them.

*{mighty}* Or, *God;* that is, his own Congregation: The noun is repeated instead of the pronoun, as Numb. 10.29. Luke 3.19. and elsewhere. Or, *the Congregation of God;* that is, of lawful Magistrates, which God owns as his ordinance, &c. Rom. 13.1, 2.

*{he judgeth}* Or, *he should judge.*

*{he judgeth among the gods}* That is, Judges, or Magistrates: as Exod. 22.8, 9, &c. The reason of it, see John 10.34, 35. *Is it not written in your Law,* &c. They ought to judge according to his law, that the sentence might be God's, and not theirs: but they did not so, ver. 2, &c. 2 Chron. 19.6.

*{gods}* Exod. 22.9.

**V. 2. ***{How long will ye judge unjustly}* For thieves and murderers find favor in judgment, when the cause of the poor and godly cannot be heard.

*{accept the persons of the wicked}* Deut. 1.17.

**V. 3. ***{Defend}* Heb. *Judge*, Psal. 43.1.

*{do justice to the}* Heb. *justify the.*

*{and needy}* Not only when he cries for help, but whensoever you know that his cause requires aid and support.

**V. 4. ***{Deliver the poor and needy}* Prov. 24.11.

**V. 5. ***{All the foundations of the earth are out of course}* Some, by *foundations,* &c. understand the corrupt Judges or Princes before spoken of; which is not so likely, because having said so much of them before, this expression, by those words, if meant of them, would rather fall, than rise. Others would have the Laws, and administration of Justice, upon which Common-Weals are chiefly founded, to be intended. But that these are elsewhere so called, we do not find: and besides, he would so but say the same thing as before. For if the Judges be corrupt and wicked, it will of necessity follow, that judgments be perverted. I therefore take the words as proverbially used, to imply an universal confusion of the state, arising especially from their impiety, and perfidious dealing, that should have been the chief pillars of it. The Grecians also used to say, τὰ ἄνω κάτω, to the same purpose: whence was his jest, shall I say, or prophecy, (for I find it recorded as a prophecy; and it fell out indeed, as he expected,) who when he died, would be buried upon his belly, because he expected that all things should shortly be turned upside down, and then he should lie right.

*{earth}* Or, *land.*

*{out of course}* Heb. *moved.*

**V. 6. ***{I have said, Ye are gods}* John 10.34.

**V. 7. ***{But ye shall die like men, and fall like one of the Princes}* This translation is old enough, it is confessed; and that is all that can be said for it, or that could move any man to embrace it. For otherwise, the very context, and reason of sense would move a man to suspect that it is not right, and that somewhat else was intended. For what a thing were this, to tell Judges or Magistrates, that although they are styled Gods, yet they shall die like Princes, or like one of the Princes; who also (all Princes) are Magistrates, and somewhat more than ordinary Magistrates. According to the Hebrew, word for word, it is: *and as one Princes ye shall fall:* Which some to make a little plainer, translate, *Et sicut quilibet;* or, *velut unus, (e quibus, scil.) principes, cadetis;* that is, *and ye Princes, shall dye as one;* or, *any one:* that is, as any ordinary man, even the meanest, who among the rest of men make but a number, as it were; neither are otherwise taken notice of. It is an elegant and emphatical expression. Neither did we appeal to common sense without cause, since even in the writings of ancient heathens, we find divers expressions to that purpose. *Ut enim caeterorum hominum,* (there is our אחד here; or, *one,*) *ita principum, illorum etiam qui sibi Dii videntur, aevum omne & breve, & fragile est;* So one of them speaking before, and to a great Emperor, I know not, whether all Christian Princes would be well pleased to be told so much; or they who by their place should tell them of it, use to discharge their duties so faithfully. These Heathens may shame them one day, if they do not. But who would expect such a lesson from an ordinary Comic? yet his words (his words I say: I go no further;) savor of so much piety, if well applied; and are so consonant to the Scripture, and so much to our purpose, that I cannot omit them, though from such a one: *Unus tibi hic dum proprius sit Jupiter, Tu istos minutos Deos flocci-feceris;* says one there, calling himself Jupiter, because he was master of the house, in opposition to the rest of the same house, under his government. The other answers, *Nugae sunt istae magnae: quasi tu nescias Repente ut emoriantur humani Joves.*

**V. 8. ***{for thou shalt inherit all Nations}* All the earth is your, by as true a right as inheritance; therefore let not evil Judges oppress your people.

# PSALM LXXXIII

## TITLE

*{A song or Psalm}* Psal. 48, tit. This Psalm seems to be composed as a form of prayer, when the Church was in great danger by reason of her enemies perhaps in Jehoshaphat's time, 2 Chron. 20.1.

*{of Asaph}* Or, *for Asaph.*

**Verse 1. ***{Keep not thou silence, O God}* Psalm 28.1 and 35.22.

**V. 2. ***{thine enemies make a tumult}* He calls them God's enemies, which are enemies to his Church.

*{hate thee}* Psal. 81.15.

**V. 3. ***{against thy hidden ones}* God's Saints are his secret ones; for he hides them in the secret of his Tabernacle, and preserves them from all dangers, Psal. 31.20.

**V. 4. ***{let us cut them off from being a Nation}* They were not content to take the Church as prisoner, but sought utterly to destroy it, Jer. 11.19.

**V. 5. ***{consent}* Heb. *heart.*

*{against thee}* Acts 9.4. They thought to have subverted your counsel, wherein the perpetuity of the Church was established, Psal. 2.1.

**V. 6. ***{The Tabernacles of Edom}* Alluding to the manner of them that dwell in tents, 2 Sam. 20.1.

**V. 7. ***{Gebal}* The Inhabitants of Gebal, (or Gebana, as Pliny, and some other ancients call it, a city of Phoenicia, near to Sidon; mentioned Ezek. 27.9. where the pride and greatness of Tyre is set out: *The ancients of Gebal, and the wise men thereof were in thee thy calkers:* and the inhabitants of it, from thence denominate Giblites, or Giblikes mentioned 1 Kings 5.18. *And Solomon's builders, and Hiram's builders did hew them, and the Giblites;* as in the margin there, according to best translations: not as it is in the Text, *and the stone-squarers* which is expressed out of the Chaldee Paraphrase. But that it is a proper name, appears besides by Josh. 13.5. *And the land of the Giblites,* &c. All other Names here occurring, verse 6, 7, 8. are known names, often mentioned in the Scriptures.

**V. 8. ***{they have holpen the children of Lot}* That is, the Moabites and Ammonites; who it seems were chiefest in this confederacy. Whence some of the chiefest Expositors infer very probably, that this Psalm was composed in Jehoshaphat, King of Judah, his time: in whose reign, as we read, 2 Chron. 20. the children of Moab and Ammon, with divers others, in great number, invaded the land. The story is there at large. It stands with much probability; though some, (upon what good grounds I know not) be of a different opinion.

*{they have holpen the children of Lot}* Heb. *They have been an arm to the children of Lot.*

**V. 9. ***{as unto the Midianites}* By these examples they were confirmed that God would not suffer his people to be utterly destroyed.

*{Midianites}* Judg. 7.22.

*{Sisera}* Judg. 4.15, 24.

**V. 10. ***{as dung for the earth}* Trodden under feet as mire, 2 Kings 9.37. Zeph. 1.17.

**V. 11. ***{their nobles}* Or, *them, and or even their nobles.*

*{Oreb}* Judg. 7.25.

*{Zebah}* Judg. 8.21.

**V. 12. ***{Let us take to our selves the houses of God in possession}* It appears their spleen was against God: they aim at his houses, both his synagogues, and the houses of his people among whom he dwelt.

**V. 13. ***{O my God, make them like a wheel}* The Hebrew word גלגל does sometimes signify a *wheel,* in the proper signification; as also, a *globe,* or *sphere.* Most interpreters are for that signification here, and think it must be understood of a wheel, globe, or rolling thing, in a steep place. But that needs not. For the bare turning of a wheel, as it is used in a cart, or chariot, may very aptly express the miserable condition of a restless spirit: and the Grecians to that very purpose use their ῥόμβος, proverbially, which signifies properly a top, such as boys by scourging make turn round. But comparing with Isa. 17.13. where the sense of this verse is more fully with addition of some words, none omitted that are here expressed; it will easily appear that the word here is not intended by the Psalmist for a proper, but metaphorical wheel; such a one as the whirlwind does raise into the air out of the dust of the ground. His words are; *They shall flee afar off, and shall be chased as the chaff of the mountains before the wind, and like a rolling thing* (so is the Hebrew גלגל there translated: but by some others, better, *velut pulvis rotatus;* that is, *as dust whirled about,*) *before the whirlwind.* The same word is used by the same Prophet in another place, Isa. 5.28. used there in the proper notion of a wheel; but with relation to the metaphorical: *their horses hoofs,* says he, *shall be counted like flint, and their wheels like a whirlwind.*

*{as the stubble before the wind}* Utterly disperse them, as chaff is blown away with the wind.

**V. 14. ***{as the flame setteth the mountains on fire}* Perhaps he means the trees on the mountains, which sometimes are fired with lightnings. See Notes on Psal. 29.6.

**V. 16. ***{that they may}* Or, *that men may,* as ver. 18.

*{that they may seek thy Name}* That others, seeing your judgments on them, may be converted: for he wishes the destruction of these enemies, ver. 10, 17. See more upon Psal. 59.13. *Consume them in wrath,* &c.

**V. 18. ***{that thou whose name alone is JEHOVAH}* See of it upon Psalm 68.4. *by thy Name JAH.*

# PSALM LXXXIV

## TITLE

*{Gittith}* See the title of Psal. 8.

*{A Psalm for the sons of Korah}* See the title of Psal. 48.

*{for}* Or, *of.*

**Verse 1. ***{How amiable are thy tabernacles?}* The Psalmist complains, that he cannot have access to the Church of God, to make profession of his faith, and to profit in Religion.

**V. 2. ***{longeth}* Psal. 42.1, 2. Of David his wonderful zeal and ardent longing for the House of God, and religious duties there performed, see upon Psal. 27.8. *Seek ye my face,* &c.

*{for the Courts of the Lord}* Wherein the Tabernacle was placed.

**V. 3. ***{Yea the sparrow hath found an house, &c.}* According as we resolve concerning the subject and occasion of the whole Psalm; so must we, differently, concerning the scope and meaning of these particular words. All Expositors seem to agree upon it unanimously, that David wrote it in some one of his flights and persecutions, whether by Saul, or Absalom, when he had not free access to either Ark or Tabernacle; and was deprived of the benefit of holy assemblies and solemnities: after which, how earnestly he was wont to long, we have elsewhere showed, as upon the 2 verse has already been noted. See the reference there. Upon this supposition, concerning the whole, this will be the meaning here: David in his wonted zeal bewails himself, and in a poetical strain (as fond and passionate lovers are wont to catch at any such occasion) expostulates and complains, that his condition should be worse than that of Birds, who had less to do with the house of God than he, and yet had free access unto it, who was denied him. This is the common Exposition, and may still be, for me; I find no such inconvenience in it, that should make me to oppose this general consent. Yet mine opinion is somewhat different; to wit, that not David's absence, but their distance, who lived in the remote parts of the land, and were wont, according to the Law, Exod. 23.17. thrice every year (besides what they might do of their own accord) though some deserts and wildernesses, and many other difficulties, in many companies together, to repair to the set-feasts and solemnities in Jerusalem; that these, I say, are the proper subject of this Psalm, or Song: written (as I conceive) in their behalf, to be used or sung by them upon the way, for their comfort and encouragement. That most part of the Psalm tends to that purpose, or at least, fits very well, may appear by the most approved Expositions of greatest part of it: why all should not, I see no reason at all to the contrary. Now upon this supposition, I should say, that these birds are mentioned, as examples of studious and sedulous creatures, which to compass their ends, that is, to provide themselves of a convenient place, both of rest and safety to themselves and their young ones, spare no labor, but seek far and near, till they have sped, whether in private or public houses, as particularly, the Temple itself; that the consideration of these, might serve for a poetical (for that must still be taken into consideration) encouragement; unto these weary Travelers, to put them in mind, that since all their welfare, both spiritual and corporal, and all their security, depended on their relation to that house, and their interest in, and to, those solemnities, they should not think much of their pains. If any objection can be against this Exposition, it must be from the words, ver. 9, 10. where David seems to pray for himself: of which more shall be said there. But a greater objection against the common opinion we shall find in the 7 verse. However, I do not peremptorily determine: I do but propose, as a thing that may be further considered of. There be other doubts concerning this verse, which we are now to take into consideration.

*{where she may lay her young, even thine altars, O Lord}* No man can well imagine, how birds should nest in the very altars, if either the structure, or the daily use of them, and frequency of people about them be considered. But *thine altars* some interpret, *near thine altars,* and suppose there might be trees near. It is not a very likely supposition; nor much to the purpose; if it were so. Except those trees were so near, (which we know was not) that they should be accounted as part of the altars themselves; which would be precisely understood here, according to the expression, to preserve the emphasis of the words. Others therefore by *altars* here, understand the Temple itself: which would do very well, if it could be confirmed by any pertinent examples, where the word *altar* is so used. For though in point of worship, *altar* and *Temple* might be used promiscuously, yet in a thing that is proper to the Temple, that *altar* should be put for *temple,* is much more unlikely. Besides, if the Rabbins' testimony, as in this case it should be considerable, they precisely deny that any Birds were suffered to come into the Temple: neither could, if suffered (which perchance would have been difficult to hinder) nest there; because of the evenness of the roof. They tell us of high places, and altars there, everywhere, which they would have the altars here mentioned. But these to be the object of David's longing, or of his expression, in reference to those that came from afar off to worship at Jerusalem, were too absurd to imagine. The way that has been found by some, and is followed by most, to clear all this, is by a different connection, or syntax, of the words, leaving out the supplement *even,* and taking that which follows, *thine altars, O Lord of hosts,* &c. as an abrupt and pathetical exclamation; as if he had said: O for your altars, O my God: where, and when shall I be so happy? your altars, your altars, &c. Or, making that of the Birds to come in by way of parenthesis, to join the latter part of the verse with the second, *My soul longeth,* &c. which is the way that some of the Rabbins also do follow. The difference is in the coherence of the words: the sense is the same, either of these two last ways. And since we have so much authority for the clear way, as the consent of so many Interpreters, not above one that I can find, and he but faintly, dissenting; I know no reason, why we should rather embrace that which is more dark, and inextricable.

*{Blessed are they that dwell in thy house}* See upon Psalm 15.1. *Who shall abide in thy Tabernacle,* and Psal. 23.6. *I will dwell,* &c.

**V. 5. ***{Blessed is the man whose strength is in thee}* That is, who will not be dismayed from a good purpose, and necessary duties, by apparent difficulties, but puts his trust in you; whose confidence is in your, not his own strength. Or, according to Junius, *Happy those, who are enabled by you, to undergo these ways (or this journey) according to their own heart's desire*.

*{in whose heart are the ways of them}* I know not what this (*of them*) does make here: it does very much obscure the truth, and hardly affords any sense at all. The old translation, *in whose heart are thy ways,* was much better. In the Hebrew it is, word for word, *in whose heart are the ways;* or, properly, *the high ways* מסלות which lead into your house, that is; spoken of, and to travelers; as all, or the far greater part at least, of Interpreters, do grant. His meaning is, They that heartily desire to come to your house to worship you at the set time, though they dwell far off. The words will afford this sense also, *Viae sunt strata in medio eorum;* as some very learned would have them translated; that is, say they, There are convenient high-ways to lead them from their several habitations to Jerusalem. But I do not conceive it so proper to the place.

**V. 6. ***{of Baca}* That is, *of mulberry trees,* which was a dry place, so that they which passed through must dig pits for water: (signifying That no lets can hinder them that are fully bent to come to God's house, neither does God use to fail them.) It may be the place mentioned, 2 Sam. 5.23, 24. It is said that the nature of the mulberry tree is, to delight in hot and dry places. See more upon the next words.

*{the rain also filleth the pools}* Unto these godly Pilgrims in their need and want of water, (one of the greatest miseries that can befall poor travelers, in hot countries especially) he proposes a double succor; the one from their own industry, by digging wells: which in the former member, *Who passing through the valley,* &c. denotes, (in case the first fail, because springs are not everywhere to be found) from God immediately, filling their pools or cisterns, prepared for that purpose, with a plentiful rain. This is the most literal sense, as I conceive; and most received. Yet is not their interpretation, because it favors of more acute sense, as long as the words will bear that too, (but first it must be known, that the word ברכות by most, in this place, translated *pools;* does oftener signify *blessings*) altogether to be rejected, who thus translate: *Qui transeuntes per vallem mori, fontem constituunt eam, quos etiam benedictionum operit pluvia:* which is by them (for out of the translation itself hardly would any man gather any such thing) further explained to this effect: Who though they pass through dry sandy places, yet comfort themselves with the remembrance of that divine Fountain to which they are going; yea though *a rain of blessings,* (that is, plentiful rain;) and stormy showers overtake, and cover them; yet through thick and thin, through fair and foul, they go on cheerfully to their God. This, though very different from the former interpretation, as to this particular verse; yet to the general scope, both come to one effect. But they that have translated מורה (here) *rain a doctor,* or teacher, (which is sometimes the signification of it) they go so far from the general drift and scope, as that I think it needless to trouble the reader with their mistakes.

*{filleth}* Heb. *covereth.*

**V. 7. ***{from strength to strength}* Or, *from company to company.* They are never weary, but increase in strength and courage, till they come to God's house.

*{every one of them in Zion appeareth before God}* Some, because of the ambiguity of the word אל which is either a preposition, importing *apud, ad:* or a noun substantive, signifying *God:* according to this latter signification, translate; *videbitur Deus Deorum in Sion:* but agree in their exposition with the former, in the general meaning; to wit, That these godly travelers shall at last reap the benefit of their long trouble, in that they shall all see God; or appear before God. An excellent type of our perpetual, and, most troublesome pilgrimage in this world, to be rewarded after with the beatifical vision of God, in a better world. But here is a great question; and they are much put to it, that would have David to have written this Psalm, when persecuted by either Saul, or his own Son Absalom, to give a satisfactory answer, how Zion comes here to be named. For all Saul's time, the Ark was not there; Zion was then no more, than every ordinary hill. The persecution by his Son, was so soon ended, that it stands with little probability that David should so complain for that time of absence; making no mention at all (which is well observed by some) in all this Psalm, (which in other Psalms, when he complains of his absence forced by the violence of persecution, he does: as Psal. 42, &c. 61.) of any sufferings in any other kind at that time, when (upon that supposition) in so much danger of losing both crown and life at one time. This has made some very judicious Expositors to forsake the common opinion, of David's writing of this in exile, and time of persecution: and to think his absence, (that also forced, in some sense; and much against his will) in the prosecution of his wars, when himself was present in person, to be the more probable both time and occasion of it. This I should far sooner believe, than what is commonly believed by others about it. But what I think most probable, I have already delivered upon the third verse, *yea the sparrow,* &c.

**V. 9. ***{and look upon the face of thine anointed}* That a King, either David, or some other, is intended by that expression, is the opinion of most; and to me too, seems most likely. Though there be different interpretations too, if I would make use of them against my own judgment. The Chaldee Paraphrast understands it of the Messiah, that was expected; and so most Rabbins after him. Now that these holy travelers should be appointed in their way, and solemn form of praying, to remember their King, I think no man will think strange. But then it follows in the next verse, *For a day in thy courts,* &c. *I had rather be a door-keeper,* &c. which being added as a reason of the former prayer, will not make good coherence, except we conceive the prayer, *and look upon the face,* &c. to be uttered by David for himself in his own person. For therefore he prays that God would be gracious unto him, and speedily deliver him from this banishment, and the sad consequence of it, his absence from God's house; because such an opinion he has of it, that, &c. If therefore David speaks this of himself, and in his own person, this may seem inconsistent with our appositions concerning the subject of the Psalm, declared before upon the third verse. I did foresee this before, as I mention it here; and yet did not think it a sufficient ground to make me change my opinion. First, because I do not think it altogether impossible, that David, though he intended it especially for the use of others, yet in penning of the Psalm, might insert somewhat in his own person. And as for the consequence, it may be very good, without any relation to any such forced absence, if David pray, that God will *look upon his face,* or bless him, because of his extraordinary love and affection (best known unto God himself) to his house and service. It may be all Kings that have loved the house of God, as David did, have not sped so well as David, in point of temporal rewards. All times are not alike. There is somewhat else that God has in store for them that love him, besides temporal rewards; or else David himself, I believe, did miss of that, which he chiefly expected. However, that consequence will be made good by David himself, the best Interpreter of himself, Psal. 132.1, 2, &c. *Lord remember David, and all his afflictions: How he sware unto the Lord, and vowed unto the mighty God of Jacob, Surely I will not come,* &c. *untill I find out a place for the Lord; an habitation for the mighty God of Jacob.* Lastly, it is not absolutely necessary, that this *For,* &c. of the tenth verse, should refer to the prayer for David: it may cohere with some of the former verses, and that of David (as elsewhere divers like) inserted by a parenthesis. Neither need I be very solicitous to answer all objections, since I leave it free to the reader, to follow what himself shall like best.

**V. 10. ***{better than a thousand}* He wishes to be but one day in God's Church, rather than a thousand among worldlings; and to be a perpetual servant in God's house, as Exod. 21.6. though in the meanest place, than to dwell at liberty among the wicked.

*{thousand}* Or, *thousands elsewhere.*

*{I had rather be a doorkeeper}* Heb. *I would choose rather to sit at the threshold.*

**V. 11. ***{a sun and shield}* The giver of all good to his, and protector of them against all evil.

*{shield}* Gen. 15.1. Psal. 119.114.

*{the Lord will give grace and glory}* Favor with him, and honor before others.

*{no good thing will he withhold}* But will from time to time increase his blessings towards his more and more.

*{no good thing will he withhold from them that walk uprightly}* Psal. 34.9, 10.

**V. 12. ***{blessed is the man that trusteth in thee}* Psal. 2.12.

# PSALM LXXXV

## TITLE

*{for}* Or, *of.*

**Verse 1. ***{Thou hast been favourable unto thy land}* They confess, that God's free mercy was the cause of all their deliverances, because he loved the land. He speaks this of deliverances in former times, as appears by the 5 verse. To what purpose, see verse 5, and upon Psal. 22.21. *For thou hast heard me from the horns of the unicorns.*

*{favourable}* Heb. *well pleased.*

**V. 2. ***{Thou hast forgiven the iniquity of thy people}* Psal. 32.1.

*{Thou hast covered all their sin}* You have buried them, that they may not come into judgment.

**V. 3. ***{thou hast turned thyself from the fierceness of thine anger}* Or, *thou hast turned thy anger from waxing hot.*

*{from the fierceness of thine anger}* Not only in withdrawing your rod, but also in forgiving our sins.

**V. 5. ***{Wilt thou be angry with us for ever?}* As in times past they had felt God's mercy in their deliverance, so now, being oppressed with the long continuance of evils, they pray unto God, That according to his nature, he would be merciful unto them.

**V. 7. ***{shew us thy salvation}* He confesses that our deliverance comes only from God's mercy, Psal. 6.4.

**V. 8. ***{He will speak peace unto his people}* He will send all prosperity to his Church, when he has sufficiently corrected them: also by his punishments the faithful shall learn to beware that they return not to like offenses.

*{but let them not}* Or, *that they may not.*

**V. 10. ***{Mercy and truth are met together}* Since it is certain, as appears by the first and following verses, that the people and Church were then afflicted, when this Psalm was written, the Future (*Mercy and truth shall meet; righteousness and peace shall kiss*) would have been more proper, as it is expressed in most other Translations, than the Preterit. Now this here, and all that follows, are expressions of happy times, and settled happiness, as founded upon righteousness and mercy, by which Kingdoms are established; and without which no peace and prosperity can be of long continuance. Some understand the *mercy,* and *truth* of God; which may be too: for they have near relation one to another. They must come down from heaven, before they be found among men; and the entertaining of those on earth, secures the passage, and keeps heaven open.

*{Mercy and truth}* I have already set down what I think most probable. But because it is an obscure place, as may appear by the variety of expositions to give the better content to the Reader, I shall mention some other interpretations. Of *truth,* joined with *mercy,* and spoken of God, how to be understood; see upon Psalm 57. ver. 10. *For thy mercy is great,* &c. Truth, so taken here, of God, &c. as it may imply the performance and accomplishment of any promise made by God to his people, in general; so more particularly, the performance of that which for the consequence and excellency, we may call the accomplishment of all promises; (and so expected by the faithful Jews, and then especially most thought upon by them, when in great trouble and calamity;) to wit, the coming of the promised Messiah, or Deliverer. This sense is followed by some of good credit and authority. There be, who refer the words to Christ and his Gospel; but in another sense making *mercy,* and *truth, righteousness,* and *peace:* to answer one another, antithetically, as they call it. They would have *truth,* to relate to *mercy;* if the *truth,* that is faith, (for so the word signifies too:) of man, or in man, as some: as either, truth; that is, integrity of life, &c. to answer to mercy in God: as the cause, and the effect, (but in several respects as we take *truth* for, either faith, or integrity:) inseparable. And so *peace* in man, to answer to the *justice in God:* not to be understood of his ordinary justice, but of that *justice of God,* by which we are justified. This interpretation, I believe, came from Junius, in his Notes upon the Bible; but is largely prosecuted by Gerhard, the Papist; who quotes Rabbins for it: though Christ, I believe he might have quoted some body else, more pertinently and truly. Calvin quotes St. Augustine much to the same purpose: and is well pleased with the matter itself, as very sound and useful, but not to serve here, as the proper meaning: which is my opinion too. However, I have named them; that the Reader, if he think fit, may have recourse unto them.

**V. 11. ***{Truth shall spring out of the earth, and righteousness shall look down from heaven}* Here is ground enough for any man to exercise his wit, to devise differences between *truth,* and *righteousness,* as here opposed; and why the one should be said to *spring out of the earth,* and the other assigned to heaven; *truth for faith,* in men, upon earth: *justice,* as before explained, by which we are justified, (called by St. Paul, *the justice of God,*) in heaven, or from heaven, &c. Many such speculations may arise from these words, if a man shall precisely stick to the letters and syllables. But take them, as with some of the best Interpreters I think they should be taken, proverbially; then the meaning is, that heaven and earth shall be full of both, both of truth and righteousness; an emphatical expression of great abundance.

**V. 12. ***{land shall yield her increase}* Psal. 67.7.
