**V. 13. ***{Righteousness shall go before him}* When peace shall be restored to the land, and with it piety and godliness shall flourish, then shall righteousness go before God: that is, men shall walk before God in righteousness; according to that of old Zacharias in the Gospel; *That we being delivered out of the hands of our enemies, might serve him without fear, in holinesse and righteousnesse before him* (which is here, *Righteousnesse shall go before him*) *all the days of our life,* Luke 1.74, 75. All which, in this and the former, from the context, though immediately intended, and so hyperbolically expressed, of temporal deliverances, and happy days to ensue upon it, had a more literal fulfilling in Christ, according to the predictions of many Prophets to the same purpose. See Psal. 45 and 72.

# PSALM LXXXVI

## TITLE

*{A prayer of David}* Or, *a prayer, being a Psalm of David*.

**Verse 1. ***{Bow down thine ear}* David in persecution thus prayed, leaving the same to the Church, as a monument, how to seek redress against their miseries.

**V. 2. ***{for I am holy}* Heb. חסיד a word of great pregnancy, and of different notions. When it is attributed to God, imports a tenderness of affection in God, towards all his creatures in general, but especially toward them that have more particular relation unto him, by mutual covenants and engagements, whether general, or particular, of faithful obedience, and religious worship, on the one side; of love, favor and protection, on the other. Such a general covenant was the Law, which extended to all the Jews: and the covenant with Abraham, in which all the Jews, or Israelites had equal interest. Of a more particular covenant, though we might find divers other examples, yet shall not need to seek further than David himself, that of all others in that kind, being the most express, and most considerable. So, God חסיד (always actively) that is, merciful and gracious; and man חסיד, sometimes actively, that is, meek, loving, charitable towards men, as affecting conformity with God, to the utmost of humane ability; and pious, religious, holy towards God, in all religious duties and actions: and sometimes passively, in the bare consideration of his nearer relation, and more particular interest in God's favor; as if you should say, God's favored or beloved Saint, or servant. Which title and privilege, although many others, as many as either were within the general, or had nearer relation to God, by some more particular covenant or promise, might challenge; yet that it did in a more excellent way belong unto David, may appear by those expressions, חסדי דוד, *the sure mercies of David,* 2 Chr. 6.42. חסדי דוד הנאמנים, *the sure mercies of David,* Isa. 55.3. The Greek Septuagint expresses it by ὅσιος, very fitly, as the word (though sometimes opposed to ἱερός) is often used; and in regard (which they have often respect unto) to its origin, coming from the Hebrew, the termination only, (as is usual) being changed. The Syriac also leaves out the י or *Jod,* saying חס for חסיד. Now to make application of what has been said to this place, though the word signifies *mansuetus,* and *misericors;* that is, meek and merciful, as some have translated it: yet I do not think that the proper signification, neither here, nor Psalm 16.10. *neither suffer thy Holy One to see corruption.* We have the same expression, Deut. 33.8. *And of Levi he said Let thy Thummim and thy Urim be with thy holy one,* &c. where no man will say, that the tribe of Levi is called God's *holy one,* because of their meekness, or mercy, of which we read nothing elsewhere; but of their zeal in God's cause, Exod. 32.25, 26, &c. by which they became afterwards God's peculiar, by a particular covenant; and God was said of them more particularly, to be their *portion,* or *inheritance,* as Deut. 10.9. and elsewhere often. Here then also, *pius,* as some; or *pius servus;* that is, *thy religious:* but, more properly, *thy devoted,* or, *devoted servant,* is certainly more proper and true. *Nam is sum, quem benignitate prosequeris:* that is, *For I am one, whom thou dost particularly favour:* Junius his translation, expresses well the meaning too, but more like a paraphrase than a translation. To this agrees our English margin, *holy;* or, *one whom thou favourest.* He explains himself in part, in the next words, when he calls God, his God; and himself his *servant, that puts his trust in him.* And so again in the fourth verse, he calls himself his servant.

**V. 3. ***{for I cry unto thee daily}* Which shows how earnestly he longed for deliverance.

*{daily}* Heb. *all the day.*

**V. 4. ***{unto thee, O Lord, do I lift up my soul}* Psal. 25.1.

**V. 5. ***{For thou, Lord, art good, and ready to forgive}* Joel 2.13.

**V. 6. ***{attend to the voice of my supplications}* By crying and calling continually, he shows that we ought not to be weary, though God grant not forthwith our request, but that we must earnestly and often call upon him, verse 7.

**V. 8. ***{Among the gods there is none like unto thee, O Lord}* Among them that are worshipped for gods in several nations, that knew not the true God. But though there be that are called gods, whether in heaven or in earth, (as there be many) yet to us, and in truth, there is but one God; as the Apostle teaches, 1 Cor. 8.5, 6. and so says the Psalmist here too; *thou art God alone,* verse 10. What is here negatively; and, in effect, positively set down by our Psalmist, the same, by Moses, is expressed by way of interrogation; *Who is like unto thee, O Lord, among the gods?* Exod. 15.11. It is in the Hebrew; מי כמכה באלים יהוה; from the first letters of which four words, (מכבי M.C.B.I.) being their Motto in their Ensigns, or Colors; came the Name and Title of those famous Worthies, who fought the Lord's battles, (as appears by the History that bears that name) with so much valor, and equal success.

*{neither are there any works like unto thy works}* Deut. 3.24.

*{like unto thy works}* He condemns all Idols, forasmuch as they can do no great works to declare that they are gods, Isa. 41.23.

**V. 10. ***{thou art God alone}* Deut. 6.4 and 32.39. Isa. 37.16 and 44.6. Mark 12.29. 1 Cor. 8.4. Ephes. 4.6.

**V. 11. ***{Teach me thy way}* He confesses himself ignorant, till God has taught him, and his heart variable, and separated from God, till God join it to him, and confirm it in his obedience.

*{Teach me thy way, O Lord, I will walk in thy truth}* Psal. 25.4 and 119.33.

**V. 13. ***{from the lowest hell}* That is, from most great danger of death, out of which none but only the mighty hand of God could deliver him.

*{hell}* Or, *grave.*

**V. 14. ***{violent}* Heb. *terrible.*

*{have sought after my soul}* He shows, that there can be no moderation nor equity where proud tyrants reign; and that the lack of God's fear is a privilege to all vice and cruelty.

*{have not set thee before them}* Psal. 54.3.

**V. 15. ***{But thou, O Lord, art a God full of compassion}* Exod. 34.6. Numb. 14.18. Psal. 103.8 and 111.4 and 145.8.

**V. 16. ***{servant, and save the son of thine handmaid}* Psalm 116.16.

*{save the son of thine handmaid}* He boasts not of his own virtues, but confesses that God of his free goodness has ever been merciful unto him, and given him power against his enemies, as to one of his own household.

**V. 17. ***{Shew me a token}* Deliver me, in that manner, that it may evidently appear that I am singularly beloved of you.

*{for good}* That my enemies may not dare to meddle with me any more, but I may be safe ever after, Psal. 41.11.

# PSALM LXXXVII

## TITLE

*{A Psalm or Song}* See Notes on the title of Psal. 30.

*{for}* Or, *of.*

**Verse 1. ***{His foundation is in the holy mountains}* The Chaldee Paraphrase, and other Rabbins make these words to be part of the Title, not of the Psalm; to this effect; *A Psalm,* &c. *whose foundation,* (or, *argument*) *is concerning the holy mountains.* To this they are induced, first, because there is no other antecedent, (in sight, or expressed) to which this relative *His* should be referred. But this מזמור, or Psalm. And in case a man would say, Jerusalem is to be understood, they answer, that the affix pronoun in יסודתו is masculine, not feminine. But as for a relative to be without an antecedent; or a Masculine affix, to relate to a collective Feminine such as Church or City is; there be many examples of both in the Scripture. Besides *His,* may refer to God as well: His foundation, &c. that is, his dwelling place, (Ark and Temple, so ordinarily styled) is now firm and constant, founded upon two holy hills, Zion and mount Moriah: whereas his dwelling place, in former days, was in a moveable Tabernacle. However, though some explode it, as an idle conceit, I do not see what inconvenience comes of it, if we should grant those words to be part of the Title. The Argument of the Psalm (a glorious argument; briefly, but very emphatically set out in this short Psalm,) is, the future amplitude of the Church, and the conversion of all Nations, (whereof many are named) to the Christian faith.

**V. 3. ***{Glorious things are spoken of thee}* Though your glorious estate does not yet appear; yet you shall be famous in after-times.

**V. 4. ***{I will make mention of Rahab}* That is, Egypt: as Psal. 89.10. *Thou hast broken Rahab in pieces:* so called in the Scripture, (for it was no common name) enigmatically, from her strength, and thereupon pride, and haughtiness: which is the proper signification of the word. This is the common opinion: but some very learned in the languages, have found out, that one part of Egypt was anciently called *rib,* which they would have to be the same as *Rahab* here; one part, (as is ordinary enough) standing for the whole. But besides, that this is but a plausible conjecture, of which there is no great certainty, as grounded upon a single testimony; if a man shall well consider of Job 26.12. *He divideth the sea with his power, and by his understanding he smiteth Rahab,* (which is by most translated, *the swelling of waters;* and by some, *place,* that is, the main Sea,) and compare with this of Job, that passage, Psal. 89.9, 10. *Thou rulest the raging of the sea,* &c. *Thou hast broken Rahab in pieces as one that is slain,* &c. in which by Rahab, most interpreters understand Egypt: he will, I say, if I be not mistaken, think it more probable, that this appellation of Egypt by that name, was grounded upon that place of Job: (for Job is generally supposed to have been written by, or before Moses,) or if not upon that very place, yet upon that use and notion of the word, (then vulgar and ordinary among the Hebrews) which it has in that place. For how fitly might that mighty host of the Egyptians, which was overthrown by God in the Red Sea, be resembled to those mounting billows of the raging Sea, there called רהב, *rehab,* which God is said there to break; yea *to smite:* (that is, to quell,) a word, of itself, more proper to a host, than to the Sea? Or, if it be certain, that a part of Egypt was called *Rib,* I shall easily grant, that the Hebrews by terming the whole land *Rahab,* alluded to that proper Name of that part: but so, as their chiefest aim was to the proper signification of *rahab,* for pride, &c. in their own language.

*{to them that know me}* Yea, *inter scientes me:* or, *inter cognitores meos;* that is, among them that know me; as most others express. That is, The time shall come, when Egyptians and Babylonians, &c. now, people that know not God, (Psal. 79.6.) shall be reckoned among them that know me; and when it shall be said of the people of those Countries, now strangers and aliens to the God and Common Wealth of Israel, that they were born (by spiritual regeneration) in Zion.

*{Ethiopia}* Heb. *Cush.*

**V. 6. ***{The Lord shall count, when he writeth up the people}* When he takes a survey of his people: and makes his Census book, wherein all that belong to him, and owned by him, are registered; called, *the writing of the house of Israel,* Ezek. 13.9.

*{that this man was born there}* He shall enter into his book, all those Nations, as true Israelites, born in Zion. The expression has some affinity with that of Isa., *One shall say, I am the Lord's, and another shall call himself by the name of Jacob; and another shall subscribe with his hand unto the Lord, and surname himself by the name of Israel,* Isa. 44.5.

**V. 7. ***{As well the singers, as the players on instruments}* The words are somewhat differently translated by some; but of the drift and scope of the words, that by them, the greatness and universality of spiritual rejoicing that shall be in those days, is set out, there is little, or no difference. Some translate, *sicut praecantor ita chorus.*

*{all my springs are in thee}* That this is spoken by the Psalmist in his own person, as professing that he will contribute all his abilities to the celebration of this public joy, and the praises of this new Jerusalem, is the opinion of many Expositors. Others make them to be God's own words to his Church, under the type of waters abundantly flowing, promising plenty of all spiritual gifts and graces. We have the same metaphor, John 7.38. *out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water:* by Isa. called, *the Wells of salvation,* Isa. 12.3.

# PSALM LXXXVIII

## TITLE

*{A Song or Psalm}* See Notes on the title of Psal. 30.

*{for}* Or, *of.*

*{Mahalath Leannoth}* On a wind-instrument, to sing by courses, as Exod. 15.21. Ezra 3.11. See Notes on the title of Psal. 5. Or, *of the disease afflicting*.

*{Maschil of Heman the Ezrahite}* Or, *a Psalm of Heman the Ezrahite*; *giving instruction*.

*{Heman the Ezrahite}* See 1 Kings 4.31.

**Verse 1. ***{I have cried day and night}* It is a question, whether with a loud voice, or in his heart only. For to *cry,* in Scripture, often is as much as to pray: as, *The righteous crieth,* &c. Psal. 34.17. and *He shall cry unto me,* 89.26. But indeed the word there in the original צעק, is different from that here: there קרא here זעק: however; Exod. 14.15. where no prayer, no words at all are mentioned, yet, *why cryest thou,* &c. which by the ancients is generally understood of mental speech, or invocation. However, as the condition of the Psalmist was at this time, it is more likely, that he expressed himself loudly: which is more natural to excessive grief, (such as is here set out) and indeed more helpful. For by free speech in such a case, the heart ready to break, is oftentimes eased. But either way, we may certainly infer, that *crying* does import earnestness, and intention, if not of the voice, yet of the heart: which is the very life of prayer, and without which no prayer can be very acceptable, or successful. The Prophet tells God here of this his crying, or earnest praying, upon good ground, as knowing that God is very apt to hear, according to his own profession and promise (though it be understood with some limitations:) Psal. 50.15. *And call upon me in the day of trouble,* &c.

**V. 2. ***{Let my prayer come before thee}* It is Calvin's opinion, that this Psalm was intended by the sacred Composer, for a form of Prayer, for them especially, who through the extremity of their misery, and want of all comforts, did apprehend themselves utterly forsaken, to keep them from desperation. It is indeed an excellent pattern for that purpose, when we shall consider both the dismalness of the complaints in every particular, as here set out; beyond which it is not easy to conceive it can be said: and the worthiness of the Author, a Prophet, and a man of such excellent wisdom in his lifetime, that his name stands upon record in Holy Writ for it: 1 Kings 4.31. Indeed I am of opinion as such a form, with due consideration of circumstances in such a case, may do much more good, than the crude and extemporary conceptions of some, whose chiefest work is, to magnify their own gifts; and to inveigh against all set forms.

*{before thee}* In the former verse, *I have cried day and night before thee:* where may be understood two ways: either as an expression of his faith and confidence; whereof see upon Psalm 38.9. *Lord all my desire,* &c. or, that he did not complain unto men so much, (as many in such cases are apt, more unto men, than unto God) but that he addressed himself immediately to God, in whom only he trusted for deliverance. And now he prays that his prayer may come *before God:* not that he doubted but God did hear him, as well praying, as crying: (whether we take them both for one, or no:) but see *before him,* now, as to be effectual and available according to the purpose of it, and that it might appear by his timely aid and succor, unto others, as well as unto himself, that he had heard him. The observation of such seeming contradictions is very profitable throughout the whole Scripture.

**V. 3. ***{full of troubles}* Psal. 123.3.

**V. 5. ***{Free among the dead}* No more regarded than a man dead and buried, Psal. 31.12. As if he said, a free Citizen of death and the grave; having nothing more to do with the living.

*{whom thou rememberest no more}* As to this present world, when they are once gone out of it; and their life, and with it, your providence over them in matters belonging to life, is at an end. Many things are spoken by holy men, by way of aggravation of their present case and misery, according to common sense, and vulgar expressions, which may not be taken as spoken positively, or dogmatically. See upon Psal. 6.5. *For in death,* &c. and Psal. 22.1. *My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me.*

*{from thy hand}* Rather, *by thy hand,* because he speaks of men slain.

**V. 7. ***{with all thy waves}* The storms of your wrath have overwhelmed me, Psal. 42.7.

*{waves}* Psal. 42.7.

**V. 8. ***{Mine acquaintance}* He attributes the loss and displeasure of his friends to God's providence, whereby he partly punishes him, and partly tries him.

*{far from me}* Job 19.13. Psal. 31.11 and 142.4.

*{an abomination unto them}* Heb. *abominations:* that is, most abominable.

*{I am shut up}* I see none end of my sorrows. I am compassed with evils on all sides, both within, and without, that I know not which way to turn: like unto one that is a close prisoner.

**V. 9. ***{Mine eye mourneth}* Mine eyes and face declare my sorrows.

**V. 10. ***{Wilt thou shew wonders to the dead?}* The Psalmist takes it for granted, that as it is proper unto God to do *wonders* (among which of all kind, strange deliverances in greatest extremities, none of the least:) so it is very fit, that God should be praised and glorified for those *wonders* that he does. So that in effect all that he aims at by those three verses, is but this, to say, that God, if he make not haste to his succor, before it be too late, he will bereave himself of no small store of thanks and praises, in suffering him to perish, who was so fully purposed to magnify him to the utmost of his ability for his deliverance. But this must be supposed to be spoken of God, and to God, by that ordinary ἀνθρωποπάθεια, without which men, as long as men, can scarce speak, or be spoken unto; and therefore ordinarily used by God himself, when he speaks unto men. And again, spoken not doctrinally, but pathetically; with reference to this present world, and that little knowledge, that was among men, in those days, of the things of the world to come. See upon Psal. 6.5. *For in death,* &c. I cannot be therefore of their opinion, though men otherwise of great account and authority, whose censure upon this place is, that it is *vox parum considerata;* and that *excusari non potest excessus:* and the like: I see no reason for such rigor, except *My God, my God,* &c. Psal. 22.1. be *vox inconsiderata* too: which I hope they will not say. Even now, that the mysteries of the Kingdom of heaven are so fully revealed, and *life and immortality come to light,* as the Apostle speaks, 2 Tim. 1.10. Yet are we allowed by God, without offense, as godly men, to pray for life earnestly: (though still with submission) at all times; but especially if in present danger and sight of death: which in strict judgment, might be construed as an argument of our infidelity: as though we believed not that it is far better for us to be with Christ in Heaven, than to converse among sinners, and subject ourselves unto sin upon earth; or loved the company of men, friends, parents, and the like; better than that of God, Angels, Saints, &c. To conclude, men at the best, must be considered as men; but men, in passions, with a double regard: first, as men, that have the natural affections of men: and secondly, as men disturbed in their senses, that have not the perfect and natural use of their reason, which in extremity, which at other times, might be expected.

**V. 12. ***{in the land of forgetfulness?}* In the grave, where men forget all that was done here, Job 14.21. and they that are alive forget them that are dead, Job 24.20.

**V. 13. ***{in the morning shall my prayer prevent thee}* Psalm 5.3.

**V. 15. ***{from my youth up}* I am ever in great dangers and sorrows, as though my life should be cut off every moment.

**V. 17. ***{daily}* Or, *all the day.*

**V. 18. ***{Lover and friend hast thou put far from me}* Psal. 38.11.

*{mine acquaintance into darkness}* None will appear to my relief: they hide themselves from me. Or, their appearance, which should be to my comfort, some enlightening to me, if they did the part of friends; proves rather an increase of my darkness, or an aggravation of my misery. Or, as some others, *through my darkness,* or, *by reason of my darkness,* (that is, present miseries and adversities) they absent themselves, &c. according to that of the Poet, *Tempora si fuerint nubila, solus eris.*

# PSALM LXXXIX

## TITLE

*{Maschil of Ethan the Ezrahite}* Or, *a Psalm for Ethan the Ezrahite, to give instruction*.

*{Ethan}* It seems to be that Ethan mentioned, 1 Kings 4.31. who living to see the Kingdom divided in Rehoboam's time, composed this Psalm. Others think it was one of that name that lived in the captivity of Babylon.

*{the Ezrahite}* Of Zerah, 1 Chron. 2.6.

**Verse 1. ***{I will sing of the mercies of the Lord}* Though the horrible confusion of things might cause them to despair of God's favor, yet the manifold examples of his mercies caused them to trust in God, though, to man's judgment, they saw none occasion.

*{to all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation.*

**V. 2. ***{For I have said}* As he that assuredly believed it in heart.

*{Mercy shall be built up for ever}* Abide firm, like a strong building; not be moveable, like tents.

*{thy faithfulness shalt thou establish in the very heavens}* Your promise will be performed, as sure as the heavens abide: or, you have showed the unchangeableness of your truth, in settling the heavens.

*{heavens}* Psal. 119.89.

**V. 3. ***{I have made a Covenant with my chosen}* The Prophet shows what was the promise of God whereon he grounded his faith.

*{sworn unto David my servant}* 2 Sam. 7.11, &c.

**V. 4. ***{Thy seed will I establish for ever}* Verse 29, 36. This is performed in Christ the Son of David.

*{to all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation.*

**V. 5. ***{the heavens shall praise thy wonders}* The Psalmist having already verses 3, 4. *I have made a covenant with my chosen,* &c. made an entrance into the matter and subject of this Psalm, (God's promises unto David, and his posterity: and a particular and pathetical description of the lamentable condition of the Kingdom, at that time, contrary, as was apprehended by them that suffered, to those promises:) he presently breaks off from that argument, and falls upon a general consideration and acknowledgment of God's power, or omnipotency rather from this here, to verse 19. *Then thou spakest,* &c. He might be thought to digress unseasonably: but in very deed, it is not done by him, but for a particular reason, and with a respect to his intended matter. For purposing in the prosecution of that argument somewhat freely to expostulate with God, he is here at the first entrance, as it were, possessed with a sacred fear and horror; and before he dares to proceed any further, he does interpose this protestation of his faith and belief, of God; as of his power, and irresistible might: so of his infinite both Justice and Mercy: that in case his passion, or compassion should extort from him any expression that might offend either God, or godly men; he might appeal to this his foregoing protestation; and his words, if any dubiously, or dangerously uttered, might be tried and read in it. This was the method of God's Saints, and holy Prophets in former times: as has already been observed upon Psal. 73.1. *Truly God is good to Israel,* &c.

*{the heavens shall praise}* Most Expositors by *heavens* here, understand the Angels: which I wonder at. For though there is no question, but it is the Angels and Archangels' work in Heaven, to praise God, whom they look upon with continual admiration, wherein also their happiness does chiefly consist; yea, and to praise God for his love and care of men; his wonderful providence and mystical dispensations towards his Church, in general, and towards particular men, for their salvation and happiness: Yet since that by *the heavens,* we may understand (as is acknowledged) the very Fabric of the heavens, literally, as Psal. 19.1. *The heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament,* &c. and that besides, the authority of that precedent, this interpretation will better agree with the coherence, as will appear upon examination; I cannot but prefer it before the other. Certainly though all God's creatures in general do their parts in their kind, to set forth the power and wisdom of their Creator: none so mean and contemptible; but if rationally, and philosophically considered, will afford a good argument to assert it against atheists and epicures: yet the *heavens* above all others, have ever been held and acknowledged by heathens themselves, who ought to that purpose. See also upon Psal. 8.2. *Out of the mouth of babes,* &c.

*{in the Congregation of the Saints}* Those, who by *heavens* in this verse understand Angels, are somewhat put to it here, (though some take no notice of it) what to make of this, *Congregation of Saints.* For if by Congregation of Saints we understand Angels, then the Psalmist does say, that Angels, *in the congregation of angels,* &c. which is not so likely. If the Church, as elsewhere; how do the Angels (properly taken) praise God in the Church, or *congregation of men?* Some therefore would have it translated, *In thy Congregation* (or Church) *the Saints,* (that is, godly people) *thy faithfulness is to wit, shall be praised:* to be supplied from the former words: Or, *thy faithfulness also in the Congregation of the Saints:* to wit, shall be praised: which comes to the same purpose. But for further confirmation of that interpretation we better like, as is there showed; we have Psalm 29.2. *Give unto the Lord the glory due unto his Name: worship the Lord in the beauty of holiness.* Where, by *beauty of holiness,* the Church, or Sanctuary is understood, as is elsewhere showed. And again verse 9. of the same Psalm, *The voice of the Lord,—and in his Temple doth every one speak of his glory.* But especially Psal. 97.6. *The heavens declare his righteousness, and all people see his glory.* See the Note there.

**V. 6. ***{who, among the sons of the mighty, can be likened unto the Lord?}* That is, the angels: or, the great princes of the world, Psal. 29.1. See upon Psal. 86.8. *Among the Gods,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{God is greatly to be feared in the assembly of the Saints}* What is here to be understood by *the assembly* (or, *congregation*) *of Saints,* see upon ver. 5. *in the congregation;* what is here, *greatly to be feared,* others express, (nearer to the Original word) *very terrible:* which does not so well agree with the doctrine and practice of some, that pretend to much holiness, who think they cannot be too familiar with God, and like themselves the better for it, that they dare speak to him without any premeditation (nay censure and condemn them that dare not:) even whatsoever offers itself to them; as a man, when he is idle, would to his mate, that had as little to do as he. And thus the Devil makes them believe is to *worship God in spirit and truth;* because it is without ceremonies. But it is to be feared that God, however now patient at such affronts, will one day appear terrible to them that have made so bold with him. And that they may not flatter themselves, as though this belonged to them that live under the Law only; let them read Hebr. 12.28, 29. *Wherefore we receiving a Kingdom,* &c.

*{of all them that are about him}* So before, of men there plainly not angels, (as most Expositors would have it here:) *Let all that be round about him, bring presents to him that ought to be feared.* See also upon Psal. 26.6. *So will I compass thine altar.*

**V. 8. ***{who is a strong Lord like unto thee? or to thy faithfulness round about thee?}* I see no color for this translation, *or to thy faithfulness round about thee;* neither do I find it anywhere else but here. I should suspect the printing, but that I find it so in all Bibles. The sense, both of itself more obvious and with all translations I have seen, agree upon is this: *O Lord God of hosts, who is a strong Lord like unto thee? and thy faithfulnesse is round about thee.* Junius elegantly, *circumvestit te:* as Psal. 104.1. *thou art clothed with honour and majesty.*

*{like unto thee}* Psal. 35.10.

**V. 9. ***{Thou rulest the raging of the sea}* We doubt not of your power, for you have showed it in ruling the sea in the greatest tempest, and pulling down the proud enemies of the Church, Psal. 93.3, 4.

*{raging of the sea}* Psal. 65.7.

**V. 10. ***{Rahab}* Or, *Egypt*. See before upon Psal. 87.4. *I will make mention of Rahab.*

*{with thy strong arm}* Heb. *with the arm of thy strength.*

**V. 11. ***{The heavens are thine, the earth also is thine}* Gen. 1.1. Psal. 24.1 and 50.12.

**V. 12. ***{Tabor and Hermon shall rejoice in thy Name}* Tabor stands westward from Jerusalem; and Hermon eastward: so that all places, east, west, north and south, have cause to rejoice in God's power, which makes them fruitful.

**V. 13. ***{a mighty arm}* Heb. *an arm with might.*

**V. 14. ***{Justice and judgment are the habitation of thy throne}* Psal. 97.2. For hereby he judges the world, and shows himself a merciful Father and faithful protector unto his.

*{habitation}* Or, *establishment.*

**V. 15. ***{the joyful sound}* Of the trumpet, calling them to the solemn assemblies, Num. 10.6. Some refer it to Num. 23.21. *The Lord his God is with him, and the shout of a King is among them;* where see Expositors. Others more generally, *jubilatio:* and expound it of spiritual joy in God, and holy raptures.

*{in the light of thy countenance}* The tokens of your favor seen in the Sanctuary.

**V. 16. ***{In thy Name shall they rejoice}* As you are made known to them in your public ordinances.

**V. 17. ***{of their strength}* They have all their strength from you.

*{thou shalt be exalted}* Psal. 75.10.

**V. 18. ***{For the Lord is our defence; and the holy One of Israel is our King}* Or, *For our strength was of the Lord, and our King of the holy One of Israel.* That is, you gavest us David a strong King in those days, ver. 19, &c.

**V. 19. ***{Then thou spakest to thy holy One}* Here the Psalmist does return to his intended subject, after a pretty long digression, from verse 5. *And the Heavens,* &c. of which digression, and the occasion of it, see upon that verse.

*{Then thou spakest}* Then: in Hebrew אז which sometimes is used of a time long before; yea sometimes, of eternity, as Psal. 93.2. *Thy throne is established of old.* Heb. *from then:* and elsewhere. Some therefore here also translate, *Olim;* that is, *long ago:* or, *time was when:* or to that effect. Or, *then,* if we keep it, may denote a certain time; to wit, In those days when Samuel lived, &c.

*{thy holy One}* Most understand Samuel, 1 Sam. 16. Others Nathan, 2 Sam. 7.4, 5. Some Rabbins with addition only of the last letter of the Alphabet, make a plural of it: which is the reading of the Greek translation also; and in very deed, those things that follow, seem to refer to both places, whereof the one contains Samuel's message from God, or vision concerning David: and the other, Nathan's. As for example: *I have laid hope upon one that is mighty,* &c. in this very verse: I know most Expositors would have this to be spoken by anticipation: *I have it mighty;* for, *one that I will make mighty.* We confess that what might soever was in David, was of God's especial favor, who had destined him to such a place. But nevertheless I think it very probable that the Psalmist here does allude to 1 Sam. 16.18. *I have seen a son of Jesse—a mighty valiant man, and a man of war,* &c. As for 2 Sam. 7. it is apparent that many verses together, with little alteration, be taken out of it. Most probable therefore that the Psalmist had respect to both those Chapters and Prophets.

*{out of the people}* One of mean parentage: or the choicest man, for parts, among all the people, 1 Sam. 10.24.

**V. 20. ***{I have found David my servant}* 1 Sam. 16.12.

**V. 22. ***{The enemy shall not exact}* He shall not be tributary (the great reproach and plague of Kingdoms) to his enemies abroad: nor infested with the insolencies and insurrections of rebellious wicked people, at home. This sense I think here probable: though I know *the son of wickedness* elsewhere used of external enemies also. If any like better of it here too, then both members (which by way of rhetorical amplification, or further confirmation, is very usual in Scripture) will come to one sense. But neither is it unusual, (as elsewhere has been showed) that the same phrase, sometimes in one and the same Chapter; yea sometimes, verse; should be used in different senses.

**V. 23. ***{I will beat down his foes before his face}* Though there shall be evermore enemies against God's Kingdom, yet he promises to overcome them.

**V. 24. ***{my mercy shall be with him}* I will mercifully perform my promises to him, notwithstanding his defects and defaults.

*{in my Name shall his horn be exalted}* His power, glory, and estate.

*{horn be exalted}* Verse 17.

**V. 25. ***{I will set his hand also in the Sea}* He shall enjoy the lands round about him by conquest. See before upon Psal. 72.8. *He shall have dominion also from sea to sea,* &c.

**V. 26. ***{Thou art my Father}* His excellent dignity shall appear herein, That he shall be named the son of God, and the first-born, wherein he is a figure of Christ.

**V. 28. ***{His seed also will I make to endure for ever}* Verse 4, 29, 36. Though for the sins of the people the whole state of this Kingdom decayed, yet God reserved still a root, till he had accomplished this promise in Christ. See 2 Sam. 7.14. *I will be his father, and he shall be my son,* &c. The same is here repeated, but with some Poetical amplifications. See also upon Psal. 72. title, &c. of the application of these words to Christ, and how they belong to Solomon.

**V. 30. ***{and walk not in my judgments}* God's law is divided into judgments, statutes, and commandments; that is, judicial, ceremonial, and moral laws, Mal. 4.4.

**V. 31. ***{break my statutes}* Heb. *profane my statutes.*

**V. 32. ***{visit their transgression with the rod}* 2 Sam. 7.14.

**V. 33. ***{Nevertheless my lovingkindness will I not utterly take from him}* Though the faithful answer not in all points to their profession, yet God will not break his Covenant with them.

*{will I not utterly take from him}* Heb. *I will not make void from him.*

*{to fail}* Heb. *to lie.*

**V. 34. ***{My Covenant will I not break}* If they profane my statutes, ver. 31. yet I will not profane my Covenant.

*{nor alter the thing that is gone out of my lips}* For God in promising, has respect to his mercy, and not to man's power in performing, Mal. 3.6. Jam. 1.17.

**V. 35. ***{that I will not lie}* Heb. *if I lie.*

**V. 36. ***{His seed shall endure for ever, and his throne as the sun before me}* Verse 4, 29. 2 Sam. 7.16. Luke 1.33. John 12.34.

*{as the sun before me}* Perpetual and glorious.

**V. 37. ***{as a faithful witness in heaven}* That is, as the Moon, which though it be sometimes in the wane, and in the eclipse, yet remains a true witness of God's Covenant, Jer. 33.20. Some by *faithful witness,* would have the rainbow to be meant; of which see Gen. 9.13, &c. But since Sun and Moon are often mentioned in this Covenant of God with David, and no express mention of the rainbow anywhere upon this occasion, it is not so likely it should be here intended.

**V. 39. ***{Thou hast made void the covenant of thy servant}* Because of the horrible confusion of things, the Prophet complains to God, as though he saw not the performance of his promise.

*{thou hast profaned his crown}* By this, he means the horrible dissipation and renting of the Kingdom by Jeroboam: unless it were another Ethan. See Notes on the Title.

**V. 40. ***{Thou hast broken down all his hedges}* Psal. 80.12. Isa. 5.5. You have made his Kingdom like a field without hedges, exposed to spoil.

**V. 42. ***{thou hast made all his enemies to rejoice}* By giving them victory over him.

**V. 44. ***{glory}* Heb. *brightness.*

**V. 45. ***{The days of his youth hast thou shortened}* It is very ordinary among Historians and other Writers, to compare the different estates of a Kingdom, to a man's age, divided and distinguished by so many stages, or periods, as childhood, youth, manhood, and the like. Florus among the rest, but imitating others before him, *Si quis ergo populum Romanum, quasi homines consideret, totamque ejus aetatem percenſeat,* &c. The meaning therefore is, that David's Kingdom, before it had attained to its maturity: or rather whilst it was in its vigor, was cropped, and began to decay.

**V. 46. ***{How long, Lord}* How long, Lord, will you hide thyself? for ever?

*{How long, Lord wilt thou hide thyself for ever? shall thy wrath burn like fire?}* Psal. 79.5.

**V. 47. ***{Remember how short my time is}* The same Author, against whose censure we excepted upon Psal. 88.10. *Wilt thou shew wonders to the dead:* (a place very like unto this:) meets us here again with the same censure in substance of matter, though in different words. And he goes, here too, upon the same ground, because such complaints seem not to proceed from one that believed the immortality of the soul, and another world after this. What I have said there in defense, may serve here. Neither is that objection much more considerable, as I conceive, that the Psalmist seems to prescribe God a time, because he presses so earnestly for a speedy deliverance. Of such earnestness, see upon Psalm 70.1. *Make haste to helpe me:* and verse 5. *Make haste unto me, O God.* But there is more in these words, to be excepted against (though it is not, that I find,) and in my judgment, not so easily to be answered. For does not the Psalmist seem plainly to charge God, that *he hath made all men* (or *created,* as it is in the Original,) *vaine?* that is, in effect miserable. Indeed the vanity of man in this kind, even of the greatest and wealthiest of men, is so conspicuous, that wisest heathens have not only acknowledged it, and by reasons, and several instances at large demonstrated it: but also professed themselves not a little posed at it, how it should come to pass, that he for whom all other things seem to have been made, and through the advantage of reason, of nearer affinity to God; should of all creatures, if all things well considered, be the most wretched and miserable. No wonder, that they should wonder, who knew nothing (or if any thing, but very obscurely) either of Adam's sin, or God's curse upon it. But how shall we excuse any man, that has known the Story of the creation, for so speaking; not of God only; but even to God himself? The Story indeed of man's creation does not tell us plainly, that Adam was created immortal: neither is any man bound precisely so to believe. By his natural constitution, being flesh and blood, he was capable of death: his body compounded of several elements, was liable to dissolution, or resolution of these elements, into their first condition: but by eating of the tree of life, planted in Paradise for that purpose, and by a particular providence of God, he should have lived in a constant temper of all humors, not liable to any decay, or defects of nature: and after a period of time appointed by God, from a terrestrial and temporary Paradise, translated without death, into an eternal and heavenly. This had been his; and this the happiness of all that had come from him, had he continued in his innocency. But of Adam's estate in Paradise, and what was intended by God about him, and his posterity, if they had not lapsed, there be many questions among the Learned: we bind no man to believe anything, in the business, more than is clearly revealed by the word of God. This is clear and undoubtable, that *death entred into the world by sin,* Rom. 5.12. and 6.23. and with death, all other misery, sorrow, sickness, penury, strife, and the like. They were no less than anathematized by the ancient Church, whoever maintained that Adam should have died, although he had not sinned. *Quicunque dicit Adam primum hominem, mortalem factum,* &c. Gennadius *De Eccles. Dogmat.* cap. 33. out of several Councils. Neither did man only become subject to *vanity,* through sin, but all creatures also (sublunary) for the sin of man, Rom. 8.20, 21. What then shall we say, for the Psalmist here? That this is spoken by him through *passionum vehementia: and doloris intemperie: and vitioso excessu:* as some here, though upon another occasion? Or if that be too rough, and rash, concerning such holy men, and such as we conceive to have been inspired: may we say then, that God, in some sense, though remote, and improper; may be said to have created man to vanity, because he created him mutable, and left him to the liberty of his own will: liable to the temptation of the Devil, who indeed was the immediate instrumental cause of all the evil that has happened unto man. This God, it is true, might have prevented, had he been pleased, and had it been best so: many ways: which because he did not, according to the rules and maxims of men (to which nevertheless we know God is not tied: neither is it the same reason:) he may be said in some sense, to have been the cause: by them especially, who groaning under the burden of some great calamity, speak it rather to exaggerate their own present misery, as thinking they have just cause to wish they had never been born, or created; in general: than to contest with God, positively and dogmatically; that they were created so and so; or to charge Him with any kind of injustice, or want of goodness for it.

*{wherefore hast thou made all men vain?}* Or, *in what vanity thou hast made all men.*

**V. 48. ***{see death}* Heb. 11.5. Or, *suffer death,* *Die.*

**V. 49. ***{swarest unto David in thy truth?}* 2 Sam. 7.15.

**V. 50. ***{how I do bear in my bosom the reproach of all the mighty people}* Which I take exceedingly to heart, Psal. 69.9.

**V. 51. ***{Wherewith thine enemies have reproached}* So he calls them that persecute the Church.

*{steps of thine anointed}* They not only ruin his Kingdom, but also revile his actions. Others with the Chaldee paraphrast, and divers of the Rabbins, by *footsteps,* understand the manifestation of the expected Messiah; as if he meant; our enemies, that is long talked among themselves of a promised Deliverer, (as Luke 1.71. & 74. in Zacharias his hymn,) our enemies now upbraided them, that in this their great necessity, no such appeared.

**V. 52. ***{Blessed be the Lord for evermore}* The voice of faith and joy, in expectation of deliverance. We must bless God as well in our adversities, as prosperities; as Job did, *The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord,* 1.21.

# PSALM XC

## TITLE

*{A prayer of Moses}* Or, *a prayer, being a Psalm of Moses*.

*{the man of God}* Deut. 33.1. Thus the Scripture uses to call the Prophets.

**Verse 1. ***{Thou hast been our dwelling place}* You have been as an house and defense unto us in all our troubles and travels now these four hundred years.

*{in all generations}* Heb. *in generation and generation.*

**V. 2. ***{from everlasting to everlasting}* Or, *from eternity to eternity.*

**V. 3. ***{thou turnest man to destruction}* Moses, by lamenting the frailty and shortness of man's life, moves God to pity.

*{Return, ye children of men}* To your Original, Gen. 3.19. Eccl. 12.7. Or, by way of irony, *Live again if you can.*

**V. 4. ***{For a thousand years in thy sight}* That is, in regard of eternity: or, compared to eternity. For that is it that the Psalmist aims at, to set out the shortness and inconsiderableness of man's life, even the longest life of man, by comparing it with the infiniteness of eternity. Which is the way that many ancient Philosophers, of both languages, have gone; being in very deed the most solid and effectual to that end, that can be used or imagined. This, understood, makes the coherence of the Psalmist his words which otherwise does not appear. For further illustration, I shall put some of their words here: *If thou shalt look* (says one of them) *behind what an infinite chaos of time doth present itself unto thee; and as infinite a chaos, if thou shalt look forward. In that which is so infinite, what difference can there be between that which liveth but three days, and him which liveth three ages?* Marcus Aurelius, *Meditations* 4.50: *Ἐν δὴ τούτῳ, τί διαφέρει ὁ τριήμερος τοῦ τριγερηνίου;* *Minorem portionem aetas nostra quam puncti habet, si omni tempori comparetur:* (that is, to eternity) *Quid ergo interest id extendere, cujus quantumcunque fuerit incrementum, non multum aberit a nihilo?* So another.

*{For a thousand years in thy sight are but as yesterday}* 2 Pet. 3.8. Though man think his life long, which is indeed most short, though it were a thousand years, yet in God's sight it is as nothing, Psal. 39.5. and as the watch, that lasts but three hours, Mark 13.35.

*{when it is past}* Or, *when he hath passed them.* He adds this; because men think of the shortness of their time when it is past, rather than when it is passing.

**V. 5. ***{as with a flood}* Besides the general necessity of dying; you sendest floods of particular judgments that suddenly take men away. So it was with the Israelites in the wilderness, verse 7, &c. Isa. 8.7, 8. Or, *as a flood that goes swiftly away, and returns not again.*

*{grass which groweth up}* Psal. 103.15. Isa. 40.6. See upon Psal. 92.7. *When the wicked.*

*{groweth up}* Or, *is changed.*

**V. 7. ***{For we are consumed by thine anger}* You callest us by your rods, to consider the shortness of our lives, and for our sins you abridges our days.

**V. 8. ***{Thou hast set our iniquities before thee}* As a Judge that proceeds according to evidence, Psal. 50.21.

*{our secret sins in the light of thy countenance}* Not known to ourselves: or, *sins of our youth*.

**V. 9. ***{passed away}* Heb. *turned away.*

*{we spend our years as a tale that is told}* Our days are not only miserable, but also short; for our sins provoke you to cut us off.

*{as a tale that is told}* Or, *as a meditation*.

**V. 10. ***{The days of our years are threescore years and ten}* Heb. *As for the days of our years, in them are seventy years*. Meaning, according to the common state of life.

*{and if by reason of strength they be fourscore years, yet is their strength labour}* Or, *or by reason of strength fourscore; but the pride (or the prime) of them is but labor*.

*{if by reason of strength they be fourscore years}* Heb. *strengths*: that is, extraordinary strength.

*{yet is their strength labour and sorrow}* Heb. *pride*: that is, their strength, pleasure, beauty, or whatsoever men are proud of in the best of their days. Or, *the prime of their days*.

*{we fly away}* Flee away speedily, like a bird.

**V. 11. ***{even according to thy fear, so is thy wrath}* There are many expositions of these words: so many, as a man may easily conceive there is not any yet satisfactory. By some these are most exact. I find these three, among many, accounted chiefest. Some therefore translate first, *Who knoweth secundum timorem tui, iram tuam?* that is, say they, Who is sensible of your wrath, as to fear you as you oughtest to be feared? Secondly, *according to thy fear, so is thy wrath;* that is, They feel the effects of your wrath most, that most fear you. For *judgement beginneth at the house of God,* 1 Pet. 4.17. and, *whom God loveth, he chasteneth,* Heb. 12.6. Thirdly, others, They only are sensible of your wrath, (to observe it, that is, and to make a right use of it) who religiously fear you. Others, either they take no notice of it; ascribing all their crosses to chance, or destiny: the Stars and Constellations, &c. Or, haply, if they do, it is to fret and murmur; or to curse and blaspheme: but not to humble themselves and submit. These I find reckoned as the chiefest. And if any can content himself with any of these, I am not against it. I could object somewhat against every one: but instead of that, I will briefly set down what I myself think is the true meaning.

Wicked men in all ages, (and that such have been of old among the Hebrews, we know by their sects) that have labored to promote Atheism, persuading men, that either there is no God at all; or if a God, yet such a one, as does not intermeddle with the affairs of men; among other specious arguments that they have used to that purpose, this (as may appear by the writings of ancient Philosophers, Epicureans and others) has been one of the chiefest, that God being a simple Spirit, is not liable to any passions, or affections; and therefore cannot be angry. Upon this they inferred, that the fear of God was a groundless thing: a mere bugbear, to keep fools or children in awe. I could show what use some reputed Christians have made, and make to this day of this argument, to overthrow some fundamentals of our Christian faith. But I will not digress so far. Only, because we would not have such an argument go without any answer at all, I will make use of the words of an ancient Heathen (whether his meaning were as right as his words, I will not inquire; it does not much concern us) upon this very occasion: in answer to an objection that was made concerning perjury, that since it was certainly known (that is the objection) that the gods could not be angry, men need not, especially upon such an extraordinary occasion, as to save a life or so, stand so much upon breach of an oath: *Quod affirmaris,* says he, *quasi Deo praesente promiseris, id retendum est. Jam enim non ad iram Deorum, quae nulla est; sed ad fidem pertinet.* So say we, God properly cannot be angry; we grant; but he is just and true.

Either we must not speak of God at all, or we must speak of him in terms taken from the similitude of man. The wrath of man, what, and how it uses to work, we know: *The wrath,* (or fear; he uses both words) *of a King,* (says Solomon,) *is as the roaring of a Lion. Whoso provoketh him to anger, sinneth against his own soul,* Prov. 19.12. & 20.2. When therefore we ascribe wrath, or fury unto God, we intend by these words, the certainty of Divine Judgments, which in his justice he does inflict upon sinners. Severe execution among men, uses to proceed from great wrath, and eager revenge; the judgments of God, infinitely more dreadful, that they may be understood to be what they are, (though that will not serve neither, to make us understand them, as indeed they are) we use the same terms. Though the speech be not proper of itself, yet it is most true, as it is intended. As if we call a crafty man, a Fox; and an innocent harmless man, a Lamb: (which are similitudes taken from the nature of beasts) if both the one and the other be so and so indeed; we speak truly, though not properly.

The Psalmist therefore when he says, that *according to his fear, so is the wrath of God:* his meaning is, as I apprehend him, in opposition to them who maintained, that God need not be feared, *quia nec potest irasci, nec nocere;* (as the forementioned heathen author speaks) that is, in the Prophet Zephaniah his expression, *That the Lord will do no good, neither will he do any evil,* Zeph. 1.12. His meaning, I say is, That the fear of God is no vain thing: that he has dreadful judgments in store, against them that fear him not, nor dread his judgments. Thus I understand him; and I am sure this Interpretation is as literal, as any other: but let others judge. See also upon Psal. 66.5. *he is terrible.*

**V. 12. ***{So teach us to number our days, that we may apply our hearts to wisdom, &c.}* To wisdom, that is, to piety and godliness. A man may number his days, that is, often consider of the shortness and uncertainty of his life, and yet make a quite contrary use of it. There is not a more ordinary motive (in the writings of ancient heathens, I am sure,) to worldly mirth and joviality, than this, because our life is short. *Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we shall die,* 1 Cor. 15.32. But that this is not the wisdom here intended, may appear by comparing with Eccles. 7.2, 3, 4. *It is better to go to the house of mourning, than to go to the house of feasting,* &c. *Sorrow is better than laughter:* &c. *The heart of the wise is in the house of mourning; but the heart of fools is in the house of mirth.*

*{apply our hearts}* Heb. *cause to come*.

**V. 13. ***{Return, O Lord, how long?}* How long will you be angry with your people?

*{let it repent thee concerning thy servants}* Cease to afflict us, as a man repenting alters his course, Deut. 32.36.

**V. 15. ***{wherein thou hast afflicted us}* In Egypt, and in the wilderness.

*{seen}* Or, *felt*, Psal. 89.48.

**V. 16. ***{Let thy work appear unto thy servants}* Your mercy, which is your chiefest work.

*{and thy glory unto their children}* Seeing God's promises appertained as well to their posterity as to them, Moses prays for their children.

**V. 17. ***{let the beauty of the Lord our God be upon us}* Meaning, that it was obscured, when he ceased to do good to his Church.

# PSALM XCI

**Verse 1. ***{In the secret place of the most High}* He that makes God his defense and trust, shall perceive his protection to be a most sure safeguard, Psal. 5.12.

*{secret place}* Psal. 32.7.

*{abide}* Heb. *lodge*.

**V. 2. ***{I will say of the Lord}* He prays to the Lord, out of confidence of the aforesaid protection.

**V. 3. ***{Surely he shall deliver thee}* Or, *For he will deliver you*.

*{he shall deliver thee}* Having practiced in the second verse what he taught in the first, now he teaches others to do the like, Psal. 32.8. & 34.11.

*{from the snare of the fowler}* Psal. 124.7. God will deliver you from secret and open dangers.

**V. 4. ***{with his feathers}* As fowls hover over their young with their wings, Deut. 32.11. & 17.8.

*{his truth shall be thy shield and buckler}* That is, his faithful keeping of his promise to help you in your necessity.

**V. 5. ***{Thou shalt not be afraid for the terror by night, &c.}* By these several expressions, verse 5, 6, 7, &c. is only intended, emphatically to set out the power of God, and the security of the godly man, resting upon this, that though his dangers be never so great, or his case, in appearance, never so desperate, yet God is able, if he see it convenient, for his glory, and the good of the sufferer, to deliver him: so that *all things shall work together for good, to them that love God,* Rom. 8.28. So that *in all these things we are more than conquerors,* ver. 37. Not that we are always actually delivered, out of every particular danger, or grievance there mentioned, (*tribulation, distress, persecution, famine, nakedness, sword,*) verse 35. as appears by verse 36. *For thy sake are we killed all the day long,* &c. but because all will turn (such is our confidence in God) to our greater good; and the more we suffer, the greater shall our reward and our glory be.

Much like to this of the Psalmist, and to the same purpose is the expression of Isaiah. *When thou passest through the waters, I will be with thee; and through the rivers, they shall not overflow thee: When thou walkest through the fire, thou shalt not be burnt; neither shall the flame kindle upon thee,* Isa. 43.2. So also Habakkuk, 3.17, 18. *Although the fig-tree shall not blossom,* &c. and Job 5.19, 20. &c. And therefore here is no ground, if the words be rightly understood, for any man absolutely to presume, or conclude, that he shall actually be delivered out of any particular danger: much less upon such a presumption, willfully to run into dangers. If such figures, the ornament of all language; such rhetorical, emphatical amplifications be allowed to human Writers, and well enough understood in ordinary language; why not to holy Writers as well, who had to do with men, as well as others; whose end also was, to use such expressions, as might affect and move? That human Writers have said as much of the security of good and godly men, I shall need to go no further than Horace his Ode, *Integer vitae scelerisque purus,* &c. Most dangerous then, and erroneous is the inference of some men, yea of some Expositors, here upon these words of the Psalmist, that no godly man can suffer by the plague, or pestilence: nor is old Lactantius his assertion much sounder, *Non potest ergo fieri, quin hominem justum inter discrimina tempestatum,* &c. that no just man can perish by war, or by tempest; *Instit.* l. 5. c. 18. Most Interpreters conclude here, that the godly are preserved in times of public calamities: which, in a right sense may be true: but withal they should have added, that all godly men are not exempted, at such times; to prevent rash judgments. See more upon Psal. 107.6. *And he delivered them,* &c.

**V. 8. ***{Only with thine eyes shalt thou behold}* You shall be but a looker on, not a feeler of those miseries.

**V. 11. ***{he shall give his Angels charge over thee}* See upon Psal. 34.7. *The Angel of the Lord.* These words were applied to Christ by the Devil, when he tempted him, Matt. 4.6. And well he might do it. For if they belong to all the faithful in general; (as Interpreters agree) much more eminently to the Head and Patron of the faithful; *the author and finisher of our faith,* Heb. 12.2. But it is observed by some Interpreters, that the Devil left out of purpose part of the verse, which was not to his purpose; *to keep thee in thy ways;* that is, say they, in the works of your calling. So that if a man employ himself out of his calling, though religious otherwise, then is he not within the compass of these promises.

**V. 13. ***{adder}* Or, *asp*.

*{the young lion and the dragon shalt thou trample under feet}* So far from hurting you, that you shall tread upon them, Rom. 16.20.

**V. 14. ***{Because he hath set his love upon me}* To assure the faithful of God's protection, he brings in God to confirm the same.

*{I will set him on high}* Above all dangers, or fear of them, Psal. 61.2.

**V. 15. ***{Call upon me}* Psal. 50.15.

**V. 16. ***{With long life will I satisfy him}* And not cut him off among others, ver. 7. But all this must be understood conditionally, if God see it fitting; for a good man may die by plague or war, 2 Chron. 35.23.

*{long life}* Heb. *length of days*.

# PSALM XCII

## TITLE

*{A Psalm or Song}* See Notes on the title of Psal. 30.

*{for the sabbath day}* To be sung on the sabbath; and it teaches us, That the right use of the sabbath stands in praising God, and meditation on his works, and not in ceasing from work only, ver. 4.

**Verse 1. ***{Good thing to give thanks unto the Lord, and to sing praises}* Psal. 147.1.

**V. 2. ***{in the morning}* For God's mercy, and fidelity in his promises, toward his, binds them to praise him continually both day and night, Psal. 55.17 and 119.62, 164.

*{every night}* Heb. *in the nights*.

**V. 3. ***{upon the harp with a solemn sound}* Or, *upon the solemn sound with the harp*.

*{a solemn sound}* Heb. *Higgaion*.

**V. 4. ***{hast made me glad through thy work}* He shows, that on the sabbath we should meditate on God's works.

**V. 5. ***{O Lord, how great are thy works}* See upon Psal. 28.5. *because they regard not the works of the Lord,* &c. and upon Psal. 41.1. *Blessed is he that wisely considereth concerning the poor,* &c.

*{thoughts are very deep}* Psal. 40.5. Rom. 11.33.

**V. 6. ***{A brutish man knoweth not}* See upon Psal. 73.22. *I was as a beast before thee;* and Psal. 41.1. *Blessed is he that considereth,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{When the wicked spring as the grass}* Psal. 37.2. The temporal prosperity of any man in general, be he good or bad, is often in the Scripture compared to flourishing grass: as particularly, Psal. 90.5, 6. *in the morning, they are like grass,* &c. and elsewhere. Nevertheless it is here, and elsewhere more particularly objected to the ungodly, because all their happiness ends with their life: it is this present world only that they trust to: in very truth therefore, most wretched they, whose happiness (not to speak of what they must suffer after death) is so fickle and transitory. As for them who look for a better after this, how long they enjoy this mortal life, or what share they have of temporal goods whilst they live, and for how long; is not, in very truth, (but that men naturally desire to live: and so account it a blessing, as natural men, to live and prosper, for a while, if it may be;) nor can be much material unto them.

*{it is that they shall be destroyed}* Since it is *a righteous thing with God to recompence tribulation,* &c. 2 Thess. 1.6, 7, 8, 9. and, *Remember that thou in thy life time receivedst thy good things,* &c. Luke 16.25. See also Wisd. 5.4, 5, 6, &c. *then fools accounted his life madness,* &c.

**V. 8. ***{But thou, O Lord, art most high}* See upon Psal. 7.7. *for their sakes therefore return thou on high.*

**V. 10. ***{But my horn shalt thou exalt}* You will strengthen me with all power, and bless me with all felicity.

*{horn shalt thou exalt}* Psal. 75.10.

**V. 11. ***{Mine eye also shall see my desire on mine enemies}* Psal. 54.7. and 112.8.

**V. 12. ***{The righteous shall flourish like the palm tree}* Hos. 14.5. Though the faithful seem to wither, and be cut down by the wicked; yet they shall grow again, and flourish in the Church of God, as the Cedars do in Mount Lebanon.

**V. 13. ***{those that be planted in the house of the Lord, shall flourish in the courts of our God}* Rather, as most other Interpreters, *Being planted in the house of the Lord, they shall flourish in the courts of our God;* which is much plainer. For *the house of the Lord,* and *the courts of God,* are but the same thing. The Temple of Jerusalem, and the Ark of it, were a type of Christ: who says of himself, *I am the vine, ye are the branches; he that abideth in me, and I in him, the same bringeth forth much fruit; for without me ye can do nothing,* John 15.5.

**V. 14. ***{They shall still bring forth fruit in old age}* See upon next words.

*{flourishing}* Heb. *green*.

**V. 15. ***{To shew that the Lord is upright}* Although this duty, to set forth the praises of God, his justice, his mercy, and the like; and to stand in defense of him, to the utmost of what ability we have, against all atheistical gainsayers and scoffers, be a duty that lies upon the young, as well as old; yet the performance of it does become old men best, because of their authority, and long experience in worldly affairs; in which respect they may be accounted more able and vigorous (*fat and flourishing,* in the Psalmist's metaphorical expression) than young men.

*{uprightness in him}* Rom. 9.14.

# PSALM XCIII

**Verse 1. ***{The Lord reigneth}* Psal. 96.10. and 97.1. and 99.1. As God by his power and wisdom has made, and governs the world: so must they be our defense against all enemies and dangers.

*{clothed with majesty}* Psal. 104.1.

*{wherewith he hath girded himself}* Ready with his Almighty Power to do his work, Isa. 8.9. Luke 12.35. That is, to defend his Church.

**V. 2. ***{Thy throne is established}* Whereon you sittest, and governest the world.

*{of old}* Heb. *from then*, Isa. 48.3. That is, from the time that the world was founded.

**V. 3. ***{The floods have lifted up, O Lord}* Psal. 65.7.

**V. 4. ***{The Lord on high is mightier than the noise of many waters}* He means, that God's power appears in ruling the furious waters: or, that God's power is greater than the power of the Sea. Some think that by these expressions, troubles and commotions of estates, in general: or more particularly, persecutions of the Church, are figuratively intended. It is likely the Psalmist might have a respect unto both, the great power and providence of Almighty God, manifestly appearing in both.

**V. 5. ***{Thy testimonies are very sure}* Besides God's power and wisdom, in creating, and governing; his great mercy also appears, in that he has given his people his word and covenant.

*{holiness becometh thine house, O Lord, for ever}* Either holiness, passively; that is, inviolableness, stability, perpetuity, grounded upon the sureness, and faithfulness of God's testimonies, by which some understand promises; or, holiness, actively; that is, all manner of reverence in outward performances and duties; but especially, a holy life and conversation becomes them that profess themselves to be servants to so great and glorious a Majesty.

*{for ever}* Heb. *to length of days*.

# PSALM XCIV

**Verse 1. ***{God to whom vengeance}* Heb. *God of revenges*.

*{to whom vengeance belongeth}* Deut. 32.35. Nah. 1.2. Whose office it is to take vengeance on the wicked.

*{shew thyself}* Heb. *shine forth*. Psal. 50.2. & 80.1.

*{shew thyself}* Show by effect, that you are judge of the world, to punish the wicked.

**V. 2. ***{Lift up thyself, thou Judge of the earth}* Psal. 7.6, 7. See more there.

**V. 3. ***{how long shall the wicked triumph?}* That is, brag of their cruelty and oppression; or, esteem themselves above others.

**V. 4. ***{speak hard things}* Psal. 31.18.

**V. 5. ***{They break in pieces thy people}* Seeing the Church was then so sore oppressed, it ought not to seem strange unto us, if we see it so now; and therefore we must call to God, to take our cause in hand.

**V. 7. ***{Yet they say, The Lord shall not see; neither shall the God of Jacob regard it}* Psal. 10.11, 13.

*{Yet they say}* He shows, that they are desperate in malice, forasmuch as they feared not God, but gave themselves wholly to do wickedly.

**V. 8. ***{ye brutish among the people}* That is, most brutish of all the people, Psal. 92.6.

**V. 9. ***{He that planted the ear, shall he not hear?}* Exod. 4.11. Prov. 20.12. He shows, that it is impossible but God should hear, see, and understand their wickedness.

**V. 10. ***{He that chastiseth the heathen, shall not he correct?}* If God punish the very heathen for their sins, it is mere folly for any of God's people, to think that God will spare them. For *judgement beginneth at the house of God,* 1 Pet. 4.17. Many satisfy themselves with this exposition; which nevertheless, if well considered is quite besides the drift and purpose: this, whatever it be, being opposed (as appears by the context) to the conscience and security of the wicked, Epicures, and Atheists, who because they did not believe any God, or that God did intermeddle with the affairs of this world, made no conscience at all, how they oppressed the godly and religious. The words therefore, which will be as proper to the Original, as this here, being translated, *He that teacheth* (or instructs) *the heathen,* (Heb. גוים the nations; that is, all the world, or all mankind) *shall not he reason, or argue?* That is, He that inspired understanding to men in general, according to that in Job; *But there is a spirit in man; and the inspiration of the Almighty giveth them understanding,* 32.8. Nay, *He that teacheth us* (Heb. מלפנו as children are taught in Schools, from their first rudiments, by little and little; as the word imports properly) *more than the beasts of the earth, and maketh us wiser than the fowls of heaven,* 35.11. That is, in effect, He that has made us rational creatures; that has endued us, and all the men of the world, with an intellectual faculty; *shall he not consider, and understand*? Or, *shall he be less rational than we*?

The argument to prove, against Atheists and Epicures, that there must needs be an original rational substance, which is as a principle of reason to others, or from which that reason, which is in other creatures, is derived; that is, in effect a God infinitely wise, &c. though vulgarly proposed, is very solid, and philosophical, as may appear by the more large disputations of ancient Philosophers to that purpose. But it will be better understood, if we produce some of their words; “As that which in me is earthly, (says one of them) I have from some common earth; and that which is moist from some other element is imparted: as my breath and life has its proper fountain; and that likewise which is dry and fiery in me: (for there is nothing which does not proceed from something: as also there is nothing, that can be reduced into mere nothing) So also οὕτω δὴ καὶ τὸ νοερὸν ἥκει ποθέν; must there be some first beginning, (or, fountain) from whence proceeded my understanding; (or, that which is rational in me.”

**V. 11. ***{The Lord knoweth the thoughts of man}* 1 Cor. 3.20. An answer to the question in the former verse; yea, the Lord knows men's vain thoughts.

**V. 12. ***{whom thou chastenest}* God has care over his, and chastens them for their welfare, that they should not perish for ever with the wicked, 1 Cor. 11.32. Heb. 12.5, 10.

**V. 15. ***{But judgment shall return unto righteousness}* God will restore a right government, that the good may be rewarded, and bad men punished, and then the godly shall follow him cheerfully.

*{shall follow it}* Heb. *shall be after it*.

**V. 16. ***{Who will rise up for me against the evildoers?}* None will.

**V. 17. ***{Unless the Lord had been my help}* He complains of them which would not help him to resist the enemies; yet was assured that God's help would not fail.

*{almost}* Or, *quickly*.

*{my soul had almost dwelt in silence}* That is, in the grave, and so it might have been translated, which would have been plainer; as also, Psal. 115.17. *The dead praise not the Lord, neither any that go down into silence.* So the Latins commonly call the dead *silentes;* as, *Qui justa silentibus illic Reddit:* where to translate *the silent,* instead of *the dead,* would be very improper.

**V. 18. ***{My foot slippeth}* Psal. 38.16. When I thought there was no way but death.

**V. 19. ***{In the multitude of my thoughts}* In my trouble and distress, I ever found present comfort and help from you.

**V. 20. ***{Shall the throne of iniquity have fellowship with thee}* Will you favor, and bear out these tyrants that abuse their power to open oppression? It may also (as by some it is) be understood of wicked Judges, the word כסא signifying a Tribunal, as well as a Throne.

*{by a law}* Psal. 58.2. Isa. 10.1.

**V. 21. ***{gather themselves together against the soul of the righteous}* Psal. 12.

**V. 23. ***{and shall cut them off in their own wickedness}* Psal. 55.23. It is a great token of God's judgment, when the purpose of the wicked is broken; but a greater, when they perish by their own malicious plots.

# PSALM XCV

**Verse 1. ***{O come let us sing unto the Lord}* It appears to be David's Psalm, Heb. 4.7.

**V. 2. ***{come before his presence}* Heb. *prevent his face*.

*{with thanksgiving}* He shows, that God's service stands not in dead ceremonies, but chiefly in the sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving, 34.12.

**V. 3. ***{above all gods}* Psal. 86.8. & 96.4. Even the angels, (who in respect of men, are thought as gods, Psal. 8.5.) are nothing in his sight: much less the idols, which man's brain invents, Psal. 96.5.

**V. 4. ***{In his}* Heb. *In whose*.

*{the strength of the hills is his also}* Or, *the heights of the hills are his*. All things, even the most secret, and powerful, are governed by his providence.

**V. 5. ***{The sea is his}* Heb. *whose the sea is*.

*{and}* Or, *for*.

**V. 6. ***{let us worship and bow down and kneel}* Some learned Rabbins are of opinion, that David here by those three words, *to worship, bow down, and kneel,* does express three several gestures and postures of body, used then in adoration, and prayer. The first (here translated, *let us worship:* but more properly by others, to this sense, *prosteramur*) a casting of the whole body upon the ground, with stretched legs and feet level to the face: the second, a low bowing of the whole body: the third, kneeling. It is certain, that all these postures were used among the Jews, in their worship: whereof examples are frequent in all parts of it.

The first of these, which may seem strangest, now that men are grown much colder in their devotions, and gladly take hold (*quo jure, qua injuria;* as elsewhere is showed) of Christ's words, concerning spiritual worship, to favor their bodies: as it was very common among devout Jews, used by Christ himself, (Matt. 26.39. *and fell on his face:* and Mark 14.35. *and fell on the ground:*) so was it in great use among ancient Christians, as to any that are versed in their writings, must needs appear. But though this be true concerning these words and rites, in general; yet whether here true, to the intention of the Psalmist, may be doubted. For had he aimed at those three several postures, a man would think, according to the rules of Rhetoric, that he should rather have begun with kneeling or incurvation, as the least of the three; and so have proceeded to prostration, the lowest, and most abject kind of adoration. Except we shall make an inverted kind (and such there be to be found sometimes) of climax of it: not unlike Psal. 1. *walketh not: standeth not: sitteth not:* not sitting, or standing, being a less commendation, (in the intention of the Psalmist, according to most Expositors) than not walking.

But I will bind no man to this interpretation. For as certain it is, that the first word is often used for worship or adoration, in general; which as it was variously performed with either kneeling, bowing, or falling; so is that word, and the Greek προσκύνησις, (which does answer it) joined with any one of those three. However, though the Psalmist do not mention all postures, yet by those two that he mentions, he may comprehend any other kind, and so be understood by us (with most Expositors) as intending all kinds of adoration, mental, corporal: all kinds, and by all sorts of expressions used among men: as who is the object, (to wit, God: *the Lord our maker*) the only object of all true adoration; due unto him from all men, all creatures of the world. So Mark, *with all thy heart, and with all thy soul,* &c. Words are multiplied upon the same occasion, to let us know, that we owe much more, than all that we can perform (do we what in us lies) can come unto.

**V. 7. ***{the people of his pasture}* Psal. 100.3.

*{and the sheep of his hand}* That is, the flock whom he governs with his own hand, Psal. 77.20. & 100.3. and therefore ought to hearken to his voice.

*{to day if ye will hear his voice}* Heb. 3.7. & 4.7.

**V. 8. ***{Harden not your heart}* By the contemning of God's Word.

*{as in the provocation, and as in the day of temptation in the wilderness}* Exod. 17.2, 7. Num. 14.22, &c.

*{provocation}* Heb. *contention*.

**V. 9. ***{tempted me, proved me, and saw my work}* 1 Cor. 10.9. Psal. 78.40.

**V. 10. ***{It is a people that do err, &c.}* Num. 14.23.

*{that do err in their heart}* They were without judgment & reason.

**V. 11. ***{that they should not enter into my rest}* Heb. *if they enter into my rest.* That is, into the land of Canaan, where he promised they should find rest, which the Apostle shows to be a type of our eternal rest to come, Heb. 4.8, 9.

# PSALM XCVI

**Verse 1. ***{O Sing unto the Lord a new song}* This whole Psalm, and part of the 105, from ver. 1. to ver. 15. inclusively; (*Saying, Touch not mine anointed, and do my Prophets no harm*) with some small alterations, and some, either contractions, (for it may be either, according as we shall suppose this or that to have been the first and original Copy) or additions, are also to be found (this from v. 23. to v. 33. inclusively; and that part of the 105 Psalm, from v. 9 to v. 12. inclusively) 1 Chron. 16. Of such differences in general, see upon Psal. 18. *I will love thee,* &c. and Psal. 53. *The fool hath said in his heart,* &c.

But in collating of these two Psalms with that Chapter, there be two things more to be observed. The first is, that what in Chronicles is exhibited as one single Psalm; in this Book it makes two, (if we may call two, one whole, and part of another) and these two, at that distance one of another, that no suspicion can be of any such coherence, either of words or matter, as we find in some other Psalms, that follow one another: as for example, between the 105 & 106. Besides, that which is first here, is last there; and that which is last here, is there first. It is not impossible, that David himself might afterwards divide, and contrive into a new form, what himself had first joined, or join afterwards, what he had first divided. Neither would it be a difficult matter to find out a coherence of sense, if we make but one Psalm of it.

However, as it does not appear by the Chronicles, that what is there put together, in order of context, was intended for different Psalms: So neither is it certain, that it was intended but for one. We read there, indeed, in our English Translation, *That on that day David delivered first this Psalm to thank the Lord,* &c. *this Psalm;* as of one: but that being but the supplement of the Translator, it may as well be supplied, *these Psalms,* as *this Psalm:* as indeed it is by some, who find three several Psalms in that Chapter: the first, from v. 8. to 23; the second from 23. to 34. the third from 34. to 37. ending in those words, *Amen, and praise the Lord.* So that the matter being uncertain, we may spare our labor of further reasoning about it.

However, this seems to be clear by the text (a second thing to be observed) that this Psalm, or Psalms, (whether two or three, or but one there, which here makes one, and part of another) were of the first, if not the very first, that ever David published: And then what reason can we give, why so many should go before, which certainly were of a later date? But whether the Psalms were digested by any other certain observations, is not certain: Certain it is, that the order of time (which is considerable in the argument of divers Psalms) was not observed. See also upon Psal. 1. and Psal. 72. last words.

I said, it seemed clear by the Text: I must add; as it is ordinarily translated. But I find a different translation of those words.
