Not to mention the vulgar Latin, which turns it quite another way; learned Grotius translates the whole verse thus; *Hoc autem imprimis eo tempore David constituit, ut per Asaph & collegas ejus laudaretur Deus;* that is, *And David at that time took especial care, that God might be praised by Asaph, and his colleagues.* Whose opinion also is, that David having made these Psalms before, as they are here represented in the book of Psalms, did so join and put them together afterwards, upon that occasion of the Ark. This Psalm then, which few Interpreters take notice of, has a double relation. For as it stands here alone, by itself, it is a mere prophecy of the conversion of the Gentiles to the faith, who therefore are here called upon to praise God for it; but as it is represented in the Chronicles as part of a longer Psalm, or joined with others, whether one, or more, it had a further and more immediate use, being then sung at and upon the bringing in of the Ark by David into Jerusalem.

**V. 4. ***{For the Lord is great}* Seeing he will reveal his greatness to all Nations, contrary to their own expectation, they ought all to worship him, contrary to their own imaginations, and only as he has appointed.

*{to be praised}* Psal. 18.3.

*{above all gods}* Psal. 95.3.

**V. 5. ***{For all the gods of the nations are idols}* Jer. 10.11.

*{but the Lord made the heavens}* Then the Idols, or whatsoever made not the heavens, are not God.

**V. 6. ***{Honour and majesty are before him}* God cannot be known but by the tokens of his honor and majesty, which are seen principally in his Sanctuary.

**V. 7. ***{Give unto the Lord, O ye kindreds of the people, give unto the Lord glory and strength}* Psal. 29.1, 2.

*{give unto the Lord glory and strength}* As by experience you see that it is only due to him.

**V. 8. ***{due unto his name}* Heb. *of his name*.

*{bring an offering}* By presenting yourselves before God, declare, that you worship him only, Psal. 68.29. Isa. 16.1.

**V. 9. ***{in the beauty of holiness}* Or, *in the glorious Sanctuary.* See upon Psal. 110.3. *The beauty of holiness.*

**V. 10. ***{Say among the heathen}* He prophesies, that the Gentiles shall be partakers, with the Jews, of God's promise, Rom. 15.9, &c.

*{the Lord reigneth}* Psal. 93.1. & 97.1.

*{righteously}* Heb. *in righteousnesses*.

*{Say among the heathen, that the Lord reigneth}* 1 Chron. 16. There is a transposition of these words; which follow there, in this order, *Let the heavens be glad, and let the earth rejoice; and let men say among the nations, The Lord reigneth. Let the sea roar,* &c. It is very likely that it was made by David himself. See before.

*{that the Lord reigneth}* Much ado there has been about this place; and some men, to this day, make great boast of it; thinking thereby to prove and evince against all gainsayers, the corruption of the Original Hebrew. Certain it is, that divers Ancients of great antiquity; as Justin Martyr, Tertullian, Cyprian, and some others, found it in their Translations, Κύριος ἐβασίλευσεν ἀπὸ ξύλου: *Dominus regnavit à ligno:* that is, *The Lord hath reigned by his cross:* who therefore suspected, that the enemies of Christ's cross, the Jews, had purposely, by omission of these words, which made against them, played the plagiarists. But the greater number of Ancients that have been since, neither found in their Copies, neither object in their Comments any such thing unto them: neither is it in either Greek or Latin Copies of these days. How therefore this happened, learned men have divers conjectures; which I do not think pertinent to this place.

It shall suffice, for further clearing of the Hebrew Text, to observe, that 1 Chron. 16. where this whole Psalm, as has been said before, stands upon record, v. 31. no such thing is to be found. I shall only add, what seems to me very probable concerning that gloss, or insertion, into the Text; and has not yet been said by any that I know of; that those words, v. 12, 13. *then shall all the trees* עצי which is the word of the gloss, and signifies a cross also) *of the wood rejoice: Before the Lord;* might be the occasion of it.

**V. 11. ***{Let the heavens rejoice, and let the earth be glad}* Psal. 96.11. 1 Chron. 16.31.

**V. 12. ***{then shall all the trees of the wood rejoice}* If the insensible creatures shall have cause to rejoice, when God appears, then much more we.

# PSALM XCVII

**Verse 1. ***{The Lord reigneth}* He shows, that where God reigns, there is all felicity and spiritual joy. See also upon ver. 7. *worship him,* &c.

*{let the multitude of Isles be glad thereof}* For the Gospel shall not only be preached in Judea, but through all countries, yea, all Islands also.

*{multitude of Isles}* Heb. *many, or great Isles, or countries*.

**V. 2. ***{Clouds and darkness are round about him}* He is thus described, to keep his enemies in fear, who contemn God.

*{righteousness and judgment are the habitation of his throne}* Psal. 89.14.

*{habitation}* Or, *establishment*.

**V. 3. ***{A fire goeth before him, and burneth up his enemies round about}* Psal. 50.3.

**V. 4. ***{the earth saw and trembled}* Psal. 18.7. & 77.18. & 104.32.

**V. 6. ***{The heavens declare his righteousness, and all the people see his glory}* Some by *heavens,* here, understand the Angels; as if he intended, that the Angels in heaven, and men, (or people) in earth, shall all concur to glorify God. Or, *The heavens,* &c. that is, God's just judgments, which by an extraordinary power he does execute upon the wicked from heaven, as it were: they declare, and make his righteousness to be acknowledged upon earth, and all men to glorify him. Or, both heaven and earth shall be witnesses of his justice, and righteous judgments. So Psal. 50.4. *He shall call to the heavens from above,* &c. *And the heavens shall declare his righteousness; for God himself is Judge.* See the notes there.

There may be another interpretation also, which I think in reference to that which immediately follows, *Confounded be all they that serve graven images,* &c. would be very proper to this place. It is this; That the Heavens, Sun and Moon, and all that goodly Fabric, plead the cause of God, against Atheists and Idolaters; in that his glory (especially consisting in the acknowledgment of his infiniteness and omnipotency; and in being the only object of our adoration and devotions) may be seen, or is there exposed, and set out to the sight of all that are not willfully blind; or upon some just judgment of God altogether bereaved of their right wits. See more upon Psal. 19.1. *The heavens declare the glory of God,* &c. and v. 3. *There is no speech nor language,* &c. See also Psal. 89.5. *And the heavens shall praise,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{Confounded be all they that serve graven images}* Exod. 20.4. Lev. 26.1. Deut. 5.8. He signifies, that God's judgments are in a readiness to destroy idolaters.

*{worship him all ye gods}* Heb. אלהים which Psal. 8.5. is translated *Angels:* *Thou hast made him little lower than the Angels;* which, if grounded upon the Apostle's authority, who allows of it, Heb. 2.7. I know no reason why the same authority should not be followed here also, since the same Apostle, Heb. 1.6. has it, *And let all the Angels of God worship him:* wherein he is generally supposed to have intended these words of the Psalmist. There is in the words an allusive opposition (well observed by some) between אלילים idols; and אלהים angels.

That this whole Psalm is principally intended of Christ his spiritual Kingdom and Dominion, appears by the Apostle's words, *And again when he bringeth in the first begotten into the world, he saith, And let all the Angels of God,* &c. *when he bringeth;* that is, as before upon Psal. 40.6. *mine ears hast thou opened:* has already been intimated: when the Psalmist describes the Son of God his coming into the world; where he speaks and prophesies of it. There is nothing indeed in the whole Psalm, but is very applicable that way: no part almost of the Psalms, but is. However, I do not conceive that this can necessarily be inferred (as some argue) from these first words, *The Lord reigneth;* as though it could not be meant of God, the Father, whose Kingdom, they say, was from everlasting, and did not then begin.

For according to the Scripture phrase, God is there said *to reign,* when the Justice of his administration (often clouded by the prosperity of the wicked in this world; whereof see upon Psal. 37. and Psal. 73, &c. at which times atheism does use to abound) by just judgments executed upon the wicked, and profane, and sacrilegious, is made conspicuous unto men. And besides, what would they make of Psal. 99. ver. 1? *The Lord reigneth — he sitteth between the Cherubims,* &c. Besides the propriety of that expression, *that sitteth between the Cherubims,* it clearly appears by the whole context, that God the Father, the only then known to the Israelites, was intended.

**V. 8. ***{The daughters of Judah rejoiced}* The villages of Judah, which are as it were the daughters of Jerusalem, the mother City, shall rejoice at the conversion of the Gentiles.

*{because of thy judgments, O Lord}* Whereby you destroyest your enemies, and enlargest your Church.

**V. 9. ***{above all gods}* Psal. 95.3.

**V. 10. ***{Ye that love the Lord, hate evil}* He requires two things of his children: the one, that they detest vice: the other, that they put their trust in God for their deliverance.

*{hate evil}* Psal. 34.14. Amos 5.15. Rom. 12.9.

**V. 11. ***{Light is sown for the righteous}* Psal. 112.4. The joy of the righteous is laid up for them, and will come forth with increase, though it appear not at the first.

*{and gladness for the upright in heart}* Psal. 64.10.

**V. 12. ***{Rejoice in the Lord, ye righteous}* Psal. 33.1.

*{at the remembrance}* Or, *to the memorial*.

# PSALM XCVIII

**Verse 1. ***{O Sing unto the Lord a new song, for he hath done marvellous things}* Psal. 96.1. Isa. 42.10.

*{a new song}* That is, some song newly made, in token of thankfulness for their wonderful deliverance, Psal. 96.1.

*{his holy arm hath gotten him the victory}* He preserves his Church miraculously, Isa. 59.16. & 63.5.

**V. 2. ***{The Lord hath made known his salvation}* Isa. 52.10. For the deliverance of his Church.

*{his righteousness hath he openly shewed}* Most Expositors understand this righteousness of Evangelical righteousness, through faith in Christ's merits, &c. upon this ground, because the subject of the whole Psalm (as generally conceived) is to set out the happiness and glory of Christ's Kingdom, and the conversion of the Nations unto him. Neither is it to be questioned, but that it was intended to that use by the Holy Ghost, by way of mystical application and interpretation. Which though to us Christians most considerable now, yet ought we not to neglect the immediate, literal, historical sense: of which, (besides that it is the ground of the mystical, and even so to be sought into, that we may know what we ground upon, and how far we may go, to keep ourselves within bounds;) good use is to be made upon several occasions, by good and warrantable consequences, and applications.

We say therefore, that some notable victory, or deliverance, having been the immediate occasion; by the word *righteousness,* we must here understand, literally, goodness and mercy: of which signification, see upon Psal. 71.2. *deliver me in thy righteousness.* And though it be granted, that Prophets sometimes speak of things future, by the Preterit, thereby the better to express their own confidence, (and to breed it in others) of a certain accomplishment: Yet here the immediate cause of these Preterits, *The Lord hath made known, hath showed,* &c. is because they related immediately to somewhat, which in very deed, was already past and done.

Now if any man shall ask, what that should be, (literally taken) that God should be said, *openly to have showed in the sight of the heathen:* as in this verse: or, *to have been seen by all the ends of the earth,* as in the next? first we answer, that we must allow sacred Poets and Orators Rhetorical amplifications, as well as others; if we allow them to be Poets and Orators: and so if it were some notable victory, or deliverance, such as we read of many in sacred records, the fame whereof could not at least, but spread to all neighbor Nations and Kingdoms on all sides; so much as is here, might well be said of it. Isa. 39.1, &c. and 2 Kings 20.12, &c. we read of Ambassadors sent of purpose by the King of Babylon, *to inquire of the wonder that was done in the land:* as is particularly expressed 2 Chron. 32.31. and that many such things happened, upon several occasions, no man can doubt, though no mention be made of it in writings of their time, now remaining. See also upon Psal. 138.4. *All the Kings of the earth.*

But besides, there might be a particular providence of God in it, that the Psalmist should light upon such expressions, as might serve his purpose, figuratively, by way of rhetorical amplification: and be applicable to Christ and his Kingdom, literally: taking literal here, not for the immediate sense, in the intention of the Psalmist: but for that which is most proper, and Grammatical to the words, opposed to Rhetorical.

*{openly shewed}* Or, *revealed*.

**V. 3. ***{He hath remembered his mercy}* God was moved by no other means to gather his Church of the Jews and Gentiles, but because he would perform his merciful promise.

**V. 6. ***{With trumpets and sound of cornet}* By this repetition, and earnest exhortation to give praises with instruments, and by stirring up the dumb creatures, he signifies, that the world is never able to praise God sufficiently for their spiritual and corporal deliverances.

**V. 7. ***{sea roar, and the fulness thereof}* Psal. 96.11.

**V. 9. ***{for he cometh to judge the earth}* Psal. 96.13.

# PSALM XCIX

**Verse 1. ***{Let the people tremble}* When God delivers his Church, all the enemies shall have cause to tremble.

*{between the Cherubims, let the earth be moved}* Exod. 25.22. Psal. 80.1.

*{be moved}* Heb. *stagger*.

**V. 2. ***{The Lord is great in Zion}* Which is the seat of his universal monarchy, Isa. 2.2, &c.

**V. 3. ***{Let them praise thy great and terrible Name}* Though the wicked rage against God, yet the godly must praise his Name, and mighty power.

*{for it is holy}* Or, *which is holy*.

**V. 4. ***{The King's strength also loveth judgment}* Or, *And, the King's strength, who loves judgment*. This King, meaning God, the King of his Church; (so called also Psalm 98.6. *make a joyful noise before the Lord, the King*) does mix his power with justice, contrary to the custom of tyrants, Job 36.5.

*{thou dost establish equity}* Heb. *equities*. That is, the most equal courses.

**V. 5. ***{worship at his footstool}* That is, before his Ark, 1 Chron. 28.2. where he promised to hear, when they worshipped him; as now he promises his spiritual presence, wheresoever his Church is assembled.

*{he is holy}* Or, *it is holy*.

**V. 6. ***{Moses and Aaron among his priests}* Moses is reckoned among the Priests; for he did the Priestly office, in consecrating Aaron and his sons. As also in his continual interceding for the people; which some conceive to be the principal aim here: which also seems by the following words to be confirmed. However, the word כהן, does also signify a principal officer or minister; as some would have it here also translated.

**V. 8. ***{though thou tookest vengeance of their inventions}* He heard their prayers, notwithstanding their infirmities; and corrected them for their sins, though they prayed unto him, Num. 20.12. Or, you did forgive the people upon Moses' prayer, though you did destroy some of them for their sins, Exod. 32.34, 35. & 32.32. Num. 14.20, 22. He might also allude to God's own expressions concerning himself, Exod. 20.5, 6. *visiting the iniquity,* &c. *and shewing mercy,* &c. as also Exod. 34.6, 7.

# PSALM C

## TITLE

*{praise}* Or, *thanksgiving*. Psal. 145.1.

**Verse 1. ***{Make a joyful noise unto the Lord}* He prophesies that God's benefit in calling the Gentiles, shall be so great, that they shall have wonderful occasion to praise his mercy, and rejoice, Psal. 95.1.

*{all ye lands}* Heb. *all the earth*.

**V. 3. ***{it is he that hath made us}* I know no reason, though some principal Expositors would not have it, but that the words may be understood *de communi creatione;* that is, of temporal life and being, as well as of spiritual. This indeed is of more consequence: but that as necessary to be known, and the more necessary perchance to be insisted upon; because secondary causes are more visible, and many through ignorance, apt enough to ascribe to bare nature, what they see daily by natural means to be done, and effected; and without such, never done. The Psalmist elsewhere does very exactly insist upon it, Psal. 139.13, 14, 15, &c. and has choice speculations about it. See also Psal. 119.73. *Thy hands,* &c.

But again, the words might be intended of the Jews, God's peculiar people; which God did choose and select from all other Nations, to be his peculiar, not to any peculiar worth of theirs, nor by any such cooperation of theirs, as that they could ascribe any part of it to themselves; to which purpose, some translate: *ipse effecit nos, (non autem nos ipsi) populum suum,* &c. making a Parenthesis of those words, *and not we ourselves.*

*{and not we ourselves}* Or, *and his we are.* This difference of sense, or expression (though both to the main matter may come to one) the one affirmative, and the other negative; proceeds from a different reading of but one letter, in a word that consists but of two: which according to different reading, may be either a Pronoun, *illi;* or a Negative, *non.* It is not worth the while to contend about such petty differences: and they do the Scripture great wrong, that would have the integrity and incorruption of it to consist in such niceties.

*{his people, and the sheep of his pasture}* Psal. 95.7.

**V. 4. ***{Enter into his gates with thanksgiving}* Though David does here express himself in words proper to the service then in use, yet so much from the words we may gather absolutely, (as Expositors of best account well observe) that our thanksgiving to God upon extraordinary occasions, should always be accompanied with some outward solemnity. See also upon Psal. 66.13. *I will go into thy house.*

**V. 5. ***{his mercy is everlasting}* He declares, that we ought never to be weary in praising God, seeing his mercies toward us last for ever, Psal. 136.1, &c.

*{to all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation*.

# PSALM CI

**Verse 1. ***{I will sing of mercy}* Of your mercy to me, and justice, when you destroyest mine adversaries, and settest me on the throne.

**V. 2. ***{O when wilt thou come unto me?}* When will you give me the Kingdom promised?

*{I will walk within my house with a perfect heart}* Till then I will guide mine own house well, and afterwards the Kingdom.

**V. 3. ***{I will set no wicked thing before mine eyes}* Either to desire it, or endeavor to accomplish it.

*{wicked thing}* Heb. *thing of Belial*.

*{I hate the work}* He shows, that Magistrates do not their duty except they be enemies to all vice, Psal. 97.10.

*{of them that turn aside}* From the law of God, Psalm 125.5.

**V. 4. ***{I will not know a wicked person}* I will not take notice of them: I will show them no grace nor favor, Psalm 1.6. Matt. 7.23.

**V. 5. ***{Whoso privily slandereth his neighbour, him will I cut off}* In promising to punish those vices, which are most pernicious in them that are about Kings, he declares by consequence, that he will punish all vice.

*{an high look, and a proud heart, will not I suffer}* Prov. 6.17, 18.

**V. 6. ***{Mine eyes shall be upon the faithful of the land}* He shows what is the true use of the sword, To punish the wicked, and to maintain the good, Rom. 13.4.

*{in a perfect way}* Or, *perfect in the way*.

**V. 7. ***{shall not tarry in my sight}* Heb. *shall not be established*.

**V. 8. ***{I will early destroy all the wicked of the Land}* Heb. *at mornings*. For then was judgment executed, Jer. 21.12. Meaning, every morning; or else, most early: That is, immediately after the act, lest it grow to further inconvenience.

# PSALM CII

## TITLE

*{of}* Or, *for*.

*{of the afflicted}* Whereby is signified, that albeit we be in never so great miseries, yet there is ever place left for prayer, Psalm 130.1. Jonah 2.1.

**Verse 1. ***{Let my cry come unto thee}* He declares, that in our prayer we must ever feel the want of that which we desire.

**V. 2. ***{Hide not thy face from me in the day when I am in trouble, incline thine ear unto me}* Psal. 27.9.

**V. 3. ***{For my days are consumed like smoke}* These excessive kinds of speech show how much the affliction of the Church ought to wound the hearts of the godly.

*{like smoke}* Or, as some read, *into smoke*.

*{and my bones are burnt as an hearth}* Job 30.30. Psal. 32.3.

**V. 4. ***{so}* Or, *because, or, while*.

*{so that I forget to eat my bread}* My sorrows were so great that I passed not for mine ordinary food.

**V. 5. ***{my bones cleave to my skin}* Job 19.20. Lam. 4.8.

*{skin}* Or, *flesh*.

**V. 6. ***{I am like a Pelican of the wilderness}* Ever mourning and solitary, casting out fearful cries, Isa. 34.11. & Zeph. 2.14. in both which places, though it be the same word, and certainly intended for the same thing, it is translated *bittern:* but Lev. 11.18. *pelican,* as it is here. It is generally conceived, (which is grounded upon the etymology of the word, קאת *Vomere,* that is, to vomit up) that it is the name of a bird in Latin called *platea,* or *platalea,* (Englished by some a *shovelard*) which as Tully, and out of him Pliny, testify, uses to swallow down shell fish as oysters, and the like; and keeping them in his stomach, till they open, casts them up again, and so eats them.

*{an owl of the desert}* Job 30.29.

**V. 7. ***{I watch}* My sorrows will not suffer me to sleep, Psalm 77.4.

*{as a sparrow alone}* That has lost his Mate: so are good men solitary, being out of the Church.

**V. 8. ***{they are mad against me, are sworn against me}* They have conspired against me by a joint oath. Or, as others, *per me jurant,* that is, *swear by me.* That is my name is a curse unto them: threatened and denounced as the utmost of temporal judgments, Lev. 26. So also Isa. 65.15. *And ye shall leave your name for a curse.* Heb. לשבועה for an oath, properly. Or, *they swear by me;* that is, I am the object of all their oaths and imprecations; such is their rage and malice against me. But that former both translation and exposition, grounded upon those places of Leviticus, and Isaiah, is the most approved, and, as I conceive, most warrantable.

**V. 9. ***{I have eaten ashes like bread}* Sitting in the ashes, he could not but eat some with his meat. See upon Psal. 42.3. *My tears.*

*{mingled my drink with weeping}* Psal. 80.5. Heb. *drinks*. Noting a continual act.

**V. 10. ***{Because of thine indignation}* He shows, that his afflictions did not so much move him, as God's displeasure.

*{thou hast lifted me up and cast me down}* As in wrestling, one takes the other up to give him the greater fall: so affliction after prosperity is the more dreadful, Psalm 73.18.

**V. 11. ***{My days are like a shadow that declineth}* Isa. 40.6. Jam. 1.10.

*{like a shadow that declineth}* Rather, is stretched out, as Exod. 6.6. for shadows at evening are longest, and then suddenly vanish, Jer. 6.4.

**V. 12. ***{Thou, O Lord, shalt endure for ever}* Lam. 5.19. Howsoever we be frail, yet your promise is sure, and the remembrance thereof shall confirm your people in all adversities.

*{thy remembrance unto all generations}* Psalm 135.13.

**V. 13. ***{shall}* Or, *will*.

*{yea, the set time is come}* That is, the seventy years appointed, Jer. 29.10, 12. or, some such time appointed for their deliverance, Rev. 2.10. & 3.10.

**V. 14. ***{in her stones}* The more that the Church is in misery and desolation, the more do the faithful love and pity it.

**V. 17. ***{the prayer of the destitute}* The Hebrew word ערער, here rendered *destitute,* is thought to signify properly *myricam, tamarisk;* by some mistaken, as Herbalists observe, for ἐρείκη, *heath.* So indeed the Hebrew word is translated in English, Jer. 17.6. *For he shall be like the heath in the desert.* And it is likely they intended *myrica.* Yet I cannot absolutely say, it is a mistake: the word *tamarisk,* would not do so well there; except we understand it of some wild tamarisk, much like unto heath (for some tamarisk grows to a great bigness) to which the description of *humilis* and *tenuis,* that is, low and slender, (as in Virgil, *Non omnes arbusta juvant, humilesque myricae:*) may be proper.

But however low and slender, yet they certainly are mistaken, that make it here, *herbam humilem & abjectam;* as some do: *Plantam,* they might have said. They also, that confound it with juniper: as some others. All this is avoided by translating *nudatissimus,* as some, or as others, *humilis,* (*destitute* here) which certainly is the intention (if not propriety, or proper signification) of the word here, and agrees very well with the etymology of it: from ערר, *nudare,* that is, to uncover. Some fetching it from a different root, (to wit, עור) have translated it, *vociferantis;* others, *excitantis se;* that is, of him that cries: and, of him that stirs up himself: but because not followed, we shall not need further to insist upon either.

**V. 18. ***{This shall be written for the generation to come, and the people which shall be created}* That is, the fame of this (so notable and wonderful) deliverance shall be transmitted to posterity by writing; and those that are not yet born, shall talk of it, and praise you for it in after ages. Or, if he mean, that the Gentiles, who shall see it, or read of it, shall be converted; then, by *the people which shall be created,* is meant, new born and regenerated; as Psal. 22. last verse, *They shall come and shall declare his righteousness, unto a people that shall be born, that he hath done this.* Or, by *the people which shall be created,* may be meant the same people, who being as it were dead and buried in the miseries of a long captivity shall, when restored again, appear as newly created, or raised by a miracle out of the grave.

**V. 19. ***{For}* Or, *When*.

*{looked down}* See upon Psalm 33.13. *The Lord looketh.*

**V. 20. ***{those that are appointed to death}* Heb. *the children of death.* Psalm 79.11. Who now in their banishment could look for nothing but death.

**V. 21. ***{To declare}* Or, *that they may declare*.

**V. 22. ***{When the people are gathered together}* He shows, that God's Name is more praised, then, when religion flourishes, and the Church increases, which is chiefly accomplished in the New Testament.

**V. 23. ***{weakened}* Heb. *afflicted*.

**V. 25. ***{Of old thou hast laid the foundations, &c.}* Whereas heaven and earth are often mentioned (and in that respect, in opposition to man's life, and all that belongs unto it, said to continue for ever,) to set out the stability and immutability of things; they are here, in comparison of God, the better to set out his absolute eternity and immutability, mentioned as things mutable, and transitory. And the setting out of God's eternity and immutability, tends to this, that since God in his own nature is thus and thus, it cannot be that they whom he loves, and who love him, and put their trust in him, should be so perishable and transitory as to be no more after this mortal life is ended. And so St. John in his first Epistle, 2.17. *The world passeth away and the lust thereof; but he that doth the will of God, abideth for ever.*

*{as a vesture shalt thou change them}* The Septuagint have it here, ἑλίξεις, *thou shalt fold them:* and so that passage is quoted Heb. 1.12. whereby the manner of the change is more particularly expressed; to wit, by way of folding. And to this agrees the Prophet's expression, Isa. 34.4. *And all the hoste of heaven shall be dissolved, and the heavens shall be rolled together as a scroll:* Or perchance it is but a similitude, or bare allusion; because when a book, (as anciently books were wont, thence called *volumes*) or a scroll of parchment is folded up, nothing of it (no letters, or words, the essential parts) is seen. It is as it were passed away. St. Peter speaking of the same, says no more, but that the heavens *shall be dissolved,* 2 Pet. 3.12.

**V. 26. ***{perish, but thou shalt endure}* Isa. 51.6.

*{endure}* Heb. *stand*.

**V. 27. ***{same, and thy years shall have no end}* Mal. 3.6. Heb. 13.8.

**V. 28. ***{The children of thy servants shall continue}* This is the literal (as I may call it) immortality, proposed in the Law to them that fear God: their surviving in their posterity, of which see upon Psalm 21.4. *length of days for ever and ever.* By it, and under the type of the Land of promise, (whereof see upon Psalm 37.2. *For they shall soon be cut down,* &c.) was figured that immortal life, and eternity of the godly, in Heaven, more fully revealed and brought to light by the Gospel of Christ: Of which see before upon Psalm 37.1. *Fret not thy self,* &c.

*{shall continue, and their seed shall be established before thee}* Psal. 69.36. Joel 3.20.

*{continue}* Heb. *dwell*.

# PSALM CIII

**Verse 1. ***{Bless the Lord, O my Soul}* He awakens his soul to praise God, showing that both understanding and affections, mind and heart, are too little to set forth his praise.

**V. 2. ***{all}* Or, *any of*.

**V. 3. ***{Who forgiveth all thine iniquities}* Remission of sins is the chief of all benefits, Psalm 32.1, 2.

*{who healeth all thy diseases}* Cures your bodily diseases.

**V. 4. ***{Who redeemeth thy life}* Frees you out of deadly dangers.

*{destruction}* Or, *the pit*, as Psal. 30.3. & 107.20.

*{crowneth}* Or, *encompasses*, as Psal. 5.10. & 32.10.

**V. 5. ***{satisfieth thy mouth with good things}* Or, *satisfies your old age with good things*. Psal. 36.8.

*{so that thy youth is renewed like the Eagles}* As the Eagle is renewed in strength by sucking blood when she cannot feed, as some say, or, by casting the feathers, as others say: so God miraculously gives strength to his people, above all man's expectation: or, he makes you vigorous, as in your youth, and active like an Eagle, Isa. 40.31.

**V. 6. ***{The Lord executeth righteousness}* Heb. *righteousnesses, and judgments*: That is, all sorts of them, or the greatest.

*{judgment for all that are oppressed}* Psalm 146.7.

**V. 7. ***{he made known his ways}* That we should walk in: or that he walks in, that is, his proceedings.

*{unto Moses}* As to his chief Minister, and next to his people, Psalm 147.19.

**V. 8. ***{The Lord is merciful and gracious, slow to anger, and plenteous in mercy}* Exod. 34.6, 7. Num. 14.18. Deut. 5.10. Neh. 9.17. Psal. 86.15. Jer. 32.18.

*{plenteous in mercy}* Heb. *great in mercy*.

**V. 9. ***{He will not always chide}* God shows, first his severe judgment, but so soon as the sinner is humbled, he receives him to mercy.

**V. 10. ***{He hath not dealt with us after our sins}* We have proved by continual experience, that his mercy has ever prevailed against our offenses.

*{nor rewarded us according to our iniquities}* Ezra 9.13.

**V. 11. ***{as the heaven is high}* Heb. *according to the height of the heaven*.

**V. 13. ***{as a father with his children}* Mal. 3.17.

**V. 14. ***{dust}* Gen. 3.19.

**V. 15. ***{As for man, his days are as grass}* He declares, that man has nothing in himself to move God to mercy, but only the confession of his infirmity and misery.

*{grass}* Psal. 90.5. and 102.11. Isa. 40.6. See upon Psal. 92.7. *when the wicked.*

**V. 16. ***{it is gone}* Heb. *it is not*.

*{the place thereof shall know it no more}* So the Scripture uses to express a total abolition of a thing out of its place, Job 7.10. & 20.9.

**V. 17. ***{But the mercy of the Lord, &c.}* See upon Psalm 49.15. *But God will redeem my soul,* &c. and 1 John 2.16, 17. *For all that is in the world,* &c. *but he that doth,* &c.

*{and his righteousness unto children's children}* See upon Psal. 102.28. *The children of thy servants,* &c.

**V. 18. ***{To such as keep his Covenant, and to those that remember his commandments to do them}* Deut. 7.9.

*{To}* Or, *of*.

*{To such as keep his Covenant}* That fear him, and obey his word.

**V. 20. ***{Ye his angels}* Psal. 148.2. In that that we, who naturally are slow to praise God, exhort the angels, which willingly do it, we stir up ourselves to consider our duty, and to awake out of our sluggishness.

*{that excel in strength}* Heb. *mighty in strength*.

**V. 21. ***{ministers of his that do his pleasure}* Heb. 1.14.

**V. 22. ***{bless the Lord, all his works}* This Psalm begins, and ends with the praises of God.

# PSALM CIV

**Verse 1. ***{Thou art clothed with honour}* Psalm 93. verse 1. The Prophet shows that we need not enter into the heavens to seek God, forasmuch as all the order of nature, with the propriety and placing of the elements, are most lively mirrors to see his Majesty in: For God when he made the world, came forth of his palace, as clothed in royal robes, as verse 2.

**V. 2. ***{as with a garment}* As a king in the day of his coronation, puts on gorgeous apparel: so did God show himself glorious in the light, newly made in the creation of the world.

*{stretchest out the heavens like a curtain}* Isa. 45.12.

**V. 3. ***{Who layeth the beams of his chambers in the waters}* Some by these *waters* understand the clouds, according to ver. 13. *He watereth the hills from his chambers.* Others, the heavens, of a watery nature: or heavenly places, where waters, according to the expression of Scripture, Psal. 148.4 and Gen. 1. (according to the exposition of some) 6. are said to be. Others make this to be the scope of the words, to oppose the power and omnipotency of God, to the skill and are of men. For men, or mortal architects in building, it is their main care to provide sufficient main supporters for their beams to rest upon; on which especially depends the firmness and stability of the whole Fabric: but this omnipotent Architect can make the very waters to be as pillars to his edifice. To this may tend that expression also, Psal. 24.2. concerning the earth; *For he hath founded it upon the Seas, and established it upon the floods,* See there.

*{who walketh upon the wings of the wind}* Moves as swiftly, as if he did fly, Psal. 18.10. Isa. 19.1.

**V. 4. ***{Who maketh his angels spirits}* This is interpreted of the angels, Heb. 1.7.

*{his angels spirits}* Or, *the winds his messengers, and the flaming fire his ministers.*

**V. 5. ***{Who laid the foundations of the earth}* Heb. *he hath founded the earth upon her bases.*

*{that it should not be removed for ever}* That is, that it should continue according to its first laws and orders, which were set unto it by God, when first created.

**V. 6. ***{Thou coveredst it with the deep as with a garment}* The waters covered the earth at first, till God made them retire, that the dry land might appear, Gen. 1.2. See upon Psal. 24.2. *upon the Seas,* so they did also cover the earth in the flood, Genesis 7.19.

**V. 7. ***{at the voice of thy thunder}* That is, at your voice, as terrible to the creatures (in point of performance and obedience to your commands) as the noise of thunder uses to be among men: or the voice of God, speaking with thunders and lightnings, as once he did to the Israelites, would be unto men.

**V. 8. ***{They}* Or, *But they*.

*{They go up by the mountains: they go down by the valleys}* Or, *The mountains ascend, the valleys descend:* Or, *They were gone up the mountains*.

**V. 9. ***{set a bound that they may not pass over}* Jer. 5.22.

**V. 10. ***{He sendeth}* Heb. *Who sends*.

*{run}* Heb. *walk*.

*{among}* Or, *between*.

**V. 11. ***{They give drink to every beast of the field}* If God provide for the very beasts, much more will he extend his provident care, to man, Matt. 6.26, 30. & 10.29, 31.

*{quench}* Heb. *break*.

**V. 12. ***{By them shall the fowls of heaven have their habitation}* By *them,* to wit, springs and rivers, where birds are very frequent, if any trees be near. This is excellently well expressed by the best of Latin Poets, speaking of the river Tiber:

*Variae circumque, supraque
Assuetae ripis volucres, & fluminis alveo,
Aethera mulcebant cantu, lucoque volabant.*

*{shall be}* Or, *have you, &c*.

*{sing}* Heb. *give a voice*.

**V. 13. ***{He watereth the hills from his chambers}* From the clouds verse 3.

**V. 14. ***{grass to grow for the cattle}* Psal. 147.8.

*{for the service of man}* He shows God's special care of man, who does not only provide necessary things for him, as herbs, and other meats, but also things to rejoice, and comfort him, as wine and oil, or ointments.

*{food}* Heb. *bread*, Job 28.5.

**V. 15. ***{wine that maketh glad the heart of man}* Judg. 9.13. Prov. 31.6, 7.

*{oil to make his face to shine}* Heb. *to make his face to shine with oil, or, more than oil*. The use of oil was very frequent (and is yet in some countries) in ancient times, both internal and external: insomuch, that next to bread and wine, nothing was thought more beneficial, or necessary to the preservation of health. This *shining,* here spoken of, may either be understood literally, of the shining of the face by anointing, which how usual it was, may appear by our Savior's advice, Matt. 6.17. where he bids them that fast, lest they should be known unto others by the sadness of their countenance, to anoint their heads, and wash their face: Or, which I rather believe, of the effects of oil in general, by the both internal and external use of it; whereby the health and vigor of the body, (here understood by *shining*) among them that use it, is ordinarily much improved. Plin. lib. 14. cap. 22. speaking of both wine and oil, says, *Duo sunt liquores corporibus humanis gratissimi, intus vini, foris olei, arborum è genere ambo praecipui, sed olei necessarius.* Of the two, he makes oil the more necessary.

**V. 16. ***{The trees of the Lord are full of sap}* Such as men did not plant, but the Lord: as follows, see Num. 24.6. or, goodly great trees, Psalm 36.6.

*{are full of sap}* Or, *saturari,* as in the vulgar Latin, that is, filled, or satisfied, to wit, with the rain, before mentioned.

**V. 17. ***{as for the stork, the fir trees are her house}* The stork has her name in Hebrew from kindness, because she feeds her parents when they are old. A good example for children to imitate.

**V. 18. ***{and the rocks for the conies}* The Hebrew word שפנים, both here, and Prov. 30.26. rendered in English, *conies,* is more probably thought by others, to signify *Erinaceum,* a Hedgehog; or some such kind, described by St. Jerome, (*Epist. 135. ad Sun. & Fret.*) to have the shape, partly of a Mouse, and partly of a Bear: and therefore called in those parts, ἀρκτόμυς: who also testifies, that Judea does abound with such, and that their habitation is in rocks. That it should not be the *cony,* may hence also very probably be inferred, because the hare and the cony are reputed but one *genus,* though a different *species;* so that both are thought to be comprehended under the general word of ארנבת, Lev. 11.6. where verse 5. the שפן is also mentioned by itself, as a different kind.

**V. 19. ***{He appointed the moon for seasons}* Or, *appointed times*: by which words some think solemn feasts, and set dates for public meetings, whether Civil or Ecclesiastical, all which were guided and directed (for the time) by the course of the Moon, to be intended. But the meaning may be more general, the same word here translated *seasons,* being also used Gen. 1.14. and the reason why the moon is rather mentioned here for that use than the sun, may be, because most nations, in those days, used to reckon the year by the Moon (which were therefore called, *anni lunares*) rather than by the Sun.

*{the Sun knoweth his going down}* מבואו which may comprehend his whole course from his rising to his setting: as also his several circuits, diurnal with other spheres; or annual, by his own proper motion. In all these he is so exact and regular, that men who cannot tell of themselves, where they themselves shall be the next day, can make Ephemerides of the Sun's course for thousand of years, (if the world last so long,) and tell precisely at what point of heaven, or degree of height he shall be every day: and what Eclipses shall happen every year. To this purpose also is Job's (the Book's, I mean, so called) expression; *Hast thou commanded the morning since thy days; and caused the day spring to know his place?* Job 38.12.

**V. 20. ***{Thou makest darkness}* By causing the Sun to set, ver. 19. Isa. 45.7.

*{All the beasts of the Forest do creep forth}* Heb. *all the beasts thereof do trample on the forest*.

**V. 21. ***{roar}* Or, *which roar*, as verse 16, 28.

*{and seek}* Or, *even to seek*.

*{and seek their meat from God}* God who has given them a natural voice to cry for food, answers it, and provides for them, Job 38.39, 41. Joel 1.20.

**V. 22. ***{The}* Or, *when the*.

**V. 23. ***{Man goeth forth to his work}* To wit, when the day springs: for the light is as it were a shield to defend man, against the tyranny and license of wild beasts.

**V. 24. ***{how manifold are thy works}* He confesses that no tongue is able to express God's works, no mind to comprehend them, Psal. 8.1.

**V. 26. ***{that Leviathan, whom thou hast made to play therein}* Whether any particular kind, as the whale (of which there be many differences) be intended by that word; or whether it be a common name for all great fishes, of which some seas afford great variety, is not easy to determine. In Job 41. we have a large description of *Leviathan;* by which one particular kind seems to be intended, entitled verse 31. *King over all the children of Pride:* (or as the Hebrew, כל בני שחץ does import, *over all beasts:* but according to the use of the word in the Arabic tongue, here observed by some, *over all water beasts*) but what that one kind is, to which that description does agree, is not so easily found. Some think the fish of the Indian seas, called *Musar;* others, some kind of Sea-Crocodile, which they say are so called in some parts of the world to be described. Certain it is, that the greatest of Sea-fishes, both here, and in Job, however called or to be called in English, are intended: among which there be of that immense bigness, as to relate particulars, though from men of credit, who profess, some of them, to have been eyewitnesses: would sound more like a fable among ordinary men, than a truth. Metaphorically, great tyrants, such as Pharaoh king of Egypt, Psal. 74.14. and others, Isa. 27.1. are so called.

*{made}* Heb. *formed*.

**V. 27. ***{These wait all upon thee}* Psal. 145.15. God is a most nourishing father, who provides for all creatures their daily food: and they must tarry till he will give it, verse 28.

**V. 28. ***{that}* Or, *when you*. So verse 29, 30.

**V. 29. ***{Thou hidest thy face, they are troubled}* As by your presence all things have life: so if you withdraw your blessings they all perish.

*{return to their dust}* Psalm 146.4. Gen. 3.19. Eccles. 12.7. Job 34.14, 15.

**V. 30. ***{Thou sendest forth}* As the death of creatures shows, that they are nothing of themselves: so their generation declares that we receive all things of our Creator.

*{thy spirit}* Your quickening power, or the breath which you give them.

*{thou renewest the face of the earth}* By producing new creatures continually, Psal. 33.6. Eccles. 1.4. Or, understand it of the creatures renewed in the spring of the year.

**V. 31. ***{shall endure}* Heb. *shall be*.

**V. 32. ***{He looketh on the earth, and it trembleth}* Who, if he look, &c. it trembles.

*{and they smoke}* Exod. 19.18. Psalm 144.5. God's severe countenance makes the earth to tremble, and burns the mountains.

**V. 33. ***{as long as I live}* Ps. 63.4. & 146.2.

**V. 35. ***{Let the sinners be consumed out of the earth}* Who infect the world, and hinder God's joy in his creatures, as much as in them lies, by abusing them.

# PSALM CV

**Verse 1. ***{O Give thanks, &c.}* These first 15 verses we have also, 1 Chron. 16. of which see more upon Psal. 96. *O sing unto the Lord a new song,* &c.

*{make known his deeds among the people}* Forasmuch as the Israelites were taken out of the world to be God's people, the Prophet wills them to show others what God had done for them.

**V. 2. ***{Sing unto him}* He would have them to praise God, with voice, instruments, and heart.

**V. 4. ***{and his strength}* By God's strength and face, he means the ark, where God declares his power, and presence, Psal. 63.2. & 78.61. See before upon Psal. 27.8. *When thou saidst, seek ye my face,* &c.

**V. 5. ***{Remember his marvellous works}* Which he has wrought in the deliverance of his people.

*{the judgments of his mouth}* That is, his ordinances, and laws, Mal. 4.4. or, his judgments which he threatened to bring on the Egyptians, Gen. 15.14. Exod. 3.20.

**V. 6. ***{his chosen}* Heb. *his chosen ones*.

**V. 7. ***{his judgments are in all the earth}* Isa. 26.9. His judgments on the Egyptians are spoken of all the world over; Exod. 9.16.

**V. 8. ***{He hath remembered his covenant for ever}* 1 Chron. 16.15. *Be ye mindful always of his covenant:* God may remember, and often does, when man has forgotten: but never forgets when man remembers.

*{which he commanded}* His promise, which he will have as firm as his command, Psalm 133.3. & 42.8. & 44.4.

**V. 9. ***{Which covenant he made with Abraham}* Gen. 17.2. & 22.16, &c. & 26.3. & 28.13. & 35.11. Luke 1.73. Heb. 6.13. The promise which God made to Abraham, to be his God, and the God of his seed after him, he renewed it, and repeated it again to Isaac and Jacob.

**V. 11. ***{unto thee will I give the land of Canaan}* Genesis 13.15. & 15.18. He shows that they should not enjoy the land of Canaan by any other means, but by reason of God's covenant made with their fathers.

*{the lot}* Heb. *the cord*, Deut. 32.9.

*{the lot of your inheritance}* Heb. *line, or, cord*: For so were inheritances divided by line, Psalm 16.6.

**V. 12. ***{a few men in number}* Genesis 34.30. Deuteronomy 7.7.

*{strangers in it}* Hebrews 11.9.

**V. 14. ***{He suffered no man to do them wrong}* He kept them from wronging the Patriarchs, that had power to do it, Genesis 35.5.

*{he reproved kings for their sakes}* That is, the kings of Egypt, and Gerar, Genesis 12.17. & 20.3.

**V. 15. ***{Touch not}* Touch them not, so as to hurt them.

*{mine anointed}* Those whom I have sanctified to be my people.

*{do my prophets no harm}* Meaning the old fathers, to whom God showed himself plainly, and who were setters forth of his word, Genesis 20.7.

**V. 16. ***{he called for a famine}* That is, effectually brought it, 2 Kings 8.1.

*{he brake the whole staff of bread}* Lev. 26.26. Ezek. 4.16. Isa. 3.1. By sending scarcity, as in the beginning of the verse.

**V. 17. ***{he sent a man before them}* Gen. 45.5.

*{even Joseph who was sold for a servant}* Gen. 37.28.

**V. 18. ***{Whose feet they hurt with fetters}* Gen. 39.20.

*{he was laid in iron}* Heb. *his soul came into iron*.

**V. 19. ***{Until the time that his word came}* The word of God to Pharaoh in a dream, which is said to try Joseph, because none else could interpret it: and then was Joseph delivered: or God's decree and promise made to Joseph in a dream, Gen. 37.6.

*{tried him}* Or, *which tried him*.

**V. 20. ***{The King sent and loosed him}* Gen. 41.14.

*{even the Ruler of the people}* Heb. *of the peoples*. A mighty king that had a multitude of subjects.

**V. 21. ***{He made him lord of his house, and ruler of all his substance}* Gen. 41.40.

*{substance}* Heb. *possession*.

**V. 22. ***{To bind his Princes at his pleasure}* To govern them, and keep them in awe, Psal. 149.8. Gen. 41.44.

**V. 23. ***{Israel also came into Egypt}* Gen. 46.6.

**V. 25. ***{He turned their heart to hate his people}* I shall, for this once seek no further, but content myself with Diodati, the Geneva Minister (a man both for his piety and learning, of great fame and reputation) his words and annotation upon this place, as I find it in the last English translation, of the year 1648. His words are these; He suffered envy, false fears, and other evil affections to induce the Egyptians to hate the people as much, as before they had loved and favored them. Now the evil work is attributed to God, not because he is author of it, which were as abominable to think, as it is impossible to be; but because he not restraining the malice of man, and suffering the Devil to tempt him with strong objects, occasions, and provocations, it must needs produce its proper effects, the event whereof God voluntarily suffered for some good end, though he reprove and condemn the wickedness and perverseness of it.

**V. 26. ***{He sent Moses his servant, and Aaron whom he had chosen}* Exod. 3.10.

**V. 27. ***{They shewed his signs among them and wonders in the land of Ham}* Exod. 7.9. Psal. 78.44, &c.

*{his signs}* Heb. *words of his signs*.

**V. 28. ***{He sent darkness, and made it dark}* Exod. 10.22. Here he begins to commemorate the plagues of Egypt: but neither does he commemorate all (for of ten, two are omitted) nor in that order that Moses set them down; to wit, as every one happened, consecutively. For here he begins at the ninth. Of this, or the very like, see before upon Psalm 78.49. *He cast upon them the fierceness of his anger,* &c.

*{and they rebelled not against his word}* That is, his word was fulfilled, as Psal. 49.14. They put them in the grave: that is, they were put in the grave: or, *his signs rebelled not*: or, *Moses and Aaron rebelled not*; that is, they did God's message, though with the peril of their lives.

**V. 29. ***{He turned their waters into blood, and slew their fish}* Exod. 7.20.

**V. 30. ***{Their Land brought forth Frogs in abundance}* Exod. 8.6.

*{in the chambers of their Kings}* Of the king and his princes, Isa. 19.2. Exod. 8.3.

**V. 31. ***{He spake, and there came divers sorts of flies, and lice in all their coasts}* Exod. 8.17, 24.

*{He spake, and there came divers sorts of flies}* So that this vermin came not by chance, but as God had appointed, and his Prophet Moses foretold.

**V. 32. ***{He gave them hail for rain}* Exod. 9.23. It was strange to see rain in Egypt, much more, it was fearful to see hail, and most of all hail mingled with fire.

*{He gave them hail for rain}* Heb. *He gave their rain hail*.

*{flaming fire}* Heb. *fire of flames*: that is, full of flames.

**V. 34. ***{He spake, and the Locusts came}* Exodus 10.4. He shows that, all creatures are armed against man, when God is enemy: as at his commandment the Grasshoppers destroyed the Land.

**V. 36. ***{He smote also all the firstborn in their Land}* Exodus 12.29.

*{chief of all their strength}* Gen. 49.3. Psalm 78.51.

**V. 37. ***{He brought them forth also with silver and Gold}* Exod. 12.35.

*{there was not one feeble person among their tribes}* none of them were sick or weak.

**V. 38. ***{Egypt was glad when they departed}* Ex. 12.33. for God's plagues caused them rather to part with the Israelites, than with their lives.

**V. 39. ***{He spread a cloud for a covering}* Exod. 13.21. It seems it was a large cloud, that could cover all the host from the heat of the Sun.

**V. 40. ***{The people asked, and he brought Quails}* Exodus 16.12. &c. Not for necessity, but for satisfying of their lust, Psalm 78.18.

**V. 41. ***{He opened the Rock, and the waters gushed out}* Exod. 17.6. Num. 20.11. 1 Cor. 10.4.

*{like a River}* See notes on Psalm 78.16.

**V. 43. ***{with joy}* When the Egyptians lamented, and were destroyed, Psalm 106.12.

*{gladness}* Heb. *singing*.

**V. 44. ***{And gave them the lands of the heathen}* Deut. 6.10, 11. Josh. 3.17.

*{they inherited the labour of the people}* the houses which they built and vineyards which the heathen planted.

**V. 45. ***{that they might observe his statutes}* God's benefits are given us, that we may serve him the more faithfully.

# PSALM CVI

**Ver. 2. ***{Praise ye the Lord}* Heb. *Hallelujah*. The Prophet exhorts the people to praise God for his benefits past, that thereby their minds may be strengthened against all present troubles and despair.

*{give thanks unto the Lord, &c.}* Psal. 107.1. & 118.1. & 136.1.

**V. 3. ***{Blessed are they that keep judgment}* He shows that it is not enough to praise God with mouth, except heart and life be answerable to God's word.

**V. 4. ***{Remember me, O Lord, with the favour that thou bearest unto thy people}* Gen. 8.1. Psal. 119.132.

*{with the favour that thou bearest unto thy people}* Let the goodwill you have borne to your people heretofore, extend itself unto me.

**V. 6. ***{We have sinned with our Fathers}* Daniel 9. verse 5. By earnest confession as well of their own as of their Fathers' sins, they show, that though utterly unworthy of God's mercies, yet they had hope, that God, according to his promise, would pity them.

**V. 7. ***{But provoked him at the Sea, even at the Red sea}* Exod. 14.11, 12.

*{even at the Red sea}* Or, *and in the Red-sea*: that is, not only before, but when they were entered into the sea: for the Egyptians followed them still.

**V. 8. ***{Nevertheless, he saved them for his name's sake}* Though they were wicked, yet God changed the order of nature to save them, for to perform his promise, and show forth his glory.

**V. 9. ***{led them through the depths, as through the wilderness}* Between the two heaps of water that were deep, Exod. 15.5.

**V. 10. ***{Of him that hated them}* Of Pharaoh.

**V. 11. ***{And the waters covered their enemies}* Exodus 14.27. & 15.5.

**V. 12. ***{then believed they his words}* Exod. 14.31. & 15.1. Psal. 105.43. The wonderful works of God caused them to believe him for a time, and to praise him.

**V. 13. ***{They soon forgat}* Heb. *they made haste, they forgot*.

*{they soon forgat his works}* Exod. 15.24. & 17.2.

*{they waited not for his counsel}* They would prevent his wisdom and providence.

**V. 14. ***{but lusted exceedingly in the wilderness}* Num. 11.4. 1 Cor. 10.6.

*{lusted exceedingly}* Heb. *lusted a lust*.

**V. 15. ***{And he gave them their request}* Num. 11.31.

*{but sent leanness into their soul}* The abundance that God gave them profited them not, but made them pine away, because God cursed it, Hag. 1.6. So some Expositors. But others with more probability, understand it of the plague, that immediately followed; as is more fully expressed, Psal. 78.30, 31. *but while the meat was yet in their mouths,* &c. It is very likely that the Psalmist intended the same thing in both places, though the expressions be somewhat different. Leanness oftentimes is a consequent (in nature) of gluttony. Now because this plague was a consequent, but in another respect, of their gluttony, it is therefore wittily and figuratively termed, *leanness.*

Another reason is given by some learned Interpreters, why the Prophet rather uses the word רזון *leanness;* than רזון *leanness,* (Moses his word, Num. 11.33.) because, as they conceive, he would thereby allude to רצון, *good will,* or *pleasure,* as if he intimated that by the רצון (or *rason,*) that is, *pleasure,* or satisfaction of their mind, they purchased to themselves רזון (*rason*) even a deadly leanness, that consumed many of them: a fit recompence for their extravagant lust. Many such tacit allusions there be in the Scriptures, the observation whereof does much conduce to the understanding of the matter itself.

**V. 16. ***{They envied Moses also in the camp}* Num. 16.1, &c.

*{Aaron the saint of the Lord}* Whom God set apart, as holy, for the Priesthood.

**V. 17. ***{The earth opened, and swallowed up Dathan, &c.}* Numb. 16.31. Deut. 11.6.

*{and swallowed up Dathan}* By the greatness of the punishment the heinousness of the offense may be concluded: for they that rise against God's ministers, rebel against him.

*{and covered the company of Abiram}* He means Korah, and his associates that were joined with them in rebellion.

**V. 18. ***{And a fire was kindled in their company}* Numbers 16.35, 49.

**V. 19. ***{They made a calf in Horeb}* Exod. 32.4.

**V. 20. ***{Thus they changed their glory into the similitude of an ox that eateth grass}* Jerem. 2. ver. 11. Rom. 1. ver. 23. He shows that all Idolaters renounce God to be their glory, when instead of him, they worship any creature, much more wood, stone, metal, or calves, Rom. 1.23, 25. or other base things, Ezek. 8. ver. 10.

**V. 23. ***{Therefore he said that he would destroy them}* Exodus 32.10.

*{had not Moses his chosen stood}* If Moses, by his intercession had not prevailed with God to pardon their rebellions.

*{in the breach}* Verse 29. A similitude from a breach in the wall of a City, wherein one stands, to keep out the enemies by force. It shows the great force of prayer to divert God's judgments, Ezek. 13.5. and 22.30. whence the plague is said to break in, verse 29.

**V. 24. ***{the pleasant land}* Heb. *a land of desire*. That is, Canaan, which was, as it were, an earnest-penny of the heavenly inheritance.

*{they believed not his word}* Heb. 3.19. That he would bring them into Canaan.

**V. 25. ***{But murmured in their tents}* Num. 14.2.

**V. 26. ***{he lifted up his hand against them}* Gen. 14.22. That is, he swore, Ezek. 20.15, 23. Psal. 95.11. Sometimes also it signifies to punish.

*{to overthrow them in the wilderness}* Num. 14.28.

**V. 27. ***{To overthrow}* Heb. *to make them fall*.

**V. 28. ***{They joined themselves also unto Baalpeor}* Num. 25.3. Which was the Idol of the Moabites.

*{and ate the sacrifices of the dead}* Sacrifices offered to the dead Idols, Psal. 115.5. which neither have, nor ever had any reality of being. This Interpretation passes for current among Expositors, scarce any, that I know of excepted. Yet, is it not satisfactory to all men. For some (no professed Expositors, but occasionally treating of these words in learning not inferior to any) make this to be the sense; They did eat, or partake of those funeral banquets, which among divers Idolatrous Nations (as the Grecians and Romans) were used by way of Solemn Parentation (whether in honor, or for any other respect) to the dead. And these they make to have been the proper sacrifices belonging to the worship of that particular Idol. Baal-Phegor perchance might be the man himself, or the name of a man, once Prince or King among the Moabites, whose memory might among other idolatrous rites, be worshipped in and by these Solemn Feasts.

This Interpretation may the better be thought of, if it be considered, first that Idols are not anywhere else in the Scripture so called, as by the common Interpretation of the word *dead,* this should be here; and secondly, that this belonging to all idols in general, to be *dead* in this sense, it is not so likely, comparing the words with the history in Moses, and the words there, this should be the meaning here, but rather somewhat that was more particular unto that particular idol. But the aggravation in the words of the Psalmist, which makes his expression much more emphatical, is also very considerable. For there was not, according to the Law, in Lev. 11. and Num. 19. any pollution, or abomination (ritual and external) greater than that which was contracted (whether by any contact, or cohabitation) from the dead. So that without respect to the Idolatry of it, *to eat of the sacrifices of the dead,* must needs sound among the Jews, as a matter of great horror and detestation. This aggravation will not hold according to that common interpretation, if *the dead* be taken here figuratively, for them that have no real being.

It is also very possible, that the Psalmist might allude to the very name of *Baal Peor.* פגר or *peger* in Hebrew does signify a dead corpse. I know *Peor,* is written with a ע not a ג in the Original. But the affinity may appear, in that many words written in the Hebrew with a *Ain,* are expressed in Greek with a *Gamma,* as *Gomorra, Gaza,* &c. and in allusions we must not look for identity, but affinity. Many such there be in the Scripture (as all men grant) in proper names especially, both in pronunciation and writing more remote than this. I shall not now make inquisition into the right meaning of those words, either Tobit 4.17. *Pour out thy bread on the burial of the just, but give nothing to the wicked:* Or Eccles. 30.18. *Delicates poured upon a mouth shut up, or messes of meat set upon a grave;* because they are but Apocrypha: Yet I mention them, because the consideration of their words may prove some confirmation to that Interpretation we here embrace.

**V. 29. ***{Thus they provoked him to anger with their inventions}* Signifying, that whatsoever man invents of himself to serve God by, is detestable, and provokes his anger.

*{inventions}* Heb. *practices*.

*{and}* Or, *so that*. So v. 32, 33.

*{break in upon them}* verse 23.

**V. 30. ***{Then stood up Phinehas, &c.}* Num. 25.7. When all others neglected God's glory, he in his zeal killed the adulterers, and so prevented God's wrath.

**V. 31. ***{for righteousness}* God counted it a righteous act, though men might think it a fruit of rash zeal.

**V. 32. ***{They angered him also at the waters of strife}* Num. 20.13.

*{so that it went ill with Moses for their sakes}* Num. 20.12. Deut. 3.26. If so notable a Prophet of God escaped not punishment, though others provoked him to sin; how much more shall they be subject to God's judgment, which cause God's children to sin?

**V. 33. ***{So that he spake unadvisedly with his lips}* Hebrew ויבטא here translated, *he spake unadvisedly;* is by others barely expressed, *& protulit;* or, *& locutus est,* that is, *and he spake,* or *uttered,* &c. So indeed the word may sometimes be used, to import bare speaking: but properly it signifies to speak, or utter any thing rashly or idly. Hence is the word βαττολογέω, and βαττολογία: in English, *battology,* or, *vain repetitions*, as the word is translated Matt. 6.7. forbidden there by our Savior, in praying, as heathenish: though still affected by some Christians, as a great gift. A profitable gift indeed it is to some that make a trade of it, Matt. 23.14. *Woe unto you Scribes,* &c.

But to the Psalmist: The words here laid to Moses his charge, are conceived to be those, Num. 20.10. *Hear now ye rebels, must we fetch you water out of this rock?* That Moses indeed did, or said somewhat there, that displeased God, appears clearly by verse 12. *And the Lord spake unto Moses,* &c. But that those were the words that offended, is but a conjecture, of which, though grounded upon some probability, there is no certainty. Some therefore to avoid this doubt, refer the words here to God himself, translating, *So that he spake it,* or *pronounced it,* (to wit, their punishment, for their infidelity, Num. 20.12.) *with his lips.*

As for the word before המרו, *they provoked:* we know it is an ordinary word, when God is intended, or plainly spoken of; as for example verse 43. following, *Many times—but they provoked him* (ימרו) *with their counsels,* &c. and it makes the matter more probable, because upon this very occasion the word (whether מרה or מרד: for they are commonly confounded, and some make but one of them:) Num. 20.24. is spoken of God, as the subject, or object: על אשר מריתם, *because ye rebelled;* as our English there: but the Septuagint, διότι παρωξύνατέ με; that is, *because ye provoked me.* I do not find fault with the English there: but I say, it is the same word, which signifies to rebel, and to provoke: which, having there plainly God for its object, it is the more likely, that the same may here also be intended. So by *Spirit,* the Spirit of God may be understood, as Ephes. 4.30. *And grieve not the holy Spirit of God,* &c. But for all this, I leave it free to the Reader, to please himself. There is no authority for the one; but, as I conceive, not less probability for the other.

**V. 34. ***{concerning whom the Lord commanded them}* Deut. 7.2.

**V. 35. ***{But were mingled among the heathen}* Judg. 1.21. By marriage, and so they were drawn to Idolatry.

**V. 36. ***{and they served their idols}* They have their name in Hebrew from sorrow, because they bring much grief to their worshippers, Psalm 16.4.

*{which were a snare unto them}* Exod. 23.33. To bring them to destruction, as snares do birds.

**V. 37. ***{they sacrificed their sons and their daughters unto devils}* He shows the great force of Idolatry, which can win us to things abhorring to nature, when God's Word cannot obtain the smallest things of us.

*{devils}* 1 Cor. 10.20.

**V. 38. ***{idols of Canaan}* 2 Kings 16.3.

**V. 39. ***{and went a whoring with their own inventions}* Then true chastity is, to cleave wholly and only unto God, Num. 15.29. Lev. 17.7.

*{inventions}* Or, *practices*.

**V. 41. ***{And}* Or, *so that*, as verse 29.

**V. 43. ***{Many times did he deliver them}* Judg. 2.16. The Prophet shows, that neither by menaces nor promises we can come to God, except we be altogether newly reformed, and that his mercy hide our wickedness.

*{brought low}* Or, *impoverished, or weakened*.

**V. 45. ***{he remembered for them his covenant}* Deuter. 30.3. Two things made God relieve them, his covenant, and his mercy.

*{and repented according to the multitude of his mercies}* Not that God is changeable in himself, but that when he stretches to us to repent, when he takes away his punishment, and forgives us.

**V. 46. ***{He made them also to be pitied of all those that carried them captives}* Heb. *and he gave them for pities:* that is, he made them find abundance of pity, Jer. 15.11.

**V. 47. ***{gather us from among the heathen}* Bring us from among the heathen to Jerusalem, to praise you.

# PSALM CVII

The drift and purpose of this Psalm is, to assert the providence of God towards men, against all, either absolute atheists, who deny the being of God; or Epicures, who deny, that God takes any care at all of men, to do them either hurt or good. Now the Divine Providence being two ways, especially, considerable; either in that order (both for the constancy, and beautifulness of it, equally admirable) which he has set to nature, in things merely natural, which we may call his Creating providence, or in those things, which in the nature of chances and accidents, because both various and uncertain (whether to many together, or to every particular man) happen in the world; which we may call his executive, or administrative providence: as the first kind was set out before, in a Psalm by itself, to wit, the 104 Psalm; which for the loftiness, and other ornaments of it, has ever been accounted an exquisite piece: so is the second, with much elegancy and sublimity, in this present Psalm.

Yet all particulars of God's providence in that kind, are not here handled, but some only, wherein it is most eminent, as in saving men in adversity, and succoring them in their manifold necessities and extremities, to which this mortal life is exposed: there being no title among men more glorious, or which commends Religion unto men more, than of a Savior and Deliverer. That all people and nations of the world in general, not any one particular Nation, are intended by the Psalmist, as the object of that goodness of God which he does here celebrate and set out; if the tenor of the words and context itself does not sufficiently (which I think it should) evince; I must appeal to the authority of best Expositors: I do not know any, not of any note at least, that makes any question of it. If any would restrain these things to the Jewish Nation, they must do violence to many particular expressions; but much more wrong to the general scope of the Psalm. And it may very well be, that the Psalmist might have another aim too, besides what has been shown hitherto, against Atheists and Epicures. It is the opinion of the Jews at this day, as by some of their glosses upon Deut. 4.19 may appear, that the providence of God is their peculiar; and that God has left all other Nations besides themselves, to the government of the Stars, and inferior powers, or Angels. It is not improbable, that some such opinion might be in those ancient times among some of them. Such an infatuation there is among many Christians too, who upon a fond persuasion, that themselves are the only children of God, look upon other men, as things that God had nothing to do with: not as creatures, at least, within compass of his either love or care: which they wholly, (and, upon that ground, would all worldly goods also, their chiefest aim:) engross to themselves. This Psalm is an ample confutation of both; whereby God's goodness and mercy to all, in all places of the world, whether Jews or Gentiles, when they call upon him with heart and affection in their perils and necessities, is vindicated and asserted. See also upon Psal. 71.5. *thou art my trust,* and Psal. 117.1. *O praise the Lord all ye nations.* Some objections against the doctrine of this Psalm we shall meet with, upon some of the following verses.

**Verse 1. ***{Give thanks unto the Lord}* Psal. 106.1. and 118.1. and 136.1. This notable sentence was in the beginning among the Jews, as some are of opinion, used as the *versus intercalares* (the foot, or tenor of the song) of many sacred hymns, being the part of the opposite Quire, at certain pauses and intervals, while they were singing.

**V. 2. ***{Let the redeemed of the Lord say so}* That is, whom God's providence has delivered from present death, not any chance or fortune.

*{redeemed}* Or, *rescued*.

**V. 3. ***{from the South}* Heb. *from the Sea*. That is, from the Red Sea, which stood southward from the land of Canaan: elsewhere *from the Sea,* is from the west, because the Mediterranean Sea stood westward.

**V. 4. ***{They}* Or, *Who*.

*{thy}* Or, *and*.

**V. 5. ***{Hungry and thirsty}* He shows that there is no affliction so grievous, out of which God cannot deliver: and also exhorts them that are delivered, to be mindful of so great a benefit.

*{thirsty}* Judg. 15.18.

**V. 6. ***{Then they cried unto the Lord}* Even they, who, when at ease, and out of danger, little think of God, and make a mock of religion, yet even these oftentimes, when in misery, by a certain instinct of nature, become religious, and earnestly call upon God: whereby it appears that there is some natural sense of Religion in the greatest Infidels, and enemies of Religion. So learned and judicious Calvin here, who has much to that purpose.

But there may be an objection. For if, as we have said, this extends to all people and nations promiscuously; that is, to mankind in general, *How shall they call on him,* may some boldly say, *in whom they have not believed? or how shall they believe in him, of whom they have not heard?* &c. in the Apostle's words, Rom. 10.14. I answer, that this is not to be understood of formal praying, according to the prescript of God's Word in all circumstances; but of such, as nature in such extremities does suggest; and such as God out of his tender mercy and compassion towards his poor creatures, is sometimes pleased to accept of in such cases. And what wonder is it, if the Ravens in their need, are said to have their cries, which God is said to hear, Psal. 147.9. Job 38.41. that men, though not otherwise instructed, or prepared, should find cries at such times, which should move God, their maker, to compassion?

Well, but it is not the true God that they cry unto, such as know him not, I mean, but some false god or Idol, as either Jupiter, or Mercury, or Neptune, and Pollux, the once proper gods of Mariners, among the ancient Heathens, or the like: and how should the true God hear them? I answer; these be acts of extraordinary mercy. If we allow Princes a power to dispense with the standing Laws to save a life; with much more reason may we allow it unto God. *They cried unto the Lord,* or, *they cry unto the Lord*; for in these preterits, a continual act is included, as Interpreters note, says the Psalmist: though they miscall him, yet unto the Lord; because under the notion of a true God, their piety is sincere; though it be erroneous. So once St. Paul told the Idolatrous Athenians; it was the true God, whom he preached, that they worshipped, though ἈΓΝΩΣΤΩΣ, ignorantly. He was willing to make the best, at that time, speaking to themselves about their devotions, the easier to win them: he could have told them as truly, but upon another consideration, that what they worshipped, were Devils, and not God; as he does elsewhere, 1 Cor. 10.20. but of purpose there, aggravating the matter, to deter others. There was some ground for both, as well for the former, as for the latter judgment. According to the first, it is, that the Psalmist says here, of all men in general, that *they cry unto the Lord* in their distress.

*{and he delivered them out of their distresses}* That is, as many as are delivered, it is by God, that they are delivered. He is the author of all salvation; both temporal and eternal, unto all men. Why all are not delivered, that call upon him at such a time, is not for us to give a reason. Sure we are, if all were, at all times, it would much alter the whole frame and constitution of the world; and by degrees we may come to ask, why God has not made all men immortal? For none would die, if always heard when they cry.

It is the same reason of the sick, as of others, verse 17, 18, &c. I think it safer to say so, than to resolve the question, as some do, that they are the wicked only, that so perish; whom God will have examples of his judgments, and just severity; as the others, whom he saves, though wicked too, of his mercy. I dare not think that all that miscarry either by land, or by water, are impious. I rather believe, that many both just and pious, undergo sometimes, in the same ship, or otherwise, the same fortune with them that are not. There be secrets of providence, which though we understand not, yet we must not deny. Christ would not have us to judge them, upon whom the Tower of Siloam fell; or them whose blood Pilate mingled with sacrifices, greater sinners than other men, Luke 13.1, 2, &c. God forbid we should condemn any for any temporal calamity.

One reason why sometimes some good and godly miscarry, as well as others, may be, that they that are so, should not be too bold and secure, which might prove very prejudicial to them in other respects; and that when they are delivered, they may be the more heartily thankful, which is for their good, and God's glory. See also upon Psal. 91.5. *Thou shalt not be afraid,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{of habitation}* Or, *inhabited*.

**V. 8. ***{Oh that men would praise}* Or, *Let them praise*, as verse 21.

**V. 9. ***{For}* Or, *Because*.

**V. 10. ***{affliction, and iron}* Job 36.8.

**V. 11. ***{because they rebelled against the words of God}* No affliction happens unto any, but for their sins, and very justly, at the hands of God: though comparatively, in regard of other greater sinners, many may be said to suffer innocently.

**V. 12. ***{Therefore he brought down their heart}* Or, *whose heart he had brought down*.

*{they fell down, and there was none}* Or, *who fell so down, that there was none*.

**V. 13. ***{Then}* Or, *When*.

*{Then they cried unto the Lord}* He shows, that one cause why God does punish us extremely, is, because we will be brought unto him by no other means, ver. 6. Hos. 5.15.

*{and}* Or, *then*.

**V. 15. ***{Oh that men would praise}* Or, *let them praise*.

**V. 16. ***{For}* Or, *because*.

*{he hath broken the gates of brass}* When there seems to man's judgment no recovery, but all things are brought to despair, then God chiefly shows his mighty power; as in opening prison-doors, and shaking off fetters, Acts 12.6, 7.

**V. 17. ***{Fools, because of their transgression}* They that have no fear of God, by sharp rods are brought to call upon him, and to find mercy, Prov. 26.3. Lam. 1.8.

*{because}* Or, *who because*.

*{and because of their iniquities}* Heb. *because of the way of their transgression*: that is, from their sinful course of life.

*{are afflicted}* Heb. *do afflict themselves*: that is, do bring afflictions upon themselves by their sins.

**V. 18. ***{Their soul}* Or, *Whole soul*.

*{their soul abhorreth all manner of meat}* Job 33.20.

*{and they}* Or, *so that they*, as Psal. 106.32.

**V. 19. ***{Then}* Or, *As when*.

*{saveth}* Or, *saved*.

**V. 20. ***{He sent his word and healed them}* Most of these expressions are taken out of Job 33.20, 21, 22, &c. and elsewhere. His word, here may have reference to those there, verse 23. *If there be a messenger with him,* &c. and chap. 36.9, 10, 11. *Then he sheweth them their work,* &c. Or, he sent his word, that is, his command. God need but speak the word, whether for health, or sickness, and the thing is done. So the Centurion in the Gospel to Christ, *Speak but the word only, and my servant shall be healed,* Matt. 8.8.

*{from their destructions}* Meaning, their diseases, which had well-nigh destroyed them.

*{destructions}* Or, *pits*; that is, graves, Ezek. 19.4, 8. Psal. 103.4.

**V. 21. ***{Oh that men would praise}* Or, *Let them praise*.

**V. 22. ***{The sacrifices of thanksgiving}* Praise and confession of God's benefits, are the true sacrifices of the Godly, Psal. 50.14, 23. and 116. Heb. 13.15.

*{rejoicing}* Heb. *singing*.

**V. 23. ***{They go down to the Sea in ships}* He shows, by God's dealings with Sea-men, what care God has over men: for in that, that he delivers them from the great dangers of the Sea, he delivers them, as it were, from a thousand deaths.

*{that go down}* Either from the mid-land, higher hilly country, as that of Judea was, to the Sea-side, where the land is lower: or, from the shore, raised by many works, in many places, down to the Sea; there lower, though generally, higher than the land.

**V. 24. ***{see}* Or, *who see*.

**V. 25. ***{For}* Or, *How*.

*{raiseth}* Heb. *makes to stand*.

*{which lifteth}* Or, *so that it lifts*.

**V. 26. ***{They}* Or, *And they*.

*{they go down again to the depths}* That is, very deep.

*{their soul is melted because of trouble}* Their fear and danger is so great. Some take it for vomiting, Psal. 22.14. and 119.28. Nah. 2.10.

**V. 27. ***{are at their wits end}* Heb. *all their wisdom is swallowed up*. When their are and means fail them, they are compelled to confess, that only God's providence does preserve them.

**V. 28. ***{Then}* Or, *When*.

*{and}* Or, *then*.

**V. 29. ***{He}* Or, *And*.

*{maketh}* Heb. *sets*.

**V. 30. ***{Then}* Or, *And*.

*{because they be quiet}* Though before every drop seemed to fight one against another, yet at his commandment they are as still, as though they were frozen, Matt. 8.26.

*{so he bringeth them}* Or, *and he has brought them*.

**V. 31. ***{Oh that men would praise}* Or, *Let them praise*.
