As for example: *How beautiful upon the mountains are the feet of him that bringeth good tidings, that publisheth peace,—that saith unto Sion, thy God reigneth,* Isa. 52.7.

The Psalmist therefore here, alluding to this common speech and use, most sweetly and elegantly declares that he also will *lift up his eyes:* but to mountains, from which he certainly knows, that salvation will come to him. Had he said no more than so; yet I do not conceive that his meaning would have been very doubtful, or obscure: not more doubtful, or obscure, than where he says, *Lead me to the rock, that is higher than I,* Psal. 61.2.

But then when in the very next verse he does so plainly express himself: *My help cometh from the Lord,* &c., I do admire how he should be so mistaken by any man. I will spare their words; but I could say no less, without doing the holy Psalmist, not to go higher, wrong. In effect, therefore, the Psalmist does disclaim all expectation of help from any other hill, than heaven: which sometimes also is called a hill, as is here observed by some: the most proper object as well of our eyes, as of our hearts: for which also man, by God's providence, *Os homini sublime dedit, caelumque tueri jussit,* &c. is fitted in his bodily structure.

*{unto the hills}* Some are of opinion that Mount Sion and Moriah, upon which the Sanctuary stood, upon one of them, at least; but supposed to stand upon both, because of the nearness, might be intended by the Psalmist. And true it is, that help, and salvation, or to that effect, is said sometimes to come from the Sanctuary, where God was pleased in those days to manifest himself, and to afford the effects of his presence, to humble supplicants, after a more peculiar manner: and not to come from the Sanctuary only, but from those hills also. See before upon Psal. 14.7. *Out of Zion:* and Psal. 5.7. *towards thy holy Temple.*

We will not therefore reject this, if any like it: but prefer, in our own judgment, the former interpretation: as more proper to the place; and more emphatical. Either of these interpretations will serve to save us from that different but violent interpretation in the margin: *Shall I lift up mine eyes to the hills? whence should my help come?* There will be no need of this shift: though, of the two, I should think that more tolerable, than to asperse the Psalmist, and to lay the blame of our mistakes upon him wrongfully.

**V. 2. ***{My help cometh from the Lord}* Psal. 124.8.

*{which made heaven and earth}* He secretly accuses man's ingratitude, who does not depend upon God's power.

**V. 3. ***{he that keepeth thee will not slumber}* He shows, that God's providence watches not only over his Church in general, but also over every member thereof in particular.

**V. 4. ***{shall neither slumber nor sleep}* He will neglect no occasion of your preservation, Psal. 127.1. Isa. 27.3.

**V. 5. ***{thy shade upon thy right hand}* Isa. 25.4.

**V. 6. ***{The sun shall not smite thee by day}* Neither heat nor cold, nor any discommodity shall be able to annoy you, Psal. 91.5. See more there.

**V. 7. ***{The Lord shall preserve thee from all evil}* The Lord shall keep you from danger both abroad, and at home, Deut. 28.6.

# PSALM CXXII

**Verse 1. ***{I Was glad}* He rejoices, that God had brought the Ark to a place, where it should remain. Or it may be, this Psalm may be of the same argument as Psalm 84. *How amiable,* &c. See more there upon verse 3. *Yea the sparrow,* &c.

**V. 2. ***{Our feet shall stand}* Whereas they were wont to wander to and fro, as the Ark removed.

**V. 3. ***{that is compact together}* By the artificial joining and beauty of the houses, is signified the concord and love that is between the Citizens.

**V. 4. ***{the tribes go up}* All the tribes do resort thither, and pray there.

*{unto the testimony of Israel}* To the Ark, the sign of God's presence, and in which were the two Tables, containing God's testimony, how he would be served, and from whence God declared his presence by Oracle, Exod. 25.21, 22.

**V. 5. ***{are set}* Heb. *do sit*.

*{thrones of the house of David}* In whose house God placed the throne of justice, and made it a figure of Christ's Kingdom.

**V. 7. ***{Peace be within thy walls}* The favor of God give you peace, and keep you from all wars, Psal. 147.14.

**V. 8. ***{For my brethren and companions sakes}* Not only for mine own sake, but for all the faithful, whom I account as my brethren, Psal. 119.63.

# PSALM CXXIII

**Verse 1. ***{Unto thee lift I up mine eyes, O thou that dwellest in the heavens}* Psal. 121.1. Psal. 115.3.

**V. 2. ***{until that he have mercy upon us}* Until he deliver us out of this misery. This argues constancy in prayer, Luke 18.1, 7.

**V. 3. ***{we are exceedingly filled with contempt}* Psal. 88.3. When the faithful are so full, that they can no more endure the oppressions and scornings of the wicked, then with hungry desires they cry for help, not ceasing till they obtain it, 2 Chron. 20.12.

# PSALM CXXIV

**Verse 1. ***{If it had not been the Lord}* He shows, That God was ready to help at need, and that there was none other way to be delivered, but only by his power.

**V. 3. ***{Then they had swallowed us up quick}* So unable were we to resist. A metaphor taken from ravenous beasts, who so speedily, and greedily devour their prey, that it seems to go quick down their throats, as Jonah did with the Whale, Jonah 1.17.

**V. 4. ***{Then the waters had overwhelmed us}* Psal. 69.2. He uses most proper similitudes of swallowing and drowning, to express the great danger that the Church had been in, and out of which God miraculously had delivered them.

**V. 7. ***{Our soul is escaped as a bird out of the snare of the fowlers}* Psal. 91.3.

*{as a bird out of the snare}* For the wicked did not only furiously rage against the faithful, but craftily imagined to destroy them.

# PSALM CXXV

**Verse 1. ***{Which cannot be removed}* Though the world be subject to mutations, yet the people of God shall stand sure, and be defended by God's providence.

**V. 3. ***{the rod of the wicked shall not rest upon the lot of the righteous}* Though God suffer his to be under the cross, (the rod, or sceptre of the wicked, or tyrannical men,) for a while; yet this cross shall not so rest upon them, that it should drive them from hope, and cause them to betake themselves to evil courses, Prov. 30.9.

*{the wicked}* Heb. *wickedness*.

**V. 5. ***{As for such}* He foretells the great prosperity of the Church, when God shall have purged it from evil doers.

*{as turn aside unto their crooked ways}* Those that fall away from God shall have their portion with those that never were with him, Psal. 73.27.

*{peace shall be upon Israel}* Psal. 128.6. Gal. 6.16.

# PSALM CXXVI

**Verse 1. ***{Turned again the captivity of Zion}* Heb. *returned the returning of Zion*.

*{like them that dream}* Their deliverance was as a thing incredible, and therefore seemed rather a dream, than a thing in truth performed, Acts 12.9.

**V. 2. ***{Then was our mouth filled with laughter}* He shows how the godly ought to rejoice, when God gathers his Church, or delivers it.

*{then said they among the heathen}* The very heathen could not but take notice of God's handy-work in our kingdom: and if the Infidels confess God's wonderful work, the faithful can never show themselves sufficiently thankful.

*{hath done great things for them}* Heb. *has magnified to do with them*.

**V. 4. ***{Turn again our captivity, O God}* He does not, as a man might think, contradict himself. For their return from that Captivity, as appears Ezra and Nehemiah, was a long work, and much opposed. Well he might therefore at the same time give thanks for that which was so happily begun, and pray for the accomplishment.

*{as the streams in the South}* It is as possible for you to bring back the rest of the captives from Babylon, as to cause streams to break out in dry places, where the Sun scorches in the South, Judg. 1.15. Or, it shall be as comfortable to us, as rivers of water in dry places.

**V. 5. ***{They that sow in tears, shall reap in joy}* The matter of this excellent sentence in a more general sense, to wit, that in the course of nature, one contrary begets another, *mel generat fel,* &c.; pleasure, sorrow; and sorrow, pleasure; has been the object of many ancient Philosophers' speculation, and has also sententiously been expressed by some of them: but by none so pithily and emphatically, as by our Psalmist here. I shall only mention two here; neither of them Philosophers, but Latin Writers both: Pliny, the elder, or uncle; and Pliny, the nephew, or younger. The first thus; *Quae magna gaudia, nisi ex malis? quae mala immensa, nisi ex ingentibus gaudiis?* I give him the precedence, because of his age: but the latter comes more fully to our purpose; and his words are very notable indeed: had he intended a paraphrase upon the Psalmist, he could not have spoken better; *Habet hos vices conditio mortalium, ut adversa ex secundis; ex adversis, secunda nascantur. Occultat utrorumque semina Deus, & plerumque bonorum malorumque causae sub diversa specie latent.* He makes it a great mystery, not of nature, as some others before him, upon this subject; (though they might mean the same thing too, by that word,) but of God's providence, plainly. See also upon Psal. 28.5. *Because they regard not the works of the Lord,* &c.

*{shall reap}* Or, *let them reap*.

*{joy}* Or, *singing*.

**V. 6. ***{bearing precious seed}* That is, seed which was scarce and dear: Or, precious, because of the effects of it, being the seed of immortality, as the words may more generally, or in the mystical sense import. But whether *precious* be the meaning of the Hebrew word, is not agreed, among Interpreters. So the word indeed is expounded Job 28.18. *for the price of wisdom is above Rubies.* It is Kimchi's exposition there. But others translate, *acquisitio sapientiae,* that is, *the purchasing of wisdom;* which is as probable a translation. Others therefore from Amos 9.13. *he that soweth* (or, *draweth forth;* as in the margin) seed: make this to be a proper word to express the manner of casting, or scattering seed-corn, by a kind of drawing, or orderly stretching of the arm; which is not done without some are. So that according to them the words should be translated, *portans tractum seminis;* that is, *bearing draw-seed;* or, *seed to be sown.* Others again there be, who would have the word to signify (as it is here in the margin) seed-basket. Somewhat may be said for every one of these different interpretations; but *tractus seminis,* as in Amos, is in my judgment, the most warrantable. The best is, none of them are much amiss to the main sense and purpose. They that translate in Job *acquisitio sapientiae,* translate here, *acquisitum semen;* which also may be allowed: though it does not follow of necessity, that because the word signifies *acquisitio* there, it must here, *acquisitum.* The word, as most others, may in different constructions, or upon different occasions, have different significations: which cannot be said of the place of Amos: because there it is spoken of seed, as here; and therefore likely to have but one signification in both places.

*{shall doubtless come}* Or, *let him come*.

*{doubtless come}* Heb. *coming come*.

# PSALM CXXVII

## TITLE

*{for Solomon}* Or, *of Solomon*, Psal. 72.

**Verse 1. ***{Except the Lord build the house}* It may be taken, for building the house, or increasing the family, Exod. 1.21.

*{that build it}* Heb. *that are builders of it in it*: or, *they labor in vain about it, that build it*.

*{except the Lord keep the city}* The public estate of the Commonwealth.

**V. 2. ***{It is vain for you to rise up early}* Which watch and ward, or, which labor hard for your living.

*{to eat the bread of sorrows}* Gotten by hard labor and care, Gen. 3.19.

*{so he giveth}* Or, *he does rightly give*.

*{he giveth}* Your care cannot bring you true rest, but God's blessing will, Prov. 10.22.

*{his beloved sleep}* It may be an allusion to Solomon's name Jedidiah, 2 Sam. 12.25.

**V. 3. ***{Lo, children are an heritage of the Lord}* Gen. 33.5. and 48.4. Josh. 24.3, 4. Begotten while their parents are young, and which may live to comfort them in their old age. See the contrary, Gen. 44.20.

**V. 5. ***{hath his quiver full of them}* Heb. *has filled his quiver with them*.

*{they shall not be ashamed}* Their children will be ready to plead for them, and to right them, when they are wronged by others: or, their enemies cannot shame them, with objecting to them the want of children, which in those days was a reproach, both to man and woman, because some curse was thought to lie upon those that had none. So Luke 1.25. Elizabeth speaks of herself, having been sterile to that time, and now with child; *Thus hath the Lord dealt with me in the days wherein he looked on me, to take away my reproach among men.*

*{shall speak with}* Or, *shall subdue*, as Psal. 18.47. or, *destroy.*

*{in the gate}* The wonted places of public appearance in those days, it being the place for Courts of justice, and trials at law, which brought great resort of people.

# PSALM CXXVIII

**Verse 1. ***{Walketh in his ways}* Psal. 119.1.

*{in his ways}* God approves not our life, except it be reformed according to his word, Psal. 119.9.

**V. 2. ***{For thou shalt eat the labour of thine hands}* Isa. 3.10. The world esteems them happy, which live in wealth and idleness; but the Holy Ghost approves them best, that live of the profit of their labors.

**V. 3. ***{thy children like olive plants}* Because God's favor appears in none outward thing, more than in increase of children, he promises to enrich the faithful with this gift.

**V. 5. ***{out of Zion}* With such blessings, as he granted from Zion, the place of his special residence.

*{thou shalt see the good}* See it with delight, and have a part in it, Psal. 27.4.

*{of Jerusalem}* The blessing on their own families, is not comfortable to good men, except the Church be blessed also, 2 Sam. 11.11.

*{and peace upon Israel}* Gal. 6.16.

# PSALM CXXIX

**Verse 1. ***{Many}* Or, *Much*.

*{may Israel now say}* The Church now afflicted ought to remember, how her condition has ever been such from the beginning, to be molested most grievously by the wicked: yet in time it has ever been delivered, Psal. 34.19.

**V. 2. ***{from my youth}* From the very first time, that I began to be a people distinguished from others, Jer. 2.2.

**V. 3. ***{The plowers plowed upon my back}* The extreme cruelty of the enemies was such, That their bodies might seem to be furrowed throughout with stripes, their oppressions were so grievous.

**V. 4. ***{The Lord is righteous}* Because God is righteous, he cannot but plague his adversaries, and deliver his, by dissipating the plots of the wicked, 2 Thess. 1.6, 7. 1 Pet. 2.9.

**V. 5. ***{Let them all be confounded}* When they see their crafty plots disappointed.

*{turned back}* By God.

*{that hate Zion}* That is, the people of God.

**V. 6. ***{as the grass upon the house tops}* He wishes, That the enemies of God, and of his people, who lift themselves most high, may be suddenly consumed with the heat of God's wrath, Psal. 37.2. and 73.19.

**V. 8. ***{Neither do they which go by, say}* That is, the wicked shall perish as that grass; to which no man wishes a blessing.

*{The blessing of the Lord be upon you}* Ruth 2.4.

# PSALM CXXX

**Verse 1. ***{Out of the depths}* Being in great distress and sorrow, he speaks as one in the depth of some mighty waters overwhelming him, Psal. 42.7. and 69.1, 2, 14. Jon. 2.5.

**V. 3. ***{If thou, Lord, shouldest mark iniquities}* So as to deal with man in extremity of justice for his sin, Psal. 123.2.

*{who shall stand}* He declares that no man can be just before God, without forgiveness of sins, Psal. 32.1. Rom. 3.23, 24.

**V. 4. ***{But there is forgiveness with thee, that thou mayest be feared}* The ordinary consequent, or effect of forgiveness is, the removing of those, whether judgments or chastisements, that we have incurred through sin. And this was David's case here, as appears by his words, verse 1. *Out of the depths;* to wit, of misery, crosses and calamities; as best Interpreters there: as also by those, verse 7. *and with him is plenteous redemption:* which are intended of deliverances from temporal calamity. His aim therefore, in saying that there is forgiveness with God, is mainly to the consequent of his forgiveness, or remission of sins; to wit, deliverance, or help in adversity. He adds therefore, *that thou mayest be feared:* that is, in effect, *ut colaris;* that you mayest be worshipped: for *fear* here, as in many other places of Scripture, is taken for the whole worship of God: which is well observed by some Expositors here. And as the Psalmist says here, that God forgives (that is, helps, and delivers) and therefore is feared; so elsewhere, that because he is feared, (or worshipped) therefore he delivers. To which purpose are those words, *Like as a father pitieth his children; so the Lord pitieth them that fear him,* Psal. 103.13.

It is a main ground of God's worship, to believe that God is merciful and gracious; that is both able, and willing to help men, (but those that fear him especially) in misery and affliction. When therefore Epicurus maintained that God was to be feared, or worshipped, *propter excellentiam naturae,* though he did not at all interest himself in human affairs, neither to do good or hurt unto any; yet to be feared, for the excellency of his Nature: wise Heathens well inferred upon it, that this was but a mere pretense of his, because he would not be thought to abolish all religion among men; but that in effect, he did absolutely deny a God; or at least, that God was to be feared or worshipped by men. *Epicurus vero ex animis hominum extraxit radicitus religionem, cum Diis immortalibus & opem & gratiam sustulit:* says one of them, who handles it at large; and concludes, *Si maxime talis est Deus, ut nulla gratia, nulla hominum charitate teneatur; valeat. Quid enim dicam propitius sit, si propitius esse potest nemini.* For what have I to do, says he, with a God that cannot be propitious? It is the very word, here used by the Latin Interpreters, *Quia apud te est propitiatio, ut timearis.* And this, *ut timearis,* being expounded more generally, *ut colaris,* as I said before it is; it is the very thing that that author does there positively maintain. Thus is the coherence, as I conceive, made very plain, which has not a little troubled Expositors, and yet still left, that way that they go commonly, in my judgment at least, very obscure. See more upon Psal. 26.3. *For thy loving kindness.*

**V. 5. ***{I wait for the Lord, my soul doth wait; and in his word do I hope}* Isa. 8.17. and 30.18.

*{in his word do I hope}* Which does assure me of God's mercy, and of the pardon of my sins.

**V. 6. ***{more than they that watch for the morning}* More than those, that watching abroad in dangerous times, and tedious weather, look frequently after the peep of day, Isa. 21.12. Psal. 119.147, 148.

*{I say, more than they that watch for the morning}* Or, *which watch unto the morning*.

**V. 7. ***{for with the Lord there is mercy}* Psal. 86.5. When I shall be assured of God's mercy, the rest of God's people may expect it, as I do.

**V. 8. ***{from all his iniquities}* He shall forgive him his sins, and so deliver him from the punishment, temporal adversity. So Psal. 103.3, 4. *Who forgiveth all thine iniquities—who redeemeth thy life from destruction,* &c.

# PSALM CXXXI

**Verse 1. ***{Mine heart is not haughty}* He sets forth his humility, as an example to rulers and governors.

*{exercised}* Heb. *walk*.

*{or in things too high for me}* Which passed the measure of his apprehension, or limits of his vocation.

*{high}* Heb. *wonderful*.

**V. 2. ***{Surely I have behaved}* The form of an oath. See Psal. 95.11.

*{myself}* Heb. *my soul*.

*{as a child that is weaned of his mother}* He was void of ambition and malicious desires; which children, so young, are not subject unto, Matt. 18.1, 3. 1 Cor. 14.20.

**V. 3. ***{from henceforth}* Heb. *from now*.

# PSALM CXXXII

**Verse 1. ***{Lord remember David}* Who was the author of this Psalm, whether David, or Solomon, is not certainly known. But either David, or Solomon, is generally agreed upon; the Book bearing the name of David, and that no other Name is here prefixed to this particular Psalm; I think it is more warrantable, if no material objection can be made against it, to ascribe it unto David himself. It is true he speaks in the third person, all the way: but a reason is given of that, because he intended it, for a set form of prayer and supplication upon a special occasion; to be used either by his son and successor Solomon, as some believe: or by the whole assembly, as others; in the like whereof we had before, Psal. 20 and 21. where David for the same reason (as there observed) speaks in the third person: *Remember thy burnt offerings,* &c.

As for this form of commending oneself to God, by this word *Remember;* we have the like Neh. 13.14, 22, 29, 31. Neither is it improbable, that Nehemiah therein did imitate David: wherein we have a further confirmation also, that David was the author. See more concerning this word and form, upon the Title of Psal. 38. *to remember.*

*{Remember David and all his afflictions}* Of all things that David did in his lifetime, among all his good acts, which made him so gracious both with God and men; and got him the reputation of an incomparable Prince upon Holy Records; there was not any thing wherein he either pleased himself more, or had more confidence in towards God, than in those things that he did for God immediately; that is, for his Ark, his Temple, and the service of his Temple, to see that settled and established in his days; or at least to provide for the settling, and establishment of it, in his son's reign. Yet strange it is to see, that notwithstanding his zeal, his piety, and the sincerity of his heart, how much he was put to it, and partly put off, and disappointed in those his godly designs.

For when he first attempted it, he was deferred by unexpected events, and durst not proceed any further: and in the mean time, God knows what inward confusion, and perplexity of spirit his soul did sustain: Three months he did continue in that case, which is very long, for such a condition. Afterwards, when he purposed to build a Temple to God, (for which who would have thought any man fitter than David? a Prince of such esteem with God, and so zealous for God, as we may boldly say, no Prince ever was more) yet even then was his personal performance in that great and glorious work, (though his purpose and desire was) not accepted of.

We would not infer from hence, that David was less dear unto God, much less, less religious, than commonly apprehended to have been: God forbid, that we should infer from this, that if good and godly Princes (as Histories will tell us of many) be disappointed of their purposes, and some of them seem to miscarry, even to the loss of their lives, and Kingdoms, when they labored and took great pains to settle religion, and to advance God's worship in a Kingdom; we should not wonder at it, and either murmur against God and his justice, because we cannot penetrate into the depth of his Counsels; or have a less opinion of the worth and piety of those Princes, because they had no better success in their pious enterprises.

David it seems, what from reason, what from divine revelation, was sufficiently secured, that God did accept of his pious endeavors, although he was not pleased to give them accomplishment, in his lifetime, and though to those that took effect, he added much trouble and affliction. For not only here, but Psal. 38 and 70, when he was in great extremity, he made bold with God, to entitle those two Psalms, (whereof see upon Psal. 38.) לזכיר *for remembrance:* as if all motives that he could possibly think of, to draw God's help and assistance, had been included in that one word, whereby he did put him in mind of his zeal for his Church, and service.

**V. 2. ***{How he}* Or, *Who*.

*{mighty God of Jacob}* Gen. 49.24.

**V. 3. ***{Surely, &c.}* Heb. *if I shall come*. The form of an oath: Let me perish if I do it, Psal. 95.11 and 131.2.

*{I will not come}* This seems very strange and unlikely (to be taken in ordinary construction) that David should so peremptorily vow, nay swear, that he would not come into his house, nor go to bed, &c. until he had done that; which could neither be done in one day, in case he had had good warrant for the doing of it; neither had any certainty, that it should be done and accomplished (as who can be, of anything future?) whilst he lived.

Junius therefore very plausibly expounds David not of his ordinary house, or bed: but of a stately new house, the building whereof he conceives intimated, 2 Sam. 5.9. and 1 Chron. 15.1. the use whereof David might well forbear for a season; or if it had so fallen out, altogether abstain from it, rather than to break his oath. I said it was plausible: but some objections may be made against it; neither do I find him followed in it by other Interpreters. Some conceive that the words may be somewhat hyperbolical, and that by *swearing* here, is only intended an earnest desire, and fixed resolution: and again, *If I come into my house,* &c. that is, to rest and enjoy myself with a contented mind: if I make it not my care, and my business; and think of it night and day, &c.

In very deed, a man may go very far in his expressions in that kind, and deliver some things absolutely, which nevertheless are presupposed to be understood upon some general conditions, or suppositions, upon the failing of which, the obligation of performance may be thought void. But in bargains or covenants between man and man, we are more precisely tied to words, there is a reason for it. But this may serve here, as to David's words.

*{into the tabernacle}* Kings' houses are but tabernacles, no abiding places, Heb. 13.14.

*{of my house}* He means, of his new house, 2 Sam. 5.9. 1 Chron. 15.1. he would neither dwell nor sleep in it, till the ark were in his resting place; that is, he would never do so quietly, till that were done.

**V. 5. ***{Until I find out a place for the Lord}* That is, the ark, which was a sign of God's presence, Num. 10.35, 36.

*{an habitation for the mighty God of Jacob}* Heb. *habitations*; that is, a stately habitation of divers rooms, ver. 7.

**V. 6. ***{we heard of it at Ephratah}* The Ark was first placed in Shiloh, in the Tribe of Ephraim: which Tribe, some Rabbins conceive to be here intended by this word Ephratah. But it is but a conjecture, which has little ground. For Ephratah may be the name of a City, otherwise called Bethlehem: the reason of both names being from the fertility of the place. The meaning, according to best Interpreters, is rather this; We inhabitants of Bethlehem, or Ephratah, we have heard of it; the fame of this solemn bringing of the Ark (whereof see before upon Psal. 47 and 68,) is come to us also: *it was found in Kiriath-jearim,* &c. See upon the next words.

*{in the fields of the wood}* Or rather, in Kiriath-jearim; the name of the place where the Ark was, when it was brought to Jerusalem by David, 1 Sam. 6.21 and 7.1, 2. Kiriath-jearim, properly signifies a Town or City, situated near woods: and such a one was this, that was so called. The sense had been much plainer so: but some would have Jerusalem understood by this expression; which is not so likely.

**V. 7. ***{we will worship at his footstool}* Meaning, before his ark.

*{worship at his footstool}* Psal. 99.5.

**V. 8. ***{Arise, O Lord, into thy rest}* Num. 10.35. 1 Chron. 6.41. Psal. 68.1. See there.

*{into thy rest}* Jerusalem; because that afterward his ark should remove to no other place.

*{the ark of thy strength}* Whence you show your strength, in hearing the prayer of your servants, and relieving them. See upon Psal. 63.2. *To see thy power and thy strength.*

**V. 9. ***{Let thy Priests be clothed with righteousness}* Let the effect of your grace so appear, both in the Priests, and in the people, that it, as evidently, be seen on them, and grace them, as a comely garment does him that wears it, Job 29.14. Isa. 61.10.

**V. 10. ***{For thy servant David's sake}* verse 1. 1 Kings 8.25. Psal. 89.20, &c. Isa. 37.35. Your covenant made to him, made good also those in the temporal estate to others of his seed, so far forth as they kept the covenant.

**V. 11. ***{Of the fruit of thy body will I set upon thy throne}* 2 Sam. 7.12. 1 Kings 8.25. 2 Chron. 6.16. Luke 1.69. Acts 2.30.

*{thy body}* Heb. *your belly, or womb*, as Prov. 31.2.

**V. 12. ***{for evermore}* Because this cannot be accomplished, but in Christ, Acts 2.30. it follows, that the promise was spiritual. But see more upon Psal. 72. *Give the King thy judgements,* &c. upon the title there; about the end.

**V. 13. ***{desired it for his habitation}* Psal. 68.16.

**V. 14. ***{This is my rest for ever}* See upon Psal. 68.16. *Yea the Lord will dwell in it for ever.*

*{for I have desired it}* Meaning, for his own sake, and not for any outward excellency in the place itself.

**V. 15. ***{abundantly}* Or, *surely*.

*{I will satisfy her poor with bread}* God will provide for the meanest in his Church.

**V. 16. ***{I will also clothe her Priests with salvation}* That is, with my protection, whereby they shall be safe.

**V. 17. ***{There will I make the horn of David to bud}* Luke 1.69. Though his force and glory for a time, seemed to be broken; yet God promises to restore them to him, and settle them.

*{lamp}* Or, *candle*. By this word, in Scripture style, is understood offspring or posterity. 1 Kings 11.36. *that David my servant may have a light* (or, *candle*) *alway before me in Jerusalem,* and 15.4. *Nevertheless, for David's sake did the Lord his God give him a lamp* (or candle) *in Jerusalem, to set up his son after him,* &c.

**V. 18. ***{His enemies will I clothe with shame}* I will clothe the one with shame, as the other with honor, verse 6; that is, put them to open and public shame, Psal. 109.29.

# PSALM CXXXIII

**Verse 1. ***{How good}* How profitable and pleasant.

*{for brethren to dwell together in unity}* Of the same faith or family, Heb. 13.1. Gen. 13.8.

*{together}* even together.

**V. 2. ***{like the precious}* He shows, by these similitudes, the commodity of brotherly love.

*{ointment upon the head}* He alludes to the fragrant ointments, Exod. 30.23. and 40.13.

*{that went down to the skirts of his garments}* Or rather, *upon the collar,* Exod. 28.32. and 39.23. for it is not likely, that so much precious oil should be poured on him, as should run down to the bottom of his robe.

**V. 3. ***{As the dew of Hermon}* The dew that fell upon those mountains, made them or the lands adjoining fruitful: so does concord bring plenty.

*{for there the Lord commanded the blessing}* Where there is such an agreement.

*{commanded the blessing}* Psal. 42.8. and 44.4. and 71.3. See upon Psal. 68.28. *Thy God hath commanded thy strength,* &c.

*{and life for evermore}* That is, constant happiness, perpetuated in, and by a blessed posterity.

# PSALM CXXXIV

**Verse 1. ***{All ye servants of the Lord}* Who are they, to whom the Psalmist does here address himself, with this title *servants of the Lord,* I find three several opinions. Some think the Levites only, because of them particularly, which we do not of others, we read that they had charge of the Temple by night, as well as by day, 1 Chron. 9.33. Others, take no notice of this, but because they conceive the last verse, *The Lord that made,* &c. to contain a set form of blessing, and that it belonged only to the Priests to bless the people, Num. 6.23. therefore they would have the Priests only intended: though afterwards they take in the Levites too; as if they also, but upon what ground they tell us not, had had a commission to bless. For certainly, Num. 6.23. by Aaron and his sons, the Priests only can be intended: as is further confirmed, Deut. 21.5. *And the Priests,—For them the Lord thy God hath chosen—to bless,* &c.

For as for Deut. 10.8. where *to minister, and to bless,* is promiscuously ascribed to the tribe of Levi in general, as in such expressions is ordinary: it must according to best Expositors be understood distinctly: *to minister and to bless;* that is, the Levites to minister; and the Priests, to bless: both Priests and Levites, so properly called, being of one and the same tribe, the tribe of Levi. Which nevertheless is not without some difficulty, or ambiguity, at least; because *to minister* was common to the Priests and Levites; as by many places of the Law does appear. But we will not insist so long upon this particular, as to answer all objections.

The more common, and probable opinion is, that it belonged unto the Priests only to bless the people. But that this last verse should be so taken, for a form of blessing, there is no necessity of that. The words may be taken, as some Expositors would have them, as from the Psalmist to the servants of the Lord, whoever intended, blessing them in great good will for their diligence and assiduity in praising God; whether of themselves, as is most likely, though he seem to exhort them to it; which may be rather an approbation: according to that of the Poet, *Qui monet ut facias, quod jam facis ipse,* &c.; or whether upon his exhortation, for their speedy performance and execution.

But lastly, there be also, who think that by *servants of the Lord,* are meant here, not only Priests and Levites, but other devout people, who in great zeal and devotion to God's service, commended in Anna, a Prophetess widow, Luke 2.36, 37. were wont to be conversant in the Temple both nights and days, in fasting and praying, and other religious duties. All that I find objected against this, is the last verse, if it be a set formula of blessing: but that being very doubtful, and as likely not to be, as has been said, the objection cannot be very strong. There is none of those opinions, but has its probability; so that the Reader, for ought I know, may be left free to his choice; which has made me to set them down all three.

*{which by night}* Heb. *in the night*. The day is not here mentioned: not but that it is intended, as well as the night; as elsewhere upon this occasion, expressed often; but because it is supposed that the night being the more unusual time, if in the nights, much more in the day: when all men, that do not make a practice of atheism and Epicurism, are wont to praise God, some part of it; morning and evening, at least.

That *to stand* in the Scripture, is sometimes for to wait, minister, or serve. *For the Priests and the Levites that stood:* see in the Hebrew, as the margin tells you: for which in the Text itself, *that waited.* This is some reason, why Priests and Levites should be understood in the former words, rather than others: but no concluding reason, because *to stand,* in that sense, is a general word, used of others, as well as of Priests and Levites, but indeed most proper unto them, whose the House of the Lord is particularly spoken of. Yet it may be too, that the word might be used of purpose here, of them that were not such, to show, that which they did, was in imitation of those, whom God did own more particularly, above other men; for that extraordinary service which they were bound unto, above other men: in imitation of them, that God might own them also, after a more particular manner, as out of all question he does such, for their zeal to Him, and his service.

**V. 2. ***{Lift up your hands in the Sanctuary}* There is neither *in,* nor *to,* in the Original, but seeing that one of the two must of necessity be supplied, there is more probability for the *to,* as I conceive, and so I find it in most interpretations, than for this *in.* See before upon Psal. 5.7. *towards thy holy Temple.* But then by the Holy, as in the original, or holiness, we must understand the Holy of Holies, or the Holy Ark: where God was supposed to reside, and from whence he was said to hear prayers: as has been observed before more than once.

**V. 3. ***{The Lord}* The Levites answer to the former exhortation, and their prayer for the people in their night-watches: suitable to the Priest's blessing, Num. 6.24. See upon the former verses.

*{that made heaven and earth}* And therefore has all power, Bless you with his Fatherly love, declared in Zion, Psal. 128.5.

# PSALM CXXXV

**Verse 2. ***{Ye that stand in the house of the Lord}* You Priests that serve God in the Temple, and you Levites that do it without in the Courts. Others, think the people to be meant by them, that stand in the Courts, Psal. 134.1, 2. Luke 1.10. See upon Psal. 134.1. *All ye servants:* and, *stand in the house.*

**V. 4. ***{For the Lord hath chosen Jacob}* That is, has freely loved the posterity of Abraham, 1 Cor. 8.5.

*{chosen Jacob unto himself}* Deut. 4.37. and 7.6, 7. and 10.15. and 26.18.

**V. 5. ***{our Lord is above all gods}* Above all that are called gods by resemblance of dignity, or false opinion of men.

*{above all gods}* Psal. 95.3. and 97.9.

**V. 6. ***{Whatsoever the Lord pleased, that did he in heaven and in earth}* Psal. 115.3.

*{that did he in heaven and in earth}* He joins God's power with his Will; that we should not separate them: and hereby he wills God's people to depend on his power, which he confirms by effects of it.

**V. 7. ***{He causeth the vapours to ascend from the ends of the earth}* Jer. 10.13.

*{he maketh lightnings for the rain}* To make way for the rain, or, to show that rain is coming.

**V. 8. ***{Who smote the firstborn of Egypt}* Exod. 12.29.

*{both of man and beast}* Heb. *from man unto beast*.

**V. 10. ***{Who smote great nations, and slew mighty Kings}* Num. 21.24, 25, 26, 34, 35.

**V. 12. ***{And gave their land for an heritage}* Josh. 12.7. He shows what good the godly receive by God's power, whereby he destroys their enemies, and provides for them.

**V. 13. ***{thy memorial, O Lord, throughout all generations}* Psal. 102.12. Exod. 3.15.

*{throughout all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation*.

**V. 14. ***{For the Lord will judge his people}* Deut. 32.36. Psal. 26.1. He shall avenge them of their enemies, Psal. 43.1.

*{he will repent himself concerning his servants}* He will take away the plagues he lays on them.

**V. 15. ***{The idols of the heathen are silver and gold}* Psal. 115.4–11. He warns his people to take heed they leave not him, to serve Idols, that can neither hear nor help them.

# PSALM CXXXVI

**Verse 1. ***{O Give thanks unto the Lord for he is good, &c.}* Psal. 106.1. and 107.1. and 118.1.

*{for his mercy endureth for ever}* Though we are to praise God in, and for all his attributes, as power, his justice, wisdom, and the like: yet this repetition throughout the whole Psalm shows, that God is chiefly praised for his mercy and goodness, whereof see upon Psal. 130.4. *For there is forgiveness with thee,* &c.; whether general, to all mankind, of which see upon Psal. 71.6. *from the womb,* and upon Psal. 107. in the argument of the Psalm, and upon Psal. 117.1. *O praise the Lord;* or particular, to his Church; wherein the riches of his mercy does most eminently appear.

*{for his mercy endureth}* This to have been a common constant form, as well for ordinary daily praises in divine service; as for extraordinary occasions, may appear by 1 Chron. 16.41. 2 Chron. 7.6. Jer. 33.11. and 2 Chron. 5.13. and 7.3. Ezra 3.11. and other places of Scripture.

**V. 2. ***{God of gods}* Deut. 10.17.

**V. 5. ***{To him that by wisdom made the heavens}* Gen. 1.1. Prov. 3.19.

**V. 6. ***{To him that stretched out the earth above the waters}* Gen. 1.9. Jer. 10.12. See upon Psal. 24.2. *upon the seas.*

**V. 7. ***{To him that made great lights}* Gen. 1.14.

**V. 8. ***{to rule by day}* Heb. *for the rulings by day*.

**V. 10. ***{To him that smote Egypt in their firstborn}* Exod. 12.29.

**V. 11. ***{And brought out Israel from among them}* Exod. 13.17. God's merciful providence for the good of man, appears in all his creatures: but chiefly, in delivering his Church out of the hand of their enemies.

**V. 12. ***{With a strong hand}* Exod. 6.6.

*{with a stretched out arm}* A similitude taken from soldiers, who stretch out their arm to use their full might and best strength in exercise of arms.

**V. 13. ***{To him which divided the red Sea into parts}* Exod. 14.21, 22.

**V. 15. ***{But overthrew Pharaoh and his host in the red sea}* Exod. 14.28.

*{overthrew}* Heb. *shaked off*.

**V. 16. ***{To him that led his people through the wilderness}* Exod. 15.22.

*{through the wilderness}* Where, for the space of forty years, he showed infinite and most strange wonders.

**V. 18. ***{And slew famous Kings}* Deut. 29.7. Psal. 135.10, 11. Declaring thereby, That no power nor authority was so dear unto him, as the love of his Church.

**V. 19. ***{Sihon king of the Amorites}* Num. 21.23.

**V. 20. ***{And Og the King of Bashan}* Num. 21.33.

**V. 21. ***{And gave their land for an heritage}* Josh. 12.7.

**V. 23. ***{Who remembered us in our low estate}* In our greatest affliction and slavery, when we looked for nothing less, than to have had any succor, Psal. 113.7.

**V. 25. ***{Who giveth food to all flesh}* Psal. 104.27. and 144.15. and 147.9. Seeing that God provides even for beasts, much more has he care over his, Matt. 6.26.

**V. 26. ***{O give thanks unto the God of heaven}* Because all ages have had most plain testimonies of God's benefits.

# PSALM CXXXVII

**Verse 1. ***{There we sat down}* That is, we abode a long time; and albeit that the country was pleasant, yet could we not stay our tears, nor turn us from the true service of God: or, sat down as men oppressed with grief, Ezra 9.3. Lam. 3.28.

*{remembered Zion}* Psal. 42.4, 6. and 102.14. Lam. 1.7.

**V. 2. ***{in the midst thereof}* To wit, of that country.

**V. 3. ***{required of us a song}* The Babylonians spoke thus in mocking us, as though, by our silence, we should signify, that we hoped no more in God.

*{a song}* Heb. the words of a song.

*{wasted us}* Heb. *laid us on heaps*; as Psal. 79.1. *they laid Jerusalem in heaps.* The Hebrew word here has been among the Rabbins much controverted. Some would have it to be a noun substantive, from a root signifying *to hang up,* which word is used before, verse 2. *We hanged our harps,* &c. So by *suspensoribus nostris,* should be intended the instruments of Music, that hanged up: that they required them to sing, and to play on their instruments. But most Christian Interpreters are agreed upon it, that it is a participle rather, or *nomen participiale,* from a word signifying *to lay in heaps,* as we have it here in the English; which is much more agreeable to the Original Context.

There be also, who think that the word may be here a Chaldaic word for the Hebrew equivalent, it being very ordinary to that Dialect, to change one Hebrew letter into another. And because the Chaldeans were they, that had been the authors of this mischief, therefore they think a Chaldee word, see somewhat to this purpose, upon Psal. 74.6. upon those words, *and hammers,* was used of purpose. But if this be granted, we have still the same sense; putting but *spoilers,* instead of *wasters.*

**V. 4. ***{strange land}* Heb. *land of a stranger*.

**V. 5. ***{If I forget thee, O Jerusalem}* Albeit the faithful are touched with their particular griefs, yet the common sorrow of the Church is most grievous unto them, and is such, as they cannot but remember and lament.

**V. 6. ***{above my chief joy}* The decay of God's Religion in their country was so grievous, that no joy could make them glad, except it were restored; and then they would rejoice as much as now they grieved.

*{my chief joy}* Heb. *the head of my joy*.

**V. 7. ***{the children of Edom}* Obad. 10, &c. According as Ezek. 25.12. Jer. 49.7. is prophesied: and Obad. ver. 12. shows, that the Edomites conspired with the Babylonians against the Jews.

*{in the day of Jerusalem}* When you did visit Jerusalem, Ezek. 7.12. Isa. 13.13.

*{Rase it}* Heb. *make bare*.

**V. 8. ***{destroyed}* Heb. *wasted*.

*{that rewardeth thee, as thou hast served us}* Heb. *that recompenseth unto you your deed which you did to us*.

**V. 9. ***{Happy shall he be that taketh}* He alludes to the prophecy, Isa. 13.16. promising good success to Cyrus and Darius, whom, though ambition moved to fight against Babylon, yet God used, as his rods, to punish his enemies, for their cruelty to his people.

*{dasheth thy little ones against the stones}* Isa. 13.16.

*{the stones}* Heb. *the rock*.

# PSALM CXXXVIII

**Verse 1. ***{Before the gods}* Either Angels, which the Jews of old and ancient Christians did believe to be present at religious assemblies; which also seems, according to learned Expositors, to have been St. Paul's opinion, 1 Cor. 11.10. *For this cause ought the woman,* &c. Or Kings, called Gods, Psal. 82.6. and 96.4. according to Psal. 119.46. *I will speak of thy testimonies,* &c. See also on ver. 4. *All the Kings of the earth,* &c.

**V. 2. ***{towards thy holy temple}* Psal. 28.2. 1 Kings 8.29, 30. John 4. Both the temple, and ceremonial service, by Christ's coming were abolished: and now none need to look toward the temple; but worship God in spirit and truth, in every place, John 4.21. 1 Tim. 2.8. However, it does not hence follow, but that certain places are to be appropriated to God for divine service, which we commonly call Churches: and that all reverence is due to such places, by all truly devout and religious: according to St. Paul's doctrine, 1 Cor. 11.22. *Have ye not houses—or despise ye the Church of God:* as best Interpreters, not ancient only, all, or most of them; but modern also, some of the most learned, and of best account, generally, do understand the word *church,* in that place. See also upon Psal. 69.9. *For the zeal of thy house,* &c.

*{for thou hast magnified thy word above all thy Name}* The construction of the words in the Original is somewhat ambiguous. It may be translated, either, *thy name above all things,* as by divers; or, *above all thy Name,* as here, and by some others: That is, say they; You have made thyself more known by your Word, than by any other way. Then, according to the first, the last word is either an ablative, *verbo tuo,* that is, *by thy word;* You have magnified your Name above all things by your Word: or joined to Name, by apposition, *thy Name, thy Word;* that is, *thy Name, and thy Word.*

David Kimchi makes a kind of *hysteron proteron* of it, making this to be the right order, as to the sense; *thou hast magnified thy Name super omne verbum tuum,* above, or in every word of your. Junius, *cum toto nomine tuo verbum tuum.* Which of all these is rightest, is not easily decided: but *thy Name above all things, by thy word;* that is, by this gracious performance of what you have promised; seems the most literal Interpretation: and having said but now, that *the Name* of God should be the object of his praises; it is not so likely, that he would presently upon it magnify anything else, above his Name.

**V. 3. ***{and strengthenedst me with strength in my soul}* You did strengthen me against all mine enemies, inward and outward.

**V. 4. ***{All the kings of the earth shall praise thee}* The kings themselves shall praise you, when they shall know you fully, as you have revealed thyself in your word to us. Or, as others more probably, divers kings of sundry nations, that have been acquainted through neighborhood, or otherwise, with the affairs of this Commonwealth, where they shall hear that by your special appointment, long before declared; and by the concurrence of many miraculous events, of a shepherd, 2 Sam. 7.8. and Psal. 78.70, 71. I am become a King, they shall glorify you. So much may be implied, 1 Chron. 14.17. *And the fame of David went out into all lands,* &c. See also upon Psal. 98.2. *his righteousness,* &c. To this also may verse 1. *before the Gods,* &c. be referred.

**V. 5. ***{they shall sing in the ways of the Lord}* They shall sing when they shall be converted. Or rather, *They shall sing of the ways:* as some translate; but even those that do not agree upon the same interpretation, to wit, They shall talk of these things, that have happened unto me, with acknowledgment and admiration of God's power and providence in it. See upon the former verse.

**V. 6. ***{Though the Lord be high, yet he hath respect unto the lowly}* Isa. 57.15.

*{afar off}* God though he dwell on high, yet is near to the lowly; but keeps aloof from the proud, Prov. 3.34. James 4.6. 1 Pet. 5.5.

**V. 8. ***{The Lord will perfect that which concerneth me}* Psal. 57.2. Phil. 1.6. Though mine enemies rage never so much, yet the Lord which has begun his work in me, will continue his favor to the end.

*{forsake not the works of thine own hands}* Job 10.8.

# PSALM CXXXIX

**Verse 1. ***{Searched me}* Jer. 12.3.

**V. 2. ***{Thou knowest my downsitting}* He confesses, that neither our actions, thoughts, nor any part of our life can be hid from God.

*{thou understandest my thought afar off}* Long before I conceive it.

**V. 3. ***{Thou compassest my path}* So that they are evidently known to you.

*{compassest}* Or, *winnowest*.

**V. 4. ***{there is not a word in my tongue}* You know my meaning before I speak: or, my secret whisperings.

**V. 5. ***{and laid thine hand upon me}* Job 41.8. You keepest me within the compass of your knowledge, like a man that will not let his servant go out of his sight. Or, if this besetting, &c. be a similitude from the manner of huntsmen, when they are in pursuit of some wild beast, as some not improbably think; then this, *and laid thine hand upon me,* must be understood in reference to the same similitude, as Job 41.8. *Lay thine hand upon him,* &c.

**V. 7. ***{Whither shall I go from thy Spirit?}* From your power and knowledge?

**V. 8. ***{If I ascend up into heaven; thou art there, &c.}* Amos 9.2, 3, 4.

**V. 9. ***{If I take the wings of the morning}* He means the Sun-beams, which suddenly fly to the earth: or, wings that could fly as swift as they shoot out, and take flight never so early.

**V. 10. ***{Even there shall thy hand}* Your power does so fast hold me, that I can escape by no means from you.

*{lead me}* He shows, that God's providence over us is not a bare knowledge, but an effectual conducting and disposing of us.

**V. 11. ***{even the night shall be light about me}* Though darkness be an hindrance to man's sight, yet it serves your eyes, as well as the light.

**V. 12. ***{the darkness hideth not from thee}* Job 26.6. Heb. 4.13.

*{hideth not}* Heb. *darkeneth not*.

*{the darkness and the light are both alike}* Heb. *as is the darkness, so is the light*.

**V. 13. ***{For thou hast possessed}* You have made me in all parts; and therefore must needs know me.

*{my reins}* My affections, and most inward motions.

*{thou hast covered me in my mother's womb}* With flesh and skin, Job 10.11.

**V. 14. ***{for I am fearfully and wonderfully made}* Considering your wonderful work in forming me, I cannot but praise you, and fear your mighty power. See before upon Psal. 8.2. *Out of the mouth of babes,* &c. and Psal. 22.9. *that took me out of the womb.*

*{right}* Heb. *greatly*.

**V. 15. ***{substance}* Or, *strength, or body*.

*{and curiously wrought}* Like a piece of needle-work: for so the word signifies.

*{in the lowest parts of the earth}* That is in my mother's womb, here below on earth, Eph. 4.9. Or, as secretly, as things formed in the bowels of the earth, Job 28.1, 2, &c. Which does not only commend the wonderful are of the workman, who can do his work without help of light; which some conceive to be the principal aim: but also sets out the incomprehensibleness of it to human understanding. For so the Grecians, of things very abstruse, and out of the reach of man's wit and capacity, use to say proverbially, *τὰ γῆς ὑπό,* that is, *things under the earth;* or, *the deep things of the earth.* See also upon Psal. 63.9. *the lower parts of the earth.*

**V. 16. ***{and in thy book all my members were written}* An allusion to curious workmen, that work after a model set before them.

*{all my members}* Heb. *all of them*.

*{which in continuance were fashioned}* Or, *what days they should be fashioned*.

*{when as yet there were none of them}* Seeing that you did know me before I was composed of either flesh or bone, much more now must you know me, when you have fashioned me.

**V. 17. ***{How precious also are thy thoughts unto me}* Psal. 40.5. How ought I to esteem in my thoughts the excellent declaration of your wisdom in the creation of man? or, how incomprehensible are your counsels? Rom. 11.33. Job 26.14.

*{thy thoughts}* Or, *thoughts of you*.

**V. 18. ***{I am still with thee}* Every morning when I awake, I see new occasions to meditate of your wisdom and to praise you. Or, when I have spent the whole day, day after day, in the consideration of these things, the next morning I am where I was; I am but at the beginning still: the more I think of it, the more wonderful it appears to me. As that ancient Philosopher, who being asked what God was; first asked one day to think of it: then two, then four, &c. because the more he thought of God, the more he understood him, as himself answered, incomprehensible.

**V. 19. ***{Surely}* Heb. *if*, Psal. 131.2.

*{Surely thou wilt slay the wicked, O God}* It seems, that which had put David at this time to this solemn profession, and ample declaration of his full persuasion of God being the searcher of the hearts of men, as he that is the maker of men, &c. was some horrid imputation, which he took much to heart, from some one or other of his bitter adversaries, as though he had been in very deed, notwithstanding all his fair professions and pretenses of zeal and piety, a wicked, bloody man. We know Shimei did once object it unto him, *Come out, come out, thou bloody man, and thou man of Belial.*

*The Lord hath returned upon thee all the blood of the house of Saul,* &c. 2 Sam. 16.7, 8. and it is more than likely that he met with many such, as by his many complaints and earnest provocations to God in divers of his Psalms, may be collected. Having therefore hitherto declared his faith at large, concerning God's Omniscience, &c. he now appeals to his justice, and before God, makes profession of his innocency.

**V. 20. ***{and thine enemies take thy Name in vain}* The words may also, as they are by some, be translated, *and thine enemies lift up* themselves, or their heads, *in vain.* Or, as by others, *they vainly extol thine enemies.* The Reader may take his choice: the words will afford it, and other parallel places will be found, what sense soever of these he pitches upon, to make it probable.

*{therefore}* Or, *them*.

**V. 21. ***{Do not I hate them, O Lord, that hate thee?}* His zeal of God's glory wrought in him a fiery indignation, and utter detestation of all such as opposed it, regarding not so much his own particular, as God's interest, Psal. 69.9. and 119.158. 2 Chron. 19.2. Prov. 29.27.

**V. 23. ***{Search me O God, and know my heart}* Verse 1. Psal. 26.2.

**V. 24. ***{see if there be any wicked way in me}* Psal. 7.4.

*{any wicked way in me}* Any course of sin that is grievous to God or man.

*{wicked way}* Heb. *way of pain, or grief*.

*{lead me in the way everlasting}* Heb. בדרך עולם, by most translated, *in via seculi;* or, *per viam seculi,* that is, say they, to the end of my life. Others, *in via antiqua:* that is, *in the old way:* as Jer. 6.16. נתיבות עולם is translated, *the old way.* The old way, that is the good and religious way; to which some would have דרך עצב in the beginning of the verse; which may be also turned, *the way of grief,* or *pain,* as in the margin, by which they understand idolatry, opposed. But though the words may be drawn to this, for עצבים is taken for idols sometimes, yet it does not seem so proper to this place. Bucer agrees with our English and Junius, *via perpetua.* See upon Psal. 24.7. *ye everlasting doors.*

# PSALM CXL

**Verse 1. ***{From the violent man}* Which persecutes me of mere malice, and without cause.

*{violent man}* Heb. *man of violences*: that is, most violent.

**V. 2. ***{Which imagine mischiefs in their heart}* They study how to bring the greatest mischief on me; that is, to kill me.

*{war}* Heb. *wars*: that is, frequent ones.

**V. 3. ***{adders poison is under their lips}* Psal. 58.4. Rom. 3.13. He shows how craftily wicked men proceed by calumnies, when they want power to do hurt.

**V. 4. ***{Keep me, O Lord, from the hands of the wicked}* God is the refuge of the godly, when they are oppressed by worldlings.

*{from the violent man}* See Notes on ver. 1.

**V. 5. ***{snare for me}* Psal. 35.7. and 57.6. and 141.9.

**V. 7. ***{thou hast covered my head in the day of battle}* He calls to God with lively faith, being assured of his mercies, because he had before-time proved, that God helped him ever in his dangers, covering his head as with an helmet, or shield, Psal. 71.4.

**V. 8. ***{further not his wicked device}* For wicked men cannot accomplish their plots, unless God give them strength.

*{lest they exalt themselves}* Or, *let them not be exalted*.

**V. 9. ***{as for the head of those that compass me about}* He desires that God would make their own words their bane, Psal. 64.8. or, let them perish by those courses that themselves plot and combine in: or, let their own imprecations fall on them, Psal. 109.17. The words may be translated also, which is followed by some of the best Interpreters: *The head of him* by which some understand Doeg; others, Saul; *that compasseth me about, let the mischief,* or perversity, *of his own lips cover him.* See upon the next words.

*{let the mischief of their own lips}* Life and death are often ascribed in the Scripture to the tongue: or, which is equivalent, to the mouth or lips. And not without cause. For either a man speaks what his heart does prompt unto him; as all men naturally should, Matt. 12.34. *out of the abundance of the heart,* &c. Or if he be a dissembler, that is, one whose heart and tongue go not together; a crafty cunning man; it is a great chance but some time or other he will be caught in his double dealing, and reap the fruits of his false heart, or tongue. Much more might be said of the tongue, of which so much is in the Scripture: but more cannot, than is by St. James, chap. 3. ver. 1, 2, &c. to ver. 13. See also Psal. 17.3. Prov. 12.13. and 18.20, 21.

**V. 10. ***{Let burning coals fall upon them}* If a man read these two verses, 9 and 10, together, and consider diligently; he may, though contrary to the current of Expositors, as may appear by what has been noted upon verse tenth, he may, I say, have just occasion to suspect, especially if he take Prov. 25.21, 22. *For thou shalt heap coals of fire upon his head,* &c. into his consideration; that the meaning of the words should rather be this: *As for those that compass me about,* &c. *Let burning coals fall upon their heads,* &c.

And though, as I said before, the current of Expositors go another way: yet some very judicious, or more plainly, Calvin upon the place, proposes an interpretation that comes very near to this; but that he misses of the place in the Proverbs, which if he had thought of, it is likely, he would have been much more confident. But then if we ground upon that place, as I think we must, if we think to carry it against so many, for this interpretation here; it must be granted, that as the words here, are an imprecation; so there also: which here, though the coherence of matter, besides the tenor of the words themselves, does evince them to be so: yet there, seems very contrary to the purpose of the wise author, whom we would not willingly believe to be the author of any such instruction, that relishes of any malice: much more contrary to the purpose and profession of Evangelical Paul, who, though he borrow the words from Solomon, yet delivers the matter of them as his own, Rom. 12.20.

See what is there noted at large in vindication of both, upon Prov. 25.21, 22. neither do we know anything now, that should move us to change our opinion there delivered concerning the right meaning of those words, except it should be this one passage of the Psalmist: which yet being so doubtful of itself, and so indifferently expounded by all, or most Expositors; I know not of what validity it may be apprehended by others, against the pregnancy, as I conceive, of so much reason that may sway us for a different sense in Solomon, and St. Paul.

And yet were it certain and sure, that both the Psalmist, and his son aimed at one and the same proverbial expression; yet is it not without example, that one and the same expression of words; yea one and the same proverb, without any variation of words, may be used in a different sense, and to a different end. Of words and phrases, being the same, used in a different, yea contrary sense, in Scripture, and elsewhere, I could give many examples: and some have been given in these Notes, upon occasion. Of Proverbs, or proverbial speeches, if my memory serve me not at this time, I doubt not but with a little help either of books, or better memory, there may be found good store.

And this I should rather pitch upon, than to believe that either Solomon, or St. Paul could intend the words in a precept of charity, so, to ordinary construction, and their authority laid aside, uncharitably. But now I have dealt so freely with the Reader, for indeed I would not conceal any truth, whether it make for, or against any opinion of mine, I shall now deal as freely and truly with him, in professing that this interpretation of the words of the Psalmist, though at first, upon comparing with Solomon's words, very plausible; yet upon further consideration, appears otherwise unto me.

I did mention Calvin before, that he had an Exposition that came very near. It is this: *Recidat molestia, quam sceleratis sermonibus mihi intentant, in capita ipsorum.* Here Calvin does not tell us, what shall become of the word *operiat,* which is set out by him; and instead of it *Recidat* put in his place. But I suppose he intended it, though for brevity's sake, he did not so fully explain himself, thus: Let the mischief, which they intended unto me by their wicked tongues, cover their own heads, which is in effect; *Recidat molestia:* &c. The evil which they intended unto me, let it fall upon their own heads.

It is plausible enough of itself, as he proposes it: but much more probable, in my judgment, if he had observed, which I wonder he does not, that there is in the words, so ordered and construed, an allusion to verse 7. *thou hast covered my head in the day of battle.* Many such secret allusions of words there be in the Scripture, of another kind, see before upon Psal. 75.6. *nor from the south:* of which we have treated elsewhere largely. If this therefore be right, as it seems unto me, very plausible; *Let burning coals fall upon them,* in the next verse, must be construed by itself; and so the expression here apparently different from Solomon's.

Most Expositors are of opinion, that the Psalmist does here allude to that fire that comes from heaven, to the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah: as the most dreadful imprecation he could think of: which is very likely. Of imprecations in general, in this Book of Psalms, See before upon Psal. 35. ver. 4. *Let them be confounded.* Of Solomon's words and meaning, see there: Prov. 25.21, 22.

*{let them be cast into the fire}* Heb. *let him cast*: to wit, God: and let all manner of mischief befall them, and so overwhelm them, that they may never recover again.

**V. 11. ***{an evil speaker}* Heb. *a man of tongue*.

*{an evil speaker be established in the earth: evil shall hunt the violent man to overthrow him}* Or, *an evil speaker, a wicked man of violence be established in the earth: let him be hunted to his overthrow.* See upon Psal. 55.23. *bloody and deceitful men.*

*{evil shall hunt the violent man to overthrow him}* God's plagues shall so from place to place pursue him; as he shall have no way to escape, Jer. 16.16.

*{overthrow}* Heb. *to destructions*: that is, to certain destruction.

**V. 12. ***{the upright shall dwell in thy presence}* That is, shall be defended and preserved by your fatherly providence and care, as one of your family.

# PSALM CXLI

**Verse 1. ***{I cry unto thee}* He shows, that there is no other refuge in our necessities, but only to fly unto God for comfort of soul or body.

**V. 2. ***{set forth}* Heb. *directed*.

*{the lifting up of my hands as the evening sacrifice}* Psal. 134.2. He desires to have his prayers as acceptable to God, as the incense and sacrifices were in the Sanctuary, Psal. 69.31. Mal. 1.11. Rev. 8.4.

**V. 3. ***{keep the door of my lips}* He desires God to keep his mouth from speaking unadvisedly; for of himself he could not do it, Psal. 39.1.

**V. 4. ***{Let me not eat of their dainties}* Let not their prosperity allure me to be wicked, as they are, Psal. 37.1. and 73.15.

**V. 5. ***{Let the righteous smite me, it shall be a kindness, and let him reprove me, it shall be an excellent oil, which shall not break my head}* Or, *Let the righteous smite me kindly, and reprove me; let not their precious oil break my head,* &c.

*{and let him reprove me}* He could well endure all reproofs, that came from a loving heart, Prov. 9.8. and 19.25. and 25.12.

*{an excellent oil}* Heb. *oil of head*, Eccles. 9.8. He compares a faithful and friendly reproof, to such an ointment as they used to anoint their heads withal.

*{it shall not break my head}* Oil cannot properly be said, as oil, to break the head. But oil being here metaphorically taken for words of reproof, which may be said, though figuratively too, to break the head, or soul, as Job 19.2. *How long will ye vex my soul, and break me in pieces with words?* what belongs to words properly, is here transferred to oil. Some translate, which the Original will bear too, *Let it never cease from my head:* that is, let me never want it.

*{for yet my prayer shall be in their calamities}* So far shall their reproofs be from offending, that I will requite them with my best prayers, when they most need them; that is, in their calamities, when the malicious, uncharitable, if they bear any small grudge, are most apt to insult. Some more emphatically, but to the same purpose; *Quò magis faciet,* &c. that is, The more they do it, the more I shall think myself bound to pray for them.

**V. 6. ***{they shall hear my words}* When the great men that now persecute me shall be overthrown, as stumbling in stony ways, then they, or the people which followed them in persecuting me, shall repent, and hearken willingly to God's word, Isa. 28.19.

**V. 7. ***{Our bones are scattered at the graves mouth}* Either he means the bones of such as died for his cause, or else that they were ready to be scattered at the grave's mouth, so that it were a miracle for them to escape death, 2 Cor. 1.9.

**V. 8. ***{mine eyes are unto thee, O God}* 2 Chron. 20.12.

*{leave not my soul destitute}* Heb. *make not my soul bare*.

**V. 10. ***{Let the wicked fall into their own nets}* Heb. *into his nets*: that is, into God's nets, whereby he catches the wicked in their own malice: or, *every one into his own net*, as Psal. 7.15. and 35.8.

*{whilst that I withal escape}* Let their fall be my deliverance.

*{withal}* Or, *alone*. See Job 29. Ezra 4.3.

*{escape}* Heb. *pass over*.

# PSALM CXLII

## TITLE

*{Maschil of David}* Or, *A Psalm of David giving instruction*.

*{when he was in the cave}* 1 Sam. 24.3.

**Verse 1. **This Psalm appears to be made after the deliverance here mentioned. See the like, Jon. 2.1.

*{cried}* David's patience and instant prayer unto God condemns their wicked rage; who, in their troubles, either despair, and murmur against God, or else seek to others rather than to God, to have redress in their miseries.

*{did I make my supplication}* The word, prayer, in the Original, signifies a demanding of justice against his foes: *crying,* signifies earnestness: making *supplication,* shows, that he sought for favor from God: *complaint,* or meditation, argues a preconsideration of what he prayed for: *showing,* intends a laying open of his troubles orderly in fit words.

**V. 3. ***{then thou knewest my path}* You knewest which way I should escape, when I knew no means of delivery: or, you did approve the way that I was in, and took care of me, Psal. 1.6.

**V. 4. ***{I looked on my right hand, and beheld}* Or, *look on the right hand, and see*.

*{refuge failed me}* I had no place in the world to flee to.

*{failed me}* Heb. *perished from me*.

*{no man cared for my soul}* Heb. *no man sought after my soul*.

**V. 5. ***{Thou}* The words following, to the end, were spoken in the cave.

*{my refuge}* Though all means failed him, yet he knew that God would never forsake him, Psal. 32.7. and 46.1.

*{my portion in the land of the living}* Psal. 16.5. and 73.26. and 119.57. Lam. 3.24.

**V. 7. ***{Bring my soul out of prison}* For he was on all sides beset with his enemies, as though he had been in a most strait prison.

*{the righteous shall compass me about}* Either to hear me speak of my wonderful deliverance, that they may rejoice and praise God with me, or else to set the crown on my head.

*{for}* Or, *when*.

# PSALM CXLIII

**Verse 1. ***{Hear my prayer, O Lord, give ear to my supplications}* Of a supposed difference between *prayer* and *supplication,* see upon Psalm 55.1. *Hide not thy self from my supplication:* where these very words are cited. Of the word *righteousness,* how to be understood in this and other like places, see upon Psal. 71.2. *Deliver me in thy righteousness.* Of *faithfulness,* or *truth,* for it is but one word in the Original Hebrew, but sometimes written full, and sometimes abbreviated by leaving out one of the radical letters, See upon Psal. 57.10. *For thy mercy is great.*

But because the word is so frequent in this Book, somewhat more shall be said here also. *Faithfulness,* they say, is ascribed unto God, for keeping of promises. It is so, often. But then we must know, that the promises of God, for which he is so termed faithful, are either particular promises, made unto particular men, as Abraham, David, and the like: or general promises, to a whole nation: or, rather, to all men in general: as for example; That he will be the protector of them that fear him; and put their trust in him: that upon true repentance and amendment of life, he will pardon iniquities: that he will be the avenger of the poor, and fatherless, that are oppressed by might: for these, and the like, *faithfulness* is ascribed unto God by the Psalmist, as often as for keeping his word, to him so often inculcated by Expositors, particularly.

Again, *faithfulness* may be ascribed unto God; not so much in reference to any express promise, as upon a presumption grounded upon his nature, and former experience. So because the creation of man, endowed with such and such faculties, is an argument of God's goodness; it may be presumed of God, as he is a *faithful Creator,* that he will not have the death or destruction of any man, Ezek. 33.11; that he will have a care of them especially, who having spent their lives in his service, commit *the keeping of their souls,* 1 Pet. 4.19. unto Him at their departure; who both as he is a Creator, knows how to value them, and how to keep them; and as he is faithful, will not disappoint them, that have trusted Him, with what was dearest unto themselves. But all I aimed at here, is but to show, that *faithfulness* is ascribed to God in divers respects, and not to be confined to particular promises.

*{into judgment with thy servant}* Job 14.3. He acknowledges a just hand of God in those things that had befallen him, though by the unjust hands of his enemies; and entreats him not to deal rigorously with him, as in justice he might.

*{in thy sight shall no man living be justified}* Exod. 34.7. Rom. 3.20. Gal. 2.16.

**V. 3. ***{For the enemy hath persecuted my soul}* There is no question, but that David, immediately and literally intended it of some one, or more of his mortal and visible enemies, that either by open violence, or by secret plots and machinations, did endeavor to bereave him of his life: or by detraction perchance, and false imputations, of his good name, to good and godly men as dear, as life itself. But in the mystical, or anagogical sense, this enemy, or enemies, may be understood of the Devil, properly called Satan, that is, an adversary, as Psal. 109.6. *and let Satan stand at his right hand:* as not only being the grand and Original enemy to mankind; but also, as he, who in very truth is the author, abetter, and fomenter of all ungodly strifes and contentions among men; and by outward and inward temptations and provocations, where God for secret causes, does not restrain him, the occasion of all slaughter, and unjust blood-shedding, John 8.40, 44.

So that whoever bears but malice in his heart, see also 1 John 3.15. *Whosoever hateth his brother,* &c. may truly be said, in some degree, to be guilty. Whosoever therefore rightly understands these things, whatever he reads in this whole Book, by way of imprecation especially, of enemies and adversaries, according to the exposition and application of the ancients, he may very profitably, whether he have any enemies in this world or no, understand it of his spiritual enemies, which he is sure he has: and no less sure, in case he have other enemies too, whether more or fewer, so far as they deal by malice and unjust violence, that it is the Devil that sets them on work, and leads them to these unjust and uncharitable courses.

I know there be many things in these imprecations, not applicable properly to spirits, but to mortal men only, that are flesh and blood: yet nothing hinders, but that he that reads them, may think of the Devil, and in some convenient sense apply all to him, as the cause of all evil, both spiritual and temporal.

*{hath smitten my life}* I must needs be slain, if you relieve me not.

*{to dwell in darkness}* I am forced to live in dark caves, like graves.

*{that have been long dead}* He acknowledges that God is his only true Physician that can heal him; for he can raise up men long since dead.

**V. 4. ***{spirit overwhelmed within me}* Psal. 142.3.

*{my heart within me is desolate}* So that he was upheld only by God's power, on which he relied, Psal. 73.26.

**V. 5. ***{I remember the days of old}* To wit, your great benefits of old, and the manifold examples of your favor toward your: or, I comfort myself with the remembrance of your favors to me in times past, and take them for a pawn of comfort in time to come, because you are the same, Psal. 77.5, 11.

*{all thy works}* Heb. *every work of your, or, all your practice*.

**V. 6. ***{my soul thirsteth after thee}* Psal. 42.1, 2. and 63.1. and 84.2.

*{as a thirsty land}* I earnestly desire to be with God in the Sanctuary, as the dry ground desires rain, Psal. 42.1, 2. and 63.1.

**V. 7. ***{Hear me speedily}* Heb. *Make haste, answer me*.

*{lest I be like, &c.}* Or, *for I am become like, &c*.

**V. 8. ***{in the morning}* In the morning, that is, speedily, and in due time, Psal. 121.8. See upon Psal. 30.5. *weeping may endure for a night,* &c.

*{cause me to know the way}* Direct me which way I may escape this danger, lest I perish in it, Psal. 28.1. and 5.8.

**V. 9. ***{I flee unto thee, to hide me}* Heb. *hide me with you*. I hid myself under the shadow of your wings, that I might be defended by your power, Psal. 91.1. and 32.7.

**V. 10. ***{Teach me}* He confesses, that both the knowledge of, and obedience to God's will in us, comes from the Spirit of God, who teaches us by his Word, gives understanding by his Spirit, and frames our hearts by his grace to obey him.

*{to do thy will}* That is, justly, and aright: for, so soon as we decline from God's will, we fall into error.

*{thy Spirit is good, lead}* Or, *let your good Spirit lead*.

*{into the land of uprightness}* Or, *in, or on, an even ground*, as Isa. 26.7, 10.

**V. 11. ***{Quicken me}* Keep me alive, and free me from this deadly danger.

*{Quicken me, O Lord, for thy name's sake}* Psal. 119.25, 37, 88, 107.

*{for thy Name's sake}* That your Name may be honored, your justice showed on persecutors, your mercy to me your servant.

**V. 12. ***{of thy mercy cut off thine enemies}* Which shall be a sign of your fatherly care of me.

*{I am thy servant}* Psal. 116.16.

# PSALM CXLIV

**Verse 1. ***{My strength}* Heb. *My rock*.

*{which teacheth my hands to war, &c.}* 2 Sam. 22.35. Who, of a poor shepherd, has made me a valiant warrior, and mighty conqueror.

*{to war}* Heb. *to the war, &c*.

**V. 2. ***{My goodness}* Or, *My mercy*. From whom alone I look for kindness and protection.

*{My goodness and my fortress, my high tower and my deliverer, &c.}* 2 Sam. 22.2, 3, 40.

*{my deliverer}* Heb. *my deliverer for me*: for the Prophet cannot satisfy himself with any words.

*{who subdueth my people under me}* He confesses, that neither by his own authority, power nor policy, his kingdom was kept quiet, but by the secret favor of God.

**V. 3. ***{Lord, what is man, that thou takest knowledge of him? or the son of man, &c.}* Job 7.17. Psal. 8.4. Heb. 2.6.

*{that thou takest knowledge of him?}* To give unto God just praise, is, To confess ourselves to be unworthy of so excellent benefits, and that he bestows them upon us of his free mercy.

**V. 4. ***{Man is like to vanity}* Job 14.2. Psal. 39.5.

**V. 5. ***{Bow thy heavens, O Lord, &c.}* Psal. 18.9. Isa. 64.1. Show thyself present on earth, by destroying my greatest enemies, who are like mountains, in comparison of other men.

**V. 6. ***{Cast forth lightning and scatter them}* Psal. 18.13, 14. Deliver me by heavenly means, for earthly means fail me.

**V. 7. ***{Send thy hand}* Heb. *hands*. He was in such danger, that he thought he needed both hands, that is, all God's power, to help him.

*{from above}* From heaven.

*{out of great waters}* Psal. 69.1, 14.

*{from the hand of strange children}* Deliver me from the tumults of strangers, that profess peace to me: or, of my own people, that carry themselves cruelly toward me, like strangers. See upon Psal. 54.3. *strangers,* &c.

**V. 8. ***{And their right hand is a right hand of falsehood}* Psal. 26.10. For though they shake hands, yet they keep not promise.

**V. 9. ***{I will sing a new song unto thee}* A rare and excellent song, as your great benefits deserve: or, a new, triumphant song after victory, framed especially upon this occasion; the argument whereof is set down, ver. 10.

*{a new song unto thee, O God}* Psal. 40.3. and 98.1.

**V. 10. ***{It is he that giveth}* Or, *Who gives*.

*{salvation}* Or, *victory*.

*{delivereth}* Or, *delivers*.

*{David his servant}* Though strange kings be called God's servants, as Cyrus, Isa. 45.1. forasmuch as he uses them to execute his judgments: yet David, and they that rule godly, are properly so called, because they serve not their own affections, but set forth God's glory.
